                                 Preface

TC 3-20.20 provides a detailed description of the maneuver force’s platoon training strategy. This training
strategy is for all platoon-size formations to achieve fire and maneuver proficiency at home station.

TC 3-20.20 includes the purpose of the integrated weapons training strategy (IWTS), its standard structure,
training requirements, the integration of combined arms assets, and resource requirements for the Armored,
Infantry, and Stryker brigade combat teams’ maneuver elements. This publication provides training
principles and techniques for units to use to gain proficiency in engaging and destroying threats efficiently
in any operational environment.

This publication includes all the required planning and preparation for a successful unit weapons training
program. Where vehicle or weapon-specific techniques in this training circular conflict with that vehicle or
weapon’s technical manual, the reader should follow the procedures in the technical manual. For the purposes
of clarity, Army standard is directly implied when discussing standard or standards throughout TC 3-20.20.

The principal audience for TC 3-20.20 is commanders, planners, trainers, and Master Gunners of maneuver
units. Commanders and staffs of Army headquarters serving as joint task force or multinational headquarters
should also refer to applicable joint or multinational doctrine concerning the range of military operations and
joint or multinational forces. Trainers and educators throughout the Army will also use this publication.

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates ensure that their decisions and actions comply with applicable United
States, international, and in some cases host-nation laws and regulations. Commanders at all levels ensure
that their Service members operate in accordance with the law of armed conflict and the rules of engagement.
(See FM 6-27 for legal compliance.)

The use of a trade or brand name does not constitute endorsement of any specific commercial product,
commodity, service, or enterprise by the United States Army.

TC 3-20.20 uses joint terms where applicable. Selected joint and Army terms and definitions appear in both
the glossary and the text. Terms for which TC 3-20.20 is the proponent publication (the authority) are marked
with an asterisk (*) in the glossary. When first defining other proponent definitions in the text, the term is
italicized with the proponent publication designator and number at the end of the definition. Following uses
of the term are not italicized.

TC 3-20.20 applies to the Active Army, Army National Guard/Army National Guard of the United States,
and United States Army Reserve unless otherwise stated.

The proponent of this publication is the United States Army Maneuver Center of Excellence. The preparing
agency is the Doctrine and Collective Training Division, Department of Tactics, Training, and Doctrine,
United States Army Maneuver Center of Excellence. Send comments and recommendations on
DA Form 2028 (Recommended Changes to Publications and Blank Forms) to Commander, United States
Army Maneuver Center of Excellence and Fort Benning, ATZK-TD (TC 3-20.20), 1 Karker Street, Fort
Benning, GA 31905-5410; by email to usarmy.benning.mcoe.mbx.doctrine@mail.mil; or submit an
electronic DA Form 2028.

                              Introduction

TC 3-20.20 provides the details of the IWTS structure specifically designed for U.S. Army maneuver
platoons. This training circular provides the training strategy for maneuver commanders to train, evaluate,
and assess their unit’s overall proficiency at home station. The maneuver platoon training strategy includes
only the critical training events to build readiness at echelon using the IWTS’s standard training structure.
The standardized strategy synchronizes with the Army’s force generation model (Regionally Aligned
Readiness and Modernization Model) and provides detailed training requirements to maneuver units.

The maneuver platoon training strategy maximizes the training aids, devices, simulators, and simulations
(TADSS), and live-fire events capabilities in a systematic manner to increase and sustain Soldier and unit
proficiency, effectiveness, and lethality. The training strategy is capability-based (unit, subordinate unit, and
integrated warfighting functions) and maximizes the training resources available across the force. The
strategy synchronizes with echelon-based proficiency benchmarks within the Army’s readiness model and
meets the training proficiency goals and standards for a ready and capable force.

The maneuver platoon training strategy encompasses all critical training that builds a unit’s ability to shoot,
move, and communicate effectively and efficiently. Although units conduct other training as part of their
training plan, the critical training path includes only those events that are conducted to ensure the maximum
experience is achieved or gained by the trained Soldier, subordinate elements, integrated warfighting
functions, and the unit.

Higher echelon and subordinate element training strategies are defined in their respective echelon-based
training publications as part of the IWTS structure and as described in TC 3-20.0. The terms defined are used
frequently in this training circular:

     • Company—a unit consisting of two or more platoons, usually of the same type, with a headquarters
          and a limited capacity for self-support (ADP 3-90).

     • Platoon—a subdivision of a company or troop consisting of two or more squads or sections
          (ADP 3-90).

     • Troop—a company-size unit in a cavalry organization (ADP 3-90).

Introductory figure-1, page xii provides common platoon formation structures and the corresponding training
publication.

       Note. A commander can create a platoon to meet the mission requirements on demand of the
       tactical situation. In most cases, placing qualified subordinate elements together with an
       experienced leader creates a valuable platoon for the commander’s mission.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  xi


---

Introduction

                   Introductory figure-1. Platoon formations and training publications

     Commanders may elect to include additional subordinate teams, crews, or squads to the platoon formation
     based on mission requirements. If small teams, crews, or squads are added to the platoon formation after the
     platoon has achieved proficiency, the commander may elect to incorporate proficient elements to the platoon
     after completing an assessment. The commander may also include proficient elements to the platoon
     formation for platoon or company training.

            Note. For example, the commander determines a platoon must include one squad, an additional
            antitank team, and a Stryker mobile gun system to meet the combatant commander’s guidance.
            The commander can include the antitank team prior to platoon training beginning or choose to
            qualify the antitank team separately then integrate them into platoon training. The platoon key
            leaders have established proficiency providing mission command and tactically employing
            proficient subordinates. In this manner, the commander can add Table VI proficient subordinates
            to that formation and still maintain the platoon’s proficiency rating.

     The platoon training strategy has up to six training events that culminate in their demonstration of proficiency.
     For platoons, the training strategy culminates with two externally evaluated proficiency assessments:
     Table IV, Field Training Exercise (FTX), and Table VI, Live-Fire Exercise (LFX). Maneuver platoon
     training consists of all the training events along the critical path to proficiency.

     Units should maximize their time and resources by designing their platoon training plans such that platoons
     train through proficiency to support their platoon and company, whether as an organic small unit, an
     attachment, or a cross-attachment. This level of synchronization will enable the unit to transition to advanced,
     higher echelon collective training seamlessly. (Introductory figure-2 shows the structure for the maneuver
     platoon training strategy.) The training strategy described within this publication follows the standard training
     structure found in TC 3-20.0 and synchronizes with the training principles found in FM 7-0.

xii           TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

               Introductory figure-2. Platoon training strategy overview

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                                 xiii


---

Introduction

PLATOON TABLES

     Platoons follow the standard six-table format of the IWTS through tactical (force-on-force) proficiency and
     live-fire proficiency. The six platoon tables are listed (shown in introductory figure-3) for context—

          • Table I–Tactical Standard Operating Procedures (TACSOPs).
          • Table II–Situational Training Exercise–Virtual (STX-V).
          • Table III–Situational Training Exercise (STX).
          • Table IV–FTX.
          • Table V–Fire Coordination Exercise (FCX).
          • Table VI–LFX.

     Introductory figure-3. Platoon tables with linkage to higher echelon collective training,
                                                             example

     This training circular describes each of the training events for Tables I through VI for the maneuver platoon.
     It includes the process of determining if a platoon-size maneuver unit retains its proficiency rating. Further,
     it provides the guidelines for building integrated training scenarios and events.

xiv  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                                                     Introduction

CHAPTERS

     Chapters 1 through 6 in this training circular provide an overview of the training event, the training event
     definition, the purpose, method, and end state of the training, and a guide to plan, prepare, execute, and assess
     each event. Chapter 7 details methods to manage the proficiency of the maneuver unit once Soldiers achieve
     proficiency.

CHAPTER 1–TABLE I, TACTICAL STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES

     Chapter 1 describes the platoon-level TACSOPs. The chapter provides general information for the unit to
     plan, prepare, execute, and assess the event using the eight-step training model.

CHAPTER 2–TABLE II, SITUATIONAL TRAINING EXERCISE–VIRTUAL

     Chapter 2 describes the platoon-level STX-V. The chapter includes the purpose, method, and end state of the
     event based on the commander’s objectives. The chapter provides general information for the unit to plan,
     prepare, execute, and assess the event using the eight-step training model.

CHAPTER 3–TABLE III, SITUATIONAL TRAINING EXERCISE

     Chapter 3 describes the platoon-level STX using various TADSS and includes the purpose, method, and end
     state of the event based on the commander’s objectives. The chapter provides general information for the
     unit to plan, prepare, and execute, the event using the eight-step training model, and for the commander to
     assess the platoon. Chapter 3 also describes the requirements for training, integrating warfighting functions,
     and other planning considerations.

CHAPTER 4–TABLE IV, FIELD TRAINING EXERCISE

     Chapter 4 describes the platoon-level FTX using various TADSS and includes the purpose, method, and end
     state of the event based on the unit’s supporting collective tasks. The chapter provides general information
     for the unit to plan, prepare, and execute the event and for commanders to assess the event using the
     operations process. Chapter 4 also describes the requirements for training, integrating warfighting functions,
     and the external evaluation (EXEVAL) requirements to meet the collective task proficiency gate.

CHAPTER 5–TABLE V, FIRE COORDINATION EXERCISE

     Chapter 5 describes the platoon-level FCX and includes the purpose, method, and end state of the event based
     on the unit’s supporting collective tasks. Chapter 5 provides general information for the unit to plan, prepare,
     execute, and assess the event using the operations process.

CHAPTER 6–TABLE VI, LIVE-FIRE EXERCISE

     Chapter 6 describes the platoon-level LFX and includes the purpose, method, and end state of the event based
     on the unit’s supporting collective tasks. The chapter provides general information for the unit to plan,
     prepare, and execute the event and for commanders to assess the event using the operations process. Chapter 6
     also describes the requirements for training, integrating warfighting functions, and the EXEVAL
     requirements to meet the live-fire proficiency.

CHAPTER 7–PLATOON PROFICIENCY

     Chapter 7 describes how maneuver units manage their proficiency ratings over time after achieving
     proficiency. The chapter includes topics for commanders to consider that directly affect their proficiency
     ratings such as personnel changes, loss of subordinate qualification ratings, evaluation expiration, and
     changes to their mission.

Introduction

APPENDIXES

     The appendixes in this training circular provide a guide to units as they build their collective training plan to
     achieve proficiency in the unit’s collective tasks based on the higher headquarters’ mission-essential tasks
     and identify—

          • The recommended training munitions, pyrotechnics, and simulators to conduct each event.
          • Collective task proficiency, gate requirements, and evaluations.
          • Warfighting function integration requirements and examples.
          • Range facility, TADSS, training area, and other enablers that support the unit’s training.

     The appendixes provide a guide to develop the various training events effectively and efficiently and outline
     the minimum tasks, actions, integration, conditions, and standards to achieve.

APPENDIX A–AMMUNITION REQUIREMENTS

     This appendix describes the recommended training ammunition, pyrotechnics, simulators, and other
     munitions that support the training strategy. Appendix A provides a detailed listing of munitions by weapon,
     system, or platform and includes various planning considerations.

APPENDIX B–BUILDING COLLECTIVE TRAINING

     Appendix B describes the integration requirements that support the platoon training strategy. Appendix B
     provides planning considerations, scenario requirements, and a detailed list of specific warfighting function
     elements that should be incorporated into the various training events.

APPENDIX C–ROLES, RESPONSIBILITIES, AND LEADER CERTIFICATION

     This appendix provides general information to evaluate collective training. Appendix C provides an overview
     of training and evaluation outlines and their use during collective training. This appendix also establishes and
     provides the evaluation criteria for live-fire proficiency.

APPENDIX D–COLLECTIVE EVALUATION

     This appendix includes basic information on certifying leaders.

APPENDIX E–COLLECTIVE TASKS BY FORMATION TYPE

     This appendix is a brief listing of the primary collective tasks for training on Tables IV, FTX, and Table VI,
     LFX.

                                                 Chapter 1

       Table I – Tactical Standard Operating Procedures

    This chapter discusses the requirements of the exercises in Platoon Table I, Tactical Standard
    Operating Procedures (TACSOPs). This chapter describes how Table I integrates into the unit
    training plan following the structure of the integrated weapons training strategy (IWTS). This
    chapter provides descriptive guidelines for the exercises listed in the Platoon Table I and
    provides the purpose, method, and end state for the training event.
    Platoon Table I verifies the Soldier’s ability to perform critical tasks, understand how the unit
    operates tactically as the mission requires, and employ the weapons and systems safely during
    training and tactical operations as part of the collective element.

EVENT OVERVIEW

     1-1. Platoon Table I, TACSOP, is a pre-live-fire hands-on performance and written evaluation of weaponry
     tasks, characteristics, capabilities, and function in the live environment. It is the culminating test after the
     platoon leadership has provided instruction on how the platoon will operate tactically (see figure 1-1).

     1-2. Platoon Table I, TACSOP, provides the platoon leader, platoon sergeant, and commander with a training
     event that educates all Soldiers within the platoon, synchronizes small-unit processes and procedures with
     the higher element, and assists in validating the units’ standard operating procedures (SOPs) and their
     associated tactics, techniques, and procedures (TTP) as the unit progresses through their unit training plan.
     Table I provides platoon leadership and commanders with an assessment of their subordinate’s understanding
     of those standards, how they apply to their tasks and purpose, and how they relate to the platoon and company
     mission.

              Figure 1-1. Platoon Table I, Tactical Standard Operating Procedures

1-3. During Table I, TASCSOPs, Soldiers must demonstrate proficiency on individual and collective tasks,
skills, and knowledge through testable (written) and performance (hands-on) evaluation. The event provides
the platoon leader and platoon sergeant with a means to instruct and reinforce platoon SOPs, doctrine
concepts, and TTP to achieve mastery of the unit’s collective tasks. Table I is a prerequisite to any live-fire
training with the platoon.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  1


---

Chapter 1

PURPOSE

     1-4. The Platoon Table I, TACSOP, provides the platoon leader, platoon sergeant, and the commander with
     an assessment of all assigned or attached Soldiers on their understanding of their individual tasks and how
     the organization executes its collective tasks to complete the unit’s assigned mission.

METHOD

     1-5. The platoon leader and platoon sergeant use Table I as an event to instruct individual and collective tasks
     to their subordinates. This enables the platoon leader and platoon sergeant to determine the quality and
     effectiveness of the platoon’s SOPs and TTP and ensure they are nested with the company’s SOPs. Table I
     also provides the platoon leader and platoon sergeant with a method to assess how well their Soldiers
     understand the platoon’s assigned roles and responsibilities during movement and maneuver and how the
     commander intends to tactically employ the organization to achieve collective task proficiency.

     1-6. Table I is a commander-defined training event that units conduct in a suitable training area with the
     appropriate equipment, weapons, and platforms. Units complete Table I between T-6 and T-execution (T-X)
     for the live-fire training event.

             Note. Table I is a commander-defined event and is designed and implemented by the platoon
             leadership. This allows the commander to ensure focus on the collective tasks, command training
             guidance, and other critical training.

END STATE

     1-7. The end state for the training event is that 90 percent of the subordinates in each platoon have
     successfully completed Table I to the commander’s satisfaction. In a live environment, all Soldiers have
     successfully demonstrated the ability to conduct the individual, supporting collective, and critical tasks the
     organization is expected to conduct during combat or deployed operations. Soldiers must adequately
     demonstrate to the platoon leadership’s satisfaction their ability to employ their weapons and systems safely
     during training and tactical operations as part of the collective element.

2  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                Table I – Tactical Standard Operating Procedures

PLAN THE TRAINING

     1-8. Platoon Table I, TACSOP, is a pre-live-fire, hands-on performance and written evaluation of weaponry
     tasks, characteristics, capabilities, and function in the live environment. It verifies the Soldier’s ability to
     perform critical tasks, understand how the unit operates tactically as the mission requires, and employ the
     weapons and systems safely during training and tactical operations as part of the collective element.

     1-9. Platoon Table I, TACSOP, is mandatory for all Soldiers prior to any collective live-fire event where they
     are—

          • Assigned as a member of the platoon.
          • Attached as a member of a specialty unit.
          • Attached as a member of a crew platform to the platoon.

     1-10. Table I, TACSOP, is resourced for one iteration of a half-day event per platoon and platoon-size unit
     during the fiscal year (active component). Leaders should make every attempt to conduct Table I prior to
     Tables II and III to apply the lessons learned from Table I appropriately during follow-on training events,
     reinforcing the unit’s SOP across the unit training plan.

PLAN SUCCESSFUL PLATOON TABLE I

     1-11. To plan a successful Platoon Table I, TACSOP, commanders must consider their goals and objectives
     to achieve overall support for collective task proficiency. Commanders should be familiar with the event’s
     framework during the planning process as follows:

Prerequisites

     1-12. There are no directed prerequisites for Platoon Table I. Platoon leadership should include sufficient
     training time for leaders to instruct their subordinates on the contents of the unit SOP and use various other
     learning events (sergeant’s time training, sand tables, virtual battlespace, and so forth) prior to execution.

Table Duration

     1-13. Table I is conducted between T-6 and T-X of any platoon live-fire event.

Environment and Conditions

     1-14. Units conduct Platoon Table I in a live environment in a setting conducive to training the requisite
     individual and collective tasks assigned by the commander.

Training Days Required

     1-15. Successful completion of Table I requires one half of 1 training day per each platoon. The commander
     determines the training leading up to the event based on training task requirements found in the Combined
     Arms Training Strategy (CATS) (see website) and the commander’s assessment of the unit’s level of
     proficiency.

Frequency

     1-16. Table I is conducted a minimum of once per year prior to any platoon live-fire training events.

Primary and Alternate Facilities

     1-17. Unit leadership will ensure a sufficient training area is secured to conduct Table I. This includes any
     classroom, motor pool, or training areas suitable for instructing and evaluating all Soldiers on individual and
     collective tasks.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  3


---

Chapter 1

Focus of Table I

     1-18. Table I should focus on the individual and collective tasks that support the company’s mission-essential
     tasks. Unit leadership should work together to create their Table I using the task sets that support the
     mission-essential tasks they execute during the unit’s training plan. Leaders of echelons below company level
     (platoons, squads, crews, teams, and other small units) prioritize the collective tasks on which their echelon
     trains. Leaders of echelons below company level select and prioritize the collective tasks for their echelon
     that are most critical to the accomplishment of their company’s prioritized mission-essential tasks. This
     provides the commander the ability to—

          • Train battle tasks, battle drills, and crew drills.
          • Validate subordinate unit or element SOPs and TTP.
          • Focus on critical tasks that directly affect the unit’s mission success.
          • Ensure units understand how warfighting functions integrate into their scheme of maneuver.
          • Support command training guidance at platoon level early in the training plan.
          • Train target hand-off tasks.
          • Train commander-selected tasks, basic tactical tasks, and enabling tactical tasks.

BATTLE TASK INTEGRATION

     1-19. The prioritized collective tasks below company level that are critical to the successful accomplishment
     of prioritized company mission-essential tasks are battle tasks. A battle task is a platoon or lower echelon
     collective task that is crucial to the successful accomplishment of a company, battery, or troop mission-
     essential task. An organization’s battle tasks can include high-payoff tasks or battle drills. These are
     integrated into the collective training plan during every training event.

     1-20. Platoon leaders with the assistance of their platoon sergeant identify and prioritize the battle tasks that
     best support their company’s prioritized mission essential tasks. This process continues to lower echelons
     with noncommissioned officers determining the prioritized battle tasks that best apply to their echelon (such
     as squad, team, or crew). Company commanders are responsible for the proper nesting of battle tasks to
     mission-essential tasks.

DRILL INTEGRATION

     1-21. Drills provide standard actions that link Soldier and collective tasks at staff and platoon levels and
     below. There are two types of drills—battle drills and crew drills:

     1-22. A battle drill is a collective action where Soldiers and leaders rapidly process information, make
     decisions, and execute without a deliberate decision-making process. For example, a staff performs a battle
     drill to exercise a command post.

     1-23. A crew drill is a collective action that the crew of a weapon or piece of equipment must perform to use
     the weapon or equipment. It is a trained response to a given stimulus, or initiated cue, such as a leader order
     or the status of the weapon or equipment. Crew drills require minimal leader orders to accomplish.

     1-24. Integration of the battle drills is critical to build the platoon’s proficiency and should be trained and
     reinforced as frequently as possible. Much like Soldier individual training is the cornerstone of unit training
     proficiency, the battle drills and crew drills build the foundation of collective training.

WARFIGHTING FUNCTION INTEGRATION

     1-25. A warfighting function is a group of tasks and systems united by a common purpose that commanders
     use to accomplish missions and training objectives (ADP 3-0). All warfighting functions possess scalable
     capabilities to mass lethal and nonlethal effects

     1-26. All maneuver platoons train and test tasks for Table I. Platoon leaders and platoon sergeants should
     focus their training on the tasks indicated in figure 1-2. The tasks listed in figure 1-1, page 1 depict how
     platoon leaders and platoon sergeants should train and evaluate the warfighting functions throughout the
     platoon’s collective training events.

Chapter 1

TRAIN THE TRAINERS

     1-27. Units execute Table I in a variety of ways. Typically, each platoon conducts preparatory training
     independently prior to Platoon Table I. The commander may elect to use the Section, Platoon, and Company
     Table I to train the platoon as a concurrent, multi-echelon event, provided they are conducted between T-6
     and T-X of any platoon or above live-fire event. When training is conducted at the platoon level, the company
     commander is the primary trainer.

     1-28. Commanders must provide unit leadership with the unit SOP, TACSOP, the selected high-payoff
     collective tasks for training and evaluation, and their respective training and evaluation outline. The platoon
     leadership are responsible to train their platoons on why and how the unit conducts the collective tasks to
     support the unit’s mission-essential task.

     1-29. Table I provides a method of assessing Soldiers’ and subordinate leader’s understanding of the
     platoon’s SOP, task, purpose, collective tasks, and tactical employment. Leaders use the information in
     Table I to develop, update, instruct, and evaluate their subordinate and attached Soldiers to fight as a
     maneuver platoon within the company.

RECON THE SITE

     1-30. The platoon TACSOP requires a training area sufficient in size to support the personnel and equipment
     used to train and evaluate all Soldiers in the unit. Depending on the method used to instruct and assess
     Soldiers on the execution of their individual and collective tasks, platoon leadership and commanders should
     consider using—

          • Vehicle stations for training and assessment of personnel.
          • Appropriate training aids, devices, simulators, and simulations (TADSS).
          • General instruction classroom facilities located near the training area for terrain board, instruction,

                retraining, and after action reviews (AARs).

     1-31. Once the method of training and evaluation is established, platoon leadership and commanders should
     secure the training location and appropriate TADSS, equipment, or systems that enhance the training event.
     Units must confirm the training location is sufficient to accommodate the number of Soldiers and vehicles
     attending training and rotations and provide a retraining capability.

ISSUE THE ORDER

     1-32. Platoon leaders use the training schedule to secure training time and space for the event. The
     commander can include an operation order (OPORD) to support the training events within the strategy or as
     part of multi-echelon training of Table I. The order should include the list of tasks that the platoon leadership
     plans to assess during the event, as well as the plan for the overall conduct of the event, resources required,
     and specific instructions to the subordinate leaders.

     1-33. Platoon leadership should provide sufficient time for subordinate leaders to train those tasks and skills
     effectively before the event and ensure the appropriate training resources are available and accessible. Upon
     receipt, subordinate leaders who have SOPs for their organizations must ensure their procedures support and
     complement the higher headquarters’ publication.

     1-34. Typically, the unit training schedule should provide all the information required for subordinate units
     to initiate movement and begin planning their preparatory training as appropriate. Commanders provide
     warning orders (WARNORDs) and fragmentary orders (FRAGORDs) during the company training meetings,
     as necessary.

6  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                Table I – Tactical Standard Operating Procedures

REHEARSE

     1-35. Platoon leadership should conduct a rehearsal of Table I with subordinate leaders, using all training
     resources, materials, and equipment available. During the rehearsal, platoon leadership ensure their unit has
     sufficient time to train to the specified standard. Platoon leadership should plan terrain board rehearsals, map
     reconnaissance, and other low-impact preparatory training to support the rehearsal.

     1-36. The rehearsal also provides platoon leadership with an opportunity to verify that the tasks selected for
     Table I are consistent with the tasks, actions, and TTP of the higher organization. The rehearsal provides an
     opportunity for the platoon leadership to synchronize the unit SOP with the TACSOP of their gaining unit
     for maneuver units that are expected to cross-attach to an external unit.

     1-37. Using a terrain model or sand table is encouraged to ensure Soldiers have a comprehensive
     understanding of the task, their specific actions, and those of their squad or platoon as part of the larger
     organization.

     1-38. Regardless of the formation type, the SOP instruction must include the procedures, tasks, or
     assignments to conduct the individual tasks, collective tasks, critical tasks, and tactical reporting requirements
     for the unit to build proficiency before the situational training exercise-virtual (STX-V), situational training
     exercise (STX), field training exercise (FTX), and live-fire events during the upcoming training density.

EXECUTE

     1-39. Table I consists of platoon leader-developed assessments that are approved by the commander. The
     method of the assessment is at the commander’s discretion.

     1-40. Units are resourced one half of 1 training day to conduct each platoon SOP during the fiscal or training
     year. Commanders may combine these days into one single Table I or multiple events based on their training
     plan. Units complete Table I between T-6 through T-X (active component and reserve component) before all
     live-fire events during the fiscal or training year.

EVALUATE THE TRAINING

     1-41. Platoon leadership should follow the appropriate training and evaluation outlines as a guide to assessing
     their subordinate key leaders and Soldiers. Used in conjunction with the unit SOP, the training and evaluation
     outlines identify critical shortfalls in the execution of collective tasks that impede the unit’s success.

     1-42. Soldiers within the organization and those attached receive training and evaluation on their
     understanding, comprehension, and retention of the information. At the completion of Table I, platoon
     leadership and their senior leaders discuss any shortcomings, gaps, or disconnects between SOPs by echelon
     and determine any corrective actions.

     1-43. Once Table I is completed, the small-unit SOP (with corrections as necessary) is provided to the unit
     commander for review and record. The platoon leadership identify the Soldiers who require additional
     training and the specific tasks that require attention.

RETRAIN

     1-44. Soldiers that fail to demonstrate the minimum understanding of the unit SOP and the tasks associated
     with their individual and the unit’s collective tasks and how they support those tasks are provided additional
     training. Platoon leadership assess each Soldier and subordinate unit, identify specific training requirements,
     and direct a retraining plan, as necessary. Platoon leadership reassess subordinates upon completion of the
     retraining efforts through a method of their choosing.

     1-45. During retraining, the units should focus on tasks where the Soldiers failed to demonstrate knowledge
     or proficiency based on the training and evaluation outlines and unit SOP. Units should also consider
     retraining associated tasks, collective tasks, and individual tasks and drills such that the Soldiers’
     understanding is reinforced by using a holistic scenario. This enables the leader to develop a more complete
     understanding of the task and performance measures and highlights critical second- and third-order effects.

Chapter 1

BEST PRACTICES

     1-46. Units should consider including the following best practices in Table I at the platoon level.
     Incorporation of these practices will assist the unit get their Soldiers to a higher proficiency rapidly. This is
     particularly useful upon reception and integration of new Soldiers to the unit.

HARD COPY TACTICAL STANDARD OPERATING PROCEDURES

     1-47. Provide hard copy TACSOPs for each Soldier. This serves as the outline to the classes prior to testing
     their knowledge as well as a textbook for the critical skills and knowledge expected of them. Ensure the
     TACSOP includes the critical tasks, warfighting function integration skills, and appropriate checklists the
     Soldier is expected to perform to support tactical employment of the platoon and company, as appropriate.

     1-48. At a minimum, TACSOP cards should be provided for specific tasks that are critical to the tactical
     mission tasks assigned to the platoon. Figure 1-3 shows an example of a call for direct fire support warfighting
     function requirement for all platoons that should be included.

                                                 Chapter 2

         Table II – Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

    This chapter describes the requirements of Platoon Table II, STX-V, and how the exercises
    integrate into the unit training plan following the structure of the IWTS. Chapter 2 provides
    descriptive guidelines for Platoon Table II and provides the purpose, method, and end state for
    the training event.
    The STX-V is a short, scenario-driven, mission-oriented tactical exercise that trains a single
    collective task or a group of related battle drills and collective tasks within a virtual
    environment.

EVENT OVERVIEW

     2-1. Platoon Table II, STX-V, is a pre-live fire, virtual environment training event that helps build unit
     proficiency in specific collective tasks that directly support the unit’s mission-essential tasks. The STX-V
     provides the platoon with multiple virtual iterations of missions and tasks that reinforce the TTP the unit
     employs to execute its missions. Platoon leadership use their unit SOP and the appropriate training and
     evaluation outlines to develop subordinate leaders’ and elements’ movement and maneuver and, to a limited
     degree, their marksmanship lethality. Table II, STX-V is a training event in the crawl stage of the training
     strategy as shown in figure 2-1.

               Figure 2-1. Platoon Table II, Situational Training Exercise-Virtual

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                                           11


---

Chapter 2

     2-2. STX-V provides the leader with a method to train and practice specific tasks using doctrinally approved
     TTP and reinforces using and validating the unit SOPs. Although mission oriented (based on higher
     headquarters’ OPORD), an STX-V is not used to train all tasks required for an operation. STX-V requires
     leader tasks (such as planning, controlling, and reporting) that build unit proficiency and enables execution
     of supporting, and critical tasks on demand during operations.

     2-3. STX-V events use commander-developed scenarios in a virtual environment that includes the following:
          • Soldiers, crews, and subordinate units.
          • Virtual simulations or weapons and systems employment.
          • Training at a real or accelerated tempo.
          • Virtual training space.
          • Integration of warfighting function capabilities.
          • Inexpensive rehearsal, training, and retraining.
          • Exceptional AAR capability.
          • Multiple iterations of battle drills.

PURPOSE

     2-4. The exercise in Platoon Table II, STX-V, ensures the unit’s assigned and attached subordinate elements
     demonstrate proficiency in a virtual environment of commander-selected critical and collective tasks. STX-V
     enables units to execute their collective tasks properly. Platoon Table II provides a virtual learning
     environment for leaders to synchronize and execute fire and maneuver, integrate indirect and direct fires,
     execute critical tasks, and complete various combat reporting requirements before conducting the tasks in a
     live environment.

     2-5. The exercises in Platoon Table II, STX-V, serve as a prerequisite for all collective platoon-level live-fire
     events. The event may be integrated into a higher echelon constructive training event fire coordination
     exercise (FCX).

METHOD

     2-6. The exercise in Table II, STX-V, uses a simulated, virtual environment that is immersive in nature to
     train, assess, and retrain collective units on tactical maneuver, movement, and engagement techniques at a
     reduced, full, or accelerated tempo. The exercise in Table II uses commander-defined scenarios to provide
     the appropriate tactical stimulus to subordinate units based on the task being trained. The scenario inspires
     units’ reactions that include the timely and accurate execution of their mission-essential, critical, collective,
     and individual tasks. The commander-designed scenario includes sufficient tactical stimulus to train and
     evaluate subordinate units operating as a cohesive formation in a virtual battlespace.

     2-7. The exercise in Table II, STX-V, is required for each assigned or attached subordinate unit, conducted
     between T-6 and T-X of the live-fire training event or density. For formations that do not have a simulation
     system, commanders should use the time to conduct the training in a live environment at a reduced tempo
     with formations using their assigned equipment.

END STATE

     2-8. In a virtual environment, the platoon, with its subordinate assigned or attached elements, have
     successfully demonstrated its ability to conduct supporting collective, critical, or directed tasks or battle tasks
     that the organization is expected to conduct during combat or deployed operations. The platoon leadership
     successfully instructs the collective tasks, validates the platoon and subordinate unit’s SOP, reinforces
     previous collective task training, and builds confidence with the unit by integrating warfighting functions
     and enablers.

     2-9. Commanders are satisfied with their subordinates’ abilities to execute all supporting collective and battle
     tasks that directly support the unit’s mission-essential tasks. The unit’s SOPs are updated as appropriate.

12  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                     Table II – Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

PLAN THE TRAINING

     2-10. The exercise listed in Platoon Table II, STX-V, is a simulations-based demonstration of employing the
     element to its primary capabilities. The exercise in this table is a commander-designed scenario using the
     authorized simulations systems that specifically teach and train the supporting collective tasks and
     mission-essential tasks for the unit. The commander’s scenario focuses on the critical skills of mission
     command, integrating warfighting functions, maneuver, fire control and distribution, and synchronization for
     the unit to achieve proficiency. The platoon leader and platoon sergeant use the unit’s TACSOPs and the
     applicable training and evaluation outlines to guide and assess the unit during Table II.

     2-11. The following platoons provide a guide to developing Table II, STX-V, events using the eight-step
     training model. Units typically develop and execute this training with limited support from their higher
     headquarters.

     2-12. Table II, STX-V, is for all subordinate units and elements within the company before any collective
     live-fire event. This training event is required for all subordinate units that are assigned or attached to the
     company as a—

          • Maneuver element.
          • Enabling specialty unit.
          • Warfighting function small unit.

     2-13. Each platoon is authorized 5 training days (40 hours) per year to conduct Table II, STX-V, prior to
     Table III, STX. Units conduct this training as part of the platoon strategy.

             Note. The platoon SOP should integrate and directly support the company and battalion SOPs.
             These SOPs, maneuver doctrine, and the associated training and evaluation outlines are primary
             references during the execution of Platoon Tables I through VI.

     2-14. For planning purposes, platoon leadership and commanders should consider the following information
     when building their Table II, STX-V, scenario for training:

          • Prerequisites:
                 Table II exercise does not have any directed prerequisites.
                 Commanders may elect to conduct Table II as a multi-echelon event.
                 Integrate Platoon Table II, Platoon STX-V into Company Table II to maximize the training
                     benefit and resources.

          • Table duration:
                 At least one 8-hour iteration of Table II is conducted between T-6 and T-X of the platoon-level
                     live-fire event.
                 Platoons should maximize their use of their available virtual systems in garrison before entering
                     the field portion of their training plan.

          • Environment and conditions:
                 Units conduct Table II in the virtual environment on an approved virtual or simulations system.
                 Units that do not have a primary virtual system may conduct Table II with TADSS in a live
                     environment in a suitable-size training area.

          • Training days required:
                 Units are provided a minimum of 5 training days for platoons to conduct collective simulations
                     training.
                 Training time is provided across the fiscal year or training year.
                 Commanders must include at least one 8-hour iteration of Table II between T-6 and T-X.

          • Frequency:
                 Table II is conducted prior to platoon live-fire training as directed by the higher headquarters.
                 Most units complete Table II once per year, unless multiple platoon-size, live-fire densities are
                     scheduled across the fiscal year or training year.
                 The CATS resources five 8-hour training days for platoon-level STX-V events.

          • Throughput—Units should use systems that accommodate their entire organization with associated
                attached enablers to maximize the virtual system’s capabilities.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  13

Chapter 2

          • Primary and alternate virtual systems:
                 Figure 2-2 lists primary and alternate virtual systems that support tactical scenarios.
                 Additional alternative means of conducting Table II include using a terrain model or conducting
                     a tactical exercise without troops.

                          Figure 2-2. Primary and alternate virtual systems by unit

    2-15. Platoon Table II trains the collective tasks critical to the tactical success of the platoon. Unit leadership
    should create their Table II using the universal task list and task sets that support the company
    mission-essential tasks. Focus should be on critical tasks necessary to achieve proficiency when executing
    their platoon collective tasks. This provides the unit leadership with the ability to—

         • Focus on critical tasks that directly affect the unit’s mission success.
         • Ensure the unit understands how warfighting functions integrate into their scheme of maneuver.
         • Support command training guidance at the platoon level early in the training plan.

14  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                     Table II – Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

SCENARIO DEVELOPMENT

     2-16. Unit leadership develop STX-V scenarios based on their assessment of the unit’s tactical proficiency,
     command training guidance, and critical collective tasks that directly affect the success of the platoon and
     company to accomplish their mission. Commanders select all the tasks trained during Table II and create a
     virtual training scenario that replicates the threat and sets realistic conditions to replicate the appropriate
     initiation of the collective tasks.

     2-17. The virtual system’s operating staff develops the scenario to leverage the training system’s variety of
     capabilities. Commanders include enablers and specialty units available within the company or battalion into
     their scenarios when practical.

     2-18. Unit leadership design their scenarios in multiple lanes, phases, or stages, where each portion of the
     scenario trains one or two supporting collective tasks of a mission-essential task in a progressive manner.
     Successfully completing each lane (based on the commander’s assessment using the SOP and training and
     evaluation outlines as guides) moves the formation tactically to the next lane.

     2-19. Platoon leadership brief training audiences on the tasks for each lane for Table II. Successful
     completion of each task builds Soldier and unit proficiency, enabling intuitive execution of supporting
     collective and individual tasks during Tables IV, V, and VI.

     2-20. Table II scenarios include training on commander-selected tasks specific to the warfighting functions
     listed in figure 2-3, page 16. Unit leadership integrate these tasks as appropriate across the STX-V lanes as
     logically and as frequently as practical. Unit leadership should include multiple repetitions of those selected
     tasks during both the day and night phase of the training scenario.

     2-21. Each warfighting function includes optional or required tasks that unit leadership integrate into their
     training scenario. For special purpose weapons, unit leadership select from the weapons available to them,
     determine the use of those special purpose weapons depending on the scenario, and determine the number of
     iterations the special purpose weapons are integrated for both day and night.

WARFIGHTING FUNCTION INTEGRATION

     2-22. Unit leadership integrate the tasks as appropriate and where deemed most beneficial. The units integrate
     warfighting functions during limited and unlimited visibility conditions across the entire Table II training
     scenario event, not on each maneuver task trained.

             Note. For example, during Table II, STX-V, with the close combat tactical trainer, commanders
             select call for indirect fire during the platoon support by fire day phase where they identify that
             platoon as receiving priority of fires. As the platoon demonstrates increased proficiency during
             the scenario, the commander includes a call for fire illumination mission during limited visibility
             conditions. Call for fire is incorporated into the training during the STX-V day operations (high
             explosive [HE] and smoke) and night operations (illumination).

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  15

Chapter 2

    Figure 2-3. Integration of warfighting functions and tasks, Table II, Situational Training
                                                      Exercise-Virtual

16         TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

                                                                                     Table II – Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

TRAIN THE TRAINERS

     2-23. Units execute Table II in a variety of ways. Typically, platoons conduct training as part of Platoon
     Table II and prior to Company Table II. The primary trainers are the platoon leader and platoon sergeant with
     the section and squad leader’s assistance. The commander, first sergeant, and executive officer support the
     platoon when necessary.

     2-24. The commander must ensure the platoon leadership has the unit SOP or TACSOPs, the selected
     high-payoff collective tasks for training and evaluation, and their respective training and evaluation outlines.
     The platoon leadership is responsible for training their subordinates on why and how the unit conducts the
     supporting collective and battle tasks to support the unit’s mission-essential task.

     2-25. Training in preparation for Table II is designed for platoon key leaders to instruct the contents and
     purpose and how the Soldier uses the unit’s TACSOPs. Assigned or attached members of a small unit are
     evaluated on the content, procedures, actions, and use of the unit SOP through their demonstrated
     performance of those tasks within the simulation system.

     2-26. Commanders brief their unit on the proper execution of the STX-V scenarios. Units prepare the trainers
     by providing the following:

          • Commander’s training objectives.
          • Scenario and lane concept with collective tasks to be trained.
          • Rules of engagement for the training event.
          • Review of the training and evaluation outlines of all the tasks to be trained.
          • Review of individual tasks that directly support or complement the collective tasks.
          • Information and timing of scenarios or lanes where key warfighting functions are integrated.
          • Risk assessment.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary references.
          • Locally produced checklists for all critical individual and supporting collective tasks for objective

                evaluations.
          • Checklists based on doctrine, the unit SOPs, and approved training and evaluation outlines from

                either the Army Training Network or the Central Army Registry websites.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  17


---

Chapter 2

RECON THE SITE

     2-27. Table II, STX-V, uses an authorized virtual training system. Commanders schedule the facility as far
     in advance as practical following the respective installation’s policies and procedures. Once the method of
     training and evaluation is established, commanders should secure the training location and appropriate
     TADSS, equipment, or systems that enhance the training event.

     2-28. Once scheduled, the unit leaders conduct a reconnaissance of the training facility to—
          • Verify sufficient systems are available and operational to support the entire unit.
          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario can be achieved with appropriate
                retraining opportunities.
          • Verify facility operating hours.
          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group discussions, briefings, and
                AARs, such as sand tables or terrain boards, terrain board kits, overhead projector with screen,
                digital communications system capabilities, and so forth.
          • Confirm appropriate warfighting function integration capabilities into the concept of training lanes
                (see figure 2-3, page 16 and figure 2-4, page 23).
          • Verify the simulation system can incorporate engineers, direct fire support, special purpose
                weapons, and sniper teams into the scenario as the commander requires.
                 The simulation may not support those functions with an immersive capability.
                 The simulation should have the ability to provide notional actions with frequency modulation
                     (FM), analog, or digital means to enable the appropriate level of coordination and
                     communication, between the units conducting training (movement and maneuver).
          • Identify the level of fire support that can be included in the training scenario.
                 This includes integrating company and battalion mortar assets.
                 Most facilities provide a fire support station from which the fire support officer (FSO) or fire
                     support team (FIST) can provide notional support through proper call for fire actions.
                 The FSO or FIST can provide fires synchronization through the exercise control (EXCON)
                     center or instructor and operator station to enhance the scenario’s combat realism (fire support).
          • Determine a realistic enemy disposition suitable for the tasks being trained.
                 The higher headquarters’ intelligence staff officer (S-2) can provide the scenario a threat
                     template that is appropriate to the unit’s mission and build a more robust and effective scenario.
                 The supporting military intelligence company can provide notional or simulation injected feeds
                     replicating shadow or other unmanned systems for scenario development, as appropriate
                     (intelligence).
          • Verify the facility can support the unit’s training while in a chemical environment, based on the
                scenario design.
                 For immersive systems, verify chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear (CBRN)
                     protective systems that can be incorporated or must be provided notionally.
                 Chemical alarms, mission-oriented protective posture, vehicle system CBRN system simulated
                     capabilities, and so forth should be identified (protection).
          • Verify the facility can support unit air defense training, if necessary, units should coordinate for
                integration of small unmanned aircraft systems (sUASs) as a current threat to stimulate react to
                contact and air defense skills (protection).
          • Verify casualty evacuation (CASEVAC) notional capabilities in support of the training scenario—
                units mostly include only FM reporting and request procedures for training purposes (sustainment).
          • Identify:
                 Command post capabilities of the facility, as well as analog and digital systems integrated into
                     the simulations suites.
                 Any external tactical operations center (TOC) configuration that can be incorporated for higher
                     headquarters’ staff integration, as appropriate (mission command).

18  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                     Table II – Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

ISSUE THE ORDER

     2-29. Platoon leaders can issue an OPORD, a WARNORD, or use the training schedule, as necessary. The
     training unit must ensure all supporting elements or enablers have sufficient time for coordination and
     preparation before the training event. WARNORDs to external organizations must include sufficient reaction
     time for the appropriate personnel and equipment. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks,
     required leaders, and equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-throughs,
     rehearsals, and AARs.

     2-30. The order should include the list of tasks to evaluate during the event, as well as the method of
     evaluation. Unit leadership should provide sufficient time for subordinate leaders to train tasks and skills and
     ensure the appropriate training resources are available and accessible. Upon receipt, subordinate leaders who
     have SOPs for their organizations must ensure their procedures support and complement the higher
     headquarters’ publication.

REHEARSE

     2-31. The unit conducts a rehearsal of the training and evaluation event with all training resources, materials,
     and equipment available. During the rehearsal, unit leadership ensure sufficient time is provided for their unit
     to train to the specified standard including AARs, retraining, and validating any optional scenarios that
     increase complexity when warranted.

     2-32. The rehearsal provides the commander and the platoon leader with an opportunity to verify the tasks
     selected for Table II are consistent with the tasks, actions, and TTP of the higher organization. For maneuver
     units that are expected to cross-attach to an external unit, the rehearsal allows platoon leadership to
     synchronize the unit SOP with the TACSOP of their gaining unit or attached personnel or organizations.

     2-33. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish and confirm—
          • Training scenarios are available to the facility instructor and operators.
          • Scenarios are proofed.
          • Analog and digital systems of the training facility are available and accessible, and how any external
                systems integrate into the event execution.
          • Sufficient external evaluators or observer-coaches/trainers (OC/Ts) and required training support
                packages are available to enable multiple iterations.
          • Appropriate doctrinal publications are available for reference.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • AAR capabilities are reviewed, including—
                 Playback capability (audio, video, digital mapping).
                 Graphic overlays.
                 Terrain boards and sand tables.
                 Integrated warfighting function capabilities (live and simulation).
                 Process for conducting direct fires.
                 Methods and actions required to provide fires.
          • Process used to incorporate intelligence activities into the scenario.
          • Sufficient immersive training stations are available and operational to support the training audience.
          • Location of retraining for subordinate units, as necessary.
          • Any uniform requirements or considerations required by the facility.
          • Reproduction capabilities are at the facility, particularly printing capabilities to support the
                evaluation process.

     2-34. Once the unit completes the walk-through and rehearsal, the platoon leader makes any necessary
     adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs, as necessary.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  19


---

Chapter 2

EXECUTE

     2-35. When the unit arrives at the training location, it should reduce the amount of administration time as
     much as possible. When the entire unit is available at the training location, the unit should—

          • Conduct a safety briefing for the training event.
          • Conduct an event briefing to establish the sequence of events for the training day.
          • Conduct any simulations training required by the facility to operate the system.
          • Transition to the proper uniform for the training event.
          • Occupy the appropriate simulations stations or integrated warfighting function workstations.
          • Assign external evaluators or OC/Ts as appropriate.
          • Issue the OPORD for the training mission.
          • Execute the training scenario.

     2-36. As the STX-V scenarios are generally built in a progressive manner, the unit executes simulation
     training lanes progressively. As the commander directs, the training unit conducts informal AARs of each
     progressive scenario within the training mission. Once the training unit completes the mission through a
     specific training objective or phase within the operation, the commander conducts a formal AAR before the
     platoon leader issues a FRAGORD for the next mission or mission task.

     2-37. During training, the assigned external evaluators or OC/Ts and platoon leadership review the
     subordinate unit’s performance following the unit SOP, prescribed TTP, and the appropriate training and
     evaluation outlines.

     2-38. Units should increase the analog and digital stimulus to training units to increase the complexity of the
     training event based on their observations. Platoon leadership should sustain the tempo of the events to
     reinforce the need to execute collective tasks rapidly on demand rather than with a canned approach to
     training.

20  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                     Table II – Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

EVALUATE THE TRAINING

     2-39. Table II is a prerequisite for the unit to conduct along with the associated subordinate and attached
     elements. The unit should include all assigned and attached Soldiers in the training event based on how the
     commander determines the unit will fight to support the higher headquarters. The intent of Table II is to train
     organizations, their subordinate units, and the attached elements to the unit on collective tasks that the
     commander deems critical to their success.

     2-40. Periodically, after training scenarios are complete, the commander directs the unit to conduct—
          • An informal AAR, facilitated by the assigned external evaluators or OC/Ts, platoon leadership,
                commander, executive officer, and first sergeant, as appropriate.
          • A formal AAR led by the commander using the training notes provided by the external evaluators
                or OC/Ts, platoon leadership, executive officer, first sergeant, the facility instructor and operators,
                and others.

     2-41. Units should use the various collective task training and evaluation outlines for all training tasks. These
     products are available for download from the Army Training Network website. The unit can also use locally
     produced checklists specific to the unit TACSOPs, collective task critical and leader tasks, or performance
     measures to gain a more objective evaluation of demonstrated performance.

     2-42. With the input from the external evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership, the platoon leadership
     identifies retraining requirements as necessary, including additional scenarios. At the completion of Table II,
     key leaders discuss with their senior leaders any shortcomings, gaps, or disconnects between SOPs by echelon
     and determine any corrective actions.

     2-43. The external evaluators or OC/Ts should provide clear and articulate comments and insight on positive
     and negative actions of the units they observed. External evaluators or OC/Ts use training and evaluation
     outlines to guide them through the evaluation process and use the doctrinal publications to support their
     comments.

     2-44. The external evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership should review the training and evaluation
     outlines, training notes, and observations to determine which tasks require additional attention through the
     remainder of the training density. They also can identify where additional complexities and tactical dilemmas
     can be induced during training where mastery has been demonstrated.

     2-45. The commander should share the overall unit performance, including observations with the entire
     training unit. This allows subordinate units to learn from other units’ best practices, procedures, and tactics,
     as well as from their shortcomings or mistakes. The unit leadership should identify leaders within the
     organization who demonstrated superior performance and tactical competence by assigning them as external
     evaluators or OC/Ts during future training events, internal and external to the organization.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  21


---

Chapter 2

RETRAIN

     2-46. Commanders and platoon leadership identify the unit or subordinate unit that requires retraining of
     specific tasks during the AAR with input from the external evaluators or OC/Ts, attached leaders, and the
     facility instructors or operators. Once the platoon leadership identifies the retraining needs of the unit, they
     should execute a retraining event of the scenario. This may consist of—

          • The entire unit executing the training scenario again.
          • A select subordinate unit executing the training scenario.
          • Rotating subordinate units through different mission tasks to support the mission-essential tasks.
          • Minor tactical adjustments for the unit or subordinate units.
          • The commander may opt to discuss different methods with the platoon leadership and to complete

                the task followed by backbriefs using terrain models or sand tables rather than executing the
                simulation scenario.

     2-47. At the completion of Table II, commanders, platoon leaders, and platoon sergeants should gain insight
     to where their unit’s SOPs are working and where they need additional attention. Unit leadership should
     capture where the SOPs and TTP require attention, update, and provide those changes or revisions to the unit
     prior to executing any live-fire event. Table II provides a simulated tactical experience for the unit and
     provides clear indications of where SOP functions need improvement.

BEST PRACTICES

     2-48. Units should consider including the following best practices in Table II at the platoon level.
     Incorporation of these practices will assist the unit get their Soldiers, teams, and platoons to a higher
     proficiency rapidly. Figure 2-4 provides a quick reference to some of the best practices for the unit to consider
     when planning, preparing, and executing Table II.

22  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

                           Table II – Situational Training Exercise–Virtual

           Figure 2-4. Situational training exercise–virtual best practices, example

2-49. Units should include other integration capability methods during their recon to build the event that
covers the platoon’s task, purpose, and tactical employment objectives effectively. Not all warfighting
functions and enablers are available at every training venue. Units should consider nonstandard training
methods to incorporate to challenge the training audience.
