                                                 Chapter 3

              Table III – Situational Training Exercise

    This chapter describes the requirements of Platoon Table III, STX, and how they integrate into
    the unit training plan following the structure of the IWTS. Chapter 3 provides descriptive
    guidelines for the Platoon Table III and provides the purpose, method, and end state for the
    training event.
    STXs are short, scenario-driven, mission-oriented tactical exercises that train a single collective
    task (training and evaluation outlines) or a group of related battle drills and collective tasks
    (training and evaluation outlines), typically executed on designated training lanes. STXs
    provide leaders with a method to train using doctrinally approved tactics and techniques.

EVENT OVERVIEW

     3-1. Platoon Table III, STX, is a pre-live fire, live-environment training event that builds unit proficiency in
     specific, commander-determined critical tasks, battle tasks, and battle drills, which directly supports the
     unit’s mission-essential tasks (see figure 3-1). The table provides the commander and the platoon leadership
     with multiple progressive iterations of collective tasks that reinforce the TTP the unit employs to execute its
     mission-essential tasks. Unit leadership use their unit SOP and the appropriate training and evaluation
     outlines to develop subordinate leaders’ and elements’ movement and maneuver and battle drill execution,
     and to a limited degree, their marksmanship lethality (TADSS-based).

                     Figure 3-1. Platoon Table III, Situational Training Exercise

3-2. Unlike a battle drill, an STX does not establish the method of execution; rather the training audience’s
leadership works through tactical dilemmas while evaluating the execution of key collective tasks. STXs may
be modified based on the factors of METT-TC (I) mission, enemy, terrain and weather, troops, and support
available, time available, civil considerations, and informational considerations. STXs typically incorporate
a situationally reactive, dynamic threat via live opposing forces (OPFORs) who provide a challenging,
realistic scenario.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  25


---

Chapter 3

     3-3. Individual groups of related tasks are trained in STX lanes. Multiple lanes are used to build proficiency
     in a progressive manner during Platoon Table III. There is no limit to the number of lanes a unit can include
     while performing Platoon Table III, STX.

     3-4. STXs use commander-developed scenarios in live environments using TADSS that include—
          • Soldiers, crews, and units with their assigned equipment, weapons, systems, and platforms.
          • Integration of critical collective tasks and battle drills.
          • Employment of their assigned weapons and systems with supported laser-based TADSS.
          • Training at a combat-realistic tempo.
          • A live-environment training area of sufficient size for offensive movement and maneuver.

PURPOSE

     3-5. The STX ensures the unit, with its assigned and attached subordinate elements, demonstrates collective
     task proficiency in a live environment. The unit synchronizes and executes fire and maneuver, integrates
     indirect and direct fires, conducts critical tasks, and completes various combat reporting requirements before
     conducting tasks under live-fire conditions.

     3-6. STX lanes provide focused training on critical, supporting, collective, and battle tasks that directly
     support the unit’s mission-essential tasks. They enable the unit to execute those trained tasks based on the
     tactical situations and dilemmas presented during the FTX.

METHOD

     3-7. Platoon STXs use training areas with multiple training lanes or phases that train collective small units
     on tactical maneuver, movement, and engagement techniques at a real-time, full tempo to build critical task
     proficiency. Platoon STXs use commander-defined scenarios to replicate the appropriate stimulus that
     inspires, initiates, or instigates a desired reaction. The desired reaction includes timely and accurately
     executing critical, supporting, collective, and individual tasks.

     3-8. Table III is required for each assigned or attached subordinate unit, conducted between T-6 and T-X of
     the live-fire training event or density. Unit leadership use progressive lanes consisting of tactically grouped
     collective tasks to challenge the unit with various tactical dilemmas.

END STATE

     3-9. The unit with subordinate assigned or attached units successfully demonstrated their ability to execute
     critical or directed collective tasks and battle drills in a live environment. The platoon leadership sufficiently
     instructed the unit on their collective tasks, validated the unit’s SOP to ensure synchronization and
     consistency, reinforced previous training, and built confidence by integrating warfighting functions and
     enablers during the execution of collective tasks.

     3-10. Unit leadership is confident in the abilities of subordinate leaders and elements to execute those trained
     tasks to standard on demand based on the tactical situation. The unit is prepared for collective training against
     a trained, unscripted, and motivated OPFOR.

26  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                               Table III – Situational Training Exercise

PLAN THE TRAINING

     3-11. Table III, STX, is a live environment, hands-on training event utilizing TADSS. Table III includes
     using blank ammunition, pyrotechnics, and battle effects simulators. This event trains and evaluates
     elements’ abilities to execute critical tasks using their organic weapons, systems, and equipment during day
     and limited visibility conditions and while operating in a CBRN environment.

     3-12. The unit and its subordinate elements use Platoon Table III, STX, to prepare for the FTX external
     evaluation (EXEVAL) of the unit’s key collective tasks (Table IV).

     3-13. Platoon Table III, STX, is training designed for all subordinate units and elements within the platoon
     prior to any collective live-fire event. The training event is required for all subordinate units that are assigned,
     attached, or designated to the platoon as a—

          • Maneuver element.
          • Enabling specialty unit.
          • Cross-attached, warfighting function small unit.

PLANNING

     3-14. Each platoon is resourced 3 training days (72 hours) per year to conduct Table III. Units typically use
     between 2 and 4 training days based on the commander’s training objectives. For planning purposes, unit
     leadership should consider the following information when building their Table III scenario for training:

          • Prerequisites:
                 There are no directed prerequisites for Table III.
                 However, the unit should have completed Tables I and II prior to executing the platoon STX.

          • Reinforcement—conducting platoon STX at the beginning of the company STX as a multi-echelon
                event to reinforce and build upon previous training of the collective tasks.

          • Table duration:
                 Platoon Table III typically requires between 2 and 4 training days (96 hours) depending on the
                     commander’s training objectives.
                 It is conducted between T-6 and T-X of any live-fire event.
                 Platoons should maximize their use of available TADSS before entering the field portion of
                     their training plan.

          • Environment and conditions:
                 Units conduct Table III in a live environment and in an approved training area sufficient in size
                     to accommodate the unit during offensive and defensive operations.
                 Units conduct the training in a series of training lanes or phases at full tempo.
                 Units include a trained and equipped OPFOR when appropriate.

          • Frequency—units conduct Table III prior to platoon live-fire training a minimum of once per fiscal
                year. Units may execute multiple, shorter duration events depending on their training plan.

          • Throughput:
                 Unit leadership should consider the number of STX lanes developed for the training event and
                     ensure sufficient time is available to train and retrain as appropriate.
                 The throughput of the entire platoon through the training lanes is dependent upon the
                     complexity and length of individual lanes, amount of training area available, OPFOR and
                     external evaluators or OC/Ts availability, and other outside influences.

          • Primary training facilities:
                 Primary training facilities are training areas that support the unit’s mission by unit type,
                     collective tasks trained, weapons and system engagement ranges (when practical), as well as
                     depth and speed of movement and maneuver required during the scenario.
                 See TC 25-1 to identify the general land and training area requirements for the unit type.

     3-15. The purpose of Table III is to train the collective tasks, which are critical to a platoon and company’s
     tactical success. Unit leadership should create their Table III scenario using the task sets and collective tasks
     that directly support their mission-essential tasks.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  27


---

Chapter 3

SCENARIO REQUIREMENTS

     3-16. To accomplish their mission, company commanders develop STX lane scenarios based on dialogue
     with the battalion, their assessment of the unit’s tactical proficiency, command training guidance, and critical
     collective tasks that directly affect the success of the platoons and company. Commanders, with input from
     platoon leadership, select all the tasks that are trained during Table III and design a series of training lane
     scenarios that replicate the threat, train collective tasks, and set realistic conditions for the operating
     environment.

     3-17. Units integrate enablers and specialty units into their scenarios. The training lanes should support the
     follow-on FTX, FCX, and live-fire exercise (LFX) while reinforcing the various SOPs and previous
     collective training events.

     3-18. Commanders design their scenarios in multiple lanes, phases, or stages, where each portion of the
     scenario trains one or two collective tasks in a progressive manner. Successfully completing each lane (based
     on the unit leadership’s assessment using the SOP and training and evaluation outlines as a guide) moves the
     formation tactically to the next lane or phase.

     3-19. Table III scenarios include training on commander-selected tasks specific to the warfighting functions
     (see figure 3-1, page 25). Platoon leadership integrate these tasks as appropriate across the STX lanes. The
     scenario should include multiple repetitions of those selected tasks during the day and night phase to increase
     proficiency.

     3-20. Each warfighting function includes optional or required tasks that commanders and the platoon
     leadership integrate into their training scenario. For special purpose weapons, unit leadership determine the
     number of iterations the special purpose weapons are integrated during both day and night. Unit leadership
     select from the special purpose weapons available to them, as well as how the scenario initiates the use of
     those special purpose weapons.

             Note. Not all maneuver units have special purpose weapons associated with or available to their
             organization.

     3-21. Unit leadership integrate tasks as appropriate. Units conduct the warfighting function integrated tasks
     during limited and unlimited visibility (night and day) conditions across the entire Table III training scenario
     event, not on each maneuver task trained (see figure 3-2).

             Note. For example, during a 4-day platoon STX at a local training area, the commander selects
             call for indirect fire during the platoon movement to contact day phase where the platoon has been
             given priority of fires. On day 2 of the STX, the platoon leadership includes training for each
             subordinate platoon to conduct a call for fire illumination mission. The platoon incorporates call
             for fire into the training during STX day operations (HE and smoke) and night operations
             (illumination).

     3-22. Conducting STX lanes at the platoon level requires a significant amount of training areas, not only for
     the trained unit (blue force), but also to provide sufficient standoff, movement, and maneuver space to support
     an OPFOR. Units must consider training areas with sufficient space to develop the tactical situation for both
     blue force and OPFOR.

     3-23. Most often, units conduct STX lanes in a progressive manner, either sequentially or in rotating task
     groups. The amount of training area available, the number of collective tasks to train, the employed weapon
     system and unit capabilities, and other factors impact which method the unit uses to progress from one lane
     to subsequent lanes.

28  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

WARFIGHTING FUNCTION INTEGRATION

     3-24. Commanders may choose to integrate specific warfighting function tasks during Table III (STX) that
     can significantly increase the platoon’s proficiency for Table IV (FTX) and Table VI (LFX) evaluations. The
     commander may also elect to integrate additional tasks listed within figure 3-2 that are deemed beneficial to
     platoon training to obtain the proficiency level on the commander-selected desired tasks. Doing so enables a
     unit to maximize training opportunities on commander-selected tasks using a progressive training model
     needed to achieve the desired end state prior to evaluation.

     lane can provide a continuous battlespace for the training unit, while the OPFOR resets and prepares for the

MULTIPLE, ROTATING SITUATIONAL TRAINING EXERCISE LANES

     3-27. When space is available, units can set up several larger training sites, each with independent OPFORs.
     This allows training and evaluation of several collective tasks in random order. When there are several
     training sites, the commander has the flexibility to initiate OPFOR actions that change the training unit’s
     decision-making process, thereby altering the sequence of the tasks trained on the lane. Doing so enables a
     more realistic series of tactical dilemmas for the unit to solve and allows more freedom of maneuver. (See
     figure 3-5.)

     3-28. Using several training sites provides a more robust and dynamic threat presentation to the training unit;
     however, it requires more OPFOR resources, preparatory training for the OPFOR, and more training areas to
     support the unit’s flexibility.

DRIVERS, BRANCHES, AND SEQUELS

     3-30. Units should plan to include a series of drivers (D), branches (B), and sequels (S) to meet their training
     objectives to achieve proficiency in their platoons. These are training terms used for maneuver collective
     training that build the story boards for the training events. They provide pre-built alterations to the tactical
     missions based on the training unit’s performance, capability, or reactions to tactical dilemmas.
     3-31. They assist in steering the training unit toward the expected conditions of the task, establish the
     appropriate tempo, build the battlefield effects, and develop the tactical situation. Figure 3-7 provides an
     example of drivers, branches, and sequels for the execution of Table III.

               Figure 3-7. Drivers, branches, and sequels matrix, example

Chapter 3

TRAIN THE TRAINERS

     3-34. Units execute platoon STX in a variety of ways. Typically, each platoon conducts training (platoon
     STX) independently as part of a platoon formation prior to the company STX. The primary trainers during
     the platoon STX are the platoon leader and the platoon sergeant, with the commander, specialty units, and
     external evaluators or OC/Ts providing formal evaluation of critical and leader tasks, performance measures,
     tactical employment mentoring, and assistance, as appropriate.

     3-35. Units provide the following to prepare the trainers:
          • Commander’s training objectives.
          • Scenario and lane concept with collective tasks to be trained.
          • Rules of engagement for the training event.
          • Review of the training and evaluation outlines of all the tasks to be trained.
          • Review of the supporting and individual tasks that directly support or complement the
                mission-essential tasks.
          • Conduct of the event brief. Commanders provide how the unit executes the STX lanes.
          • Where key warfighting functions are integrated into the scenarios or lanes.
          • Risk assessment.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary references.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, blanks, and battle effects simulators used during the training event.
          • Multiple Integrated Laser Engagement System (MILES) gear certification.
          • Instruction on the proper conduct of an AAR.
          • Instruction on proper adjudication of training.

     3-36. To prepare for the best training event for the platoon, the unit should have specific training and
     certification for specific leaders and support personnel. Units should consider training and certifying the
     following personnel or teams common to STX events to augment their training.

OBSERVER-COACHES/TRAINERS

     3-37. Much like crew platform evaluators, the external evaluators or OC/Ts receive formal training in
     leadership roles during any collective exercise. This training is second only to executing the task with their
     organization. Units should dedicate sufficient time to external evaluators or OC/Ts certification to leaders
     who can provide experienced mentorship, with the complete understanding that their external evaluators or
     OC/Ts task is also formal training for them.

FIRE SUPPORT PERSONNEL

     3-38. Inclusion of an FSO, fire direction center, and mortar platoon or platoon leadership provides for subject
     matter expertise during the training event. These leaders should be certified Infantry Mortar Leader Course
     graduates and have experience in their position. They can assist in evaluating call for fire mission tasks,
     provide direction and guidance for any fire marker administrative personnel, and provide mentorship to the
     training unit on all aspects of indirect fire use.

SIMULATORS AND PYROTECHNICS

     3-39. Units employing fire marker personnel or battlefield effects devices to increase the training realism
     should certify those personnel on the proper use, handling, storage, and accountability of the various
     pyrotechnics and battle effects simulator systems planned to support the training event. Units should also
     coordinate with the supporting installation ammunition supply point for any ammunition information notices,
     safety of use messages, or required training on the devices and training munitions scheduled for the event.

38  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                               Table III – Situational Training Exercise

RECON THE SITE

     3-40. Platoons use an authorized training area or series of training areas for Table III, STX exercise. Units
     follow the installation’s policies and procedures, and formally schedule the training location as far in advance
     as practical. Once the unit has established the method of training and evaluation, it should secure the training
     location and appropriate TADSS equipment or systems that enhance the training event.

     3-41. Once scheduled, the commander, training developers, subordinate commanders, and platoon leaders
     conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the entire unit through multiple training lanes.
          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario can be achieved with appropriate

                retraining opportunities.
          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group discussions, briefings, and

                AARs.
          • Ensure sufficient space is available to conduct retraining.
          • Ensure sufficient space is available to support the use of a sand table or terrain board.
          • Confirm appropriate warfighting function integration capabilities into the concept of the training

                lanes.
          • Verify the training area can incorporate direct fire support, special purpose weapons, and sniper

                teams into the scenario, as the commander directs.
                 Identify authorized dig locations and required permits within the training area.
                 Identify restricted terrain, environmental considerations, and any NO-GO areas within the

                     training sites (movement and maneuver).
          • Ensure sufficient space is available for refueling and rearming.
          • Identify any hazards in the training area.
          • Identify a TADSS (laser-based system) troubleshooting, alignment, and contact team support area.
          • Identify the level of fire support that can be included into the training scenario—the FSO or FIST

                can provide fires synchronization to enhance the scenario’s combat realism (fire support).
          • Provide the scenario with a threat template that is appropriate to the unit’s mission and build a more

                robust and effective scenario (higher headquarters’ S-2).
          • Verify the training area can support the unit’s training while in a chemical environment, based on

                the scenario design (protection).
          • Verify CASEVAC and notional medical evacuation (MEDEVAC) capabilities in support of the

                training scenario—units mostly include only FM reporting and request procedures for training
                purposes (sustainment).
          • Identify command post capabilities and locations in the training areas (mission command).
          • Identify any external TOC configurations that can be incorporated for higher headquarters’ element
                integration, as appropriate (mission command).

     3-42. When conducting a reconnaissance of the training location, units should include reviewing the training
     area to accommodate integration of the required and optional warfighting functions. Figure 3-10, page 40
     provides some considerations for each training requirement.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  39

    Figure 3-10. Recon the training site for warfighting function integration, examples

40         TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

                                                                                               Table III – Situational Training Exercise

ISSUE THE ORDER

     3-43. Commanders can issue an OPORD, WARNORD, or use the training schedule, as necessary. The
     training unit must ensure all supporting elements or enablers have sufficient time to coordinate and prepare
     before the training event. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-throughs, rehearsals, and AARs.
     WARNORDs for external organizations must include sufficient reaction time for the appropriate personnel
     and equipment needed.

     3-44. The OPORD or WARNORD should include the list of tasks that will be evaluated during the event and
     the method of evaluation. Unit leadership should provide sufficient time for subordinate leaders to train the
     tasks and skills listed in the order. Unit leadership should ensure the appropriate training resources are
     available and accessible. Upon receipt, subordinate leaders who have SOPs for their organizations must
     ensure their procedures support and complement the higher headquarters’ publication.

REHEARSE

     3-45. The unit uses all training resources, materials, and equipment available to conduct rehearsals of training
     events. During the rehearsal, platoon leadership ensures their unit has sufficient time to train to the specified
     standard including AARs and retraining time. Unit leadership ensure their unit verifies optional scenarios
     that increase complexity when warranted.

     3-46. The platoon leadership use the rehearsal as an opportunity to verify the tasks selected for Table III are
     consistent with the tasks, actions, and TTP of the higher organization. For maneuver units that expect to
     cross-attach to an external unit, the rehearsal is an opportunity for the platoon leadership to synchronize the
     unit SOP with the TACSOP of their gaining unit or attached personnel or organizations.

     3-47. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish and confirm—
          • The OPFOR understands the training scenarios.
          • The flow of training units through the STX lanes is feasible.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide standoff distances for the unit and OPFOR to build the
                appropriate tempo of each STX lane.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and to the higher headquarters
                regardless of their location.
          • Additional external evaluators or OC/Ts training support packages are complete and provide for
                multiple iterations.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • AAR capabilities are reviewed.
          • Available warfighting function enablers are integrated with the unit or appropriate subordinate unit.
          • The process for conducting direct fire support tasks.
          • Methods and actions required to provide fires.
          • The process used to incorporate intelligence activities into the scenario.
          • Real world CASEVAC and MEDEVAC plans.
          • Key leader locations.

     3-48. Once the unit completes the walk-through and training rehearsal, the platoon leader makes any
     necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs, as necessary.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  41


---

Chapter 3

EXECUTE

     3-49. Once the unit arrives at the training location, they should—
          • Conduct a safety briefing for the training event.
          • Conduct sensitive item checks before implementing training.
          • Assign external evaluators or OC/Ts as appropriate.
          • Conduct the event briefing to establish the sequence of events for the training day.
          • Issue the OPORD for the training mission.
          • Occupy the appropriate STX lane as the commander directs.
          • Execute the training scenario.

     3-50. The training unit conducts informal AARs of each progressive scenario within the training mission as
     the commander or platoon leadership directs. Once the training unit completes the mission through a specific
     training objective or phase of the operation, the commander or platoon leadership conducts a formal AAR
     before issuing a FRAGORD for the next mission or mission task.

     3-51. During training, the assigned external evaluators or OC/Ts, commander, platoon leader, and platoon
     sergeant review the subordinate unit’s performance. They follow the unit SOP, prescribed TTP, and the
     appropriate training and evaluation outlines.

     3-52. Commanders should escalate the analog and digital stimuli to the training units to increase the
     complexity of the training event based on their observations. Platoon leadership should sustain the tempo of
     the events to reinforce the need to execute collective tasks rapidly and on demand rather than with a canned
     approach to training.

     3-53. Units should ensure they use their driver, branch, and sequel execution matrix created during the
     planning process. The matrix will provide consistency in training and tempo of the operation by providing
     pre-defined cues and stimulus to the training unit.

42  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                               Table III – Situational Training Exercise

EVALUATE THE TRAINING

     3-54. The unit should include all assigned and attached Soldiers and elements in the training event based on
     how the commander and the platoon leadership determine the unit should execute its collective tasks to
     support the combatant commander. The intent of STXs is to train the organization, including its subordinate
     and attached units, on specific collective tasks that the commander deems critical.

     3-55. Periodically after training scenarios are complete, the platoon leadership directs the unit to conduct—
          • An informal AAR, facilitated by the assigned external evaluators or OC/Ts, commander, platoon
                leader, and platoon sergeant, as appropriate.
          • A formal AAR led by the commander and platoon leader using the training notes provided by the
                external evaluators or OC/Ts, platoon sergeant, subordinate leadership, the facility instructor,
                operators, and others.

     3-56. At the completion of the training event, key leaders discuss with their senior leaders any shortcomings,
     gaps, or disconnects among echelon SOPs, and determine corrective actions, if any. With the input from the
     external evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership, the commander provides retraining opportunities as
     necessary, including additional scenarios.

             Note. At the completion of the platoon training strategy, the key leaders and their commander will
             assess their performance during all six training events (Tables I through VI), review their SOPs,
             and validate their accuracy and effectiveness for continued use.

     3-57. The external evaluators or OC/Ts should provide clear and articulate comments and insight into the
     positive and negative actions of the subordinate units they observed. External evaluators or OC/Ts should
     use the training and evaluation outlines to guide them through the evaluation process and use the doctrinal
     publications to support their comments.

     3-58. The external evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership should review the training and evaluation outlines
     and training notes and observations to determine which tasks require additional attention through the
     remainder of the training density. They also can identify where additional complexities to the scenario can
     be induced during training where mastery has been demonstrated.

     3-59. The external evaluators or OC/Ts should share the overall performance of the unit, including
     observations, with the entire training unit. This allows the subordinate units to learn from other units’ best
     practices, procedures, and tactics, and at times from their shortcomings or mistakes. The unit leadership
     should identify leaders within the organization who demonstrate superior performance and tactical
     competence and assign them as external evaluators or OC/Ts for future training events, internal and external
     to the organization.

     3-60. At the completion of Platoon Table III, STX, platoon leaders and platoon sergeants should gain insight
     to where their unit’s SOPs are working and where they need additional attention. If time is available, platoon
     leadership should update and provide those changes or revisions to the unit prior to executing any live-fire
     event.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  43


---

Chapter 3

RETRAIN

     3-61. Unit leadership identify the unit or subordinate unit that requires retraining of specific tasks during the
     AAR with input from the external evaluators or OC/Ts, attached leaders, and the facility instructors or
     operators. Once the commander or the platoon leadership identifies the retraining needs of the unit, they
     should execute a retraining event of the scenario. This may consist of—

          • The entire platoon conducting the training scenario again.
          • Rotating platoons through different mission tasks in specific phases of the STX lanes to reinforce

                supporting collective tasks that need additional repetitions to achieve the desired proficiency.
          • Minor tactical adjustments for the unit, and the platoon leader may opt to discuss different methods

                to complete the task followed by backbriefs using terrain models or sand tables rather than executing
                the entire training scenario.

     3-62. Once the unit leadership are satisfied with the unit’s demonstrated performance, they should review the
     unit SOP and TTP for required updates, revisions, or modifications again. The platoon leader and platoon
     sergeant should assess which components of the unit’s SOP and TTP need additional attention during the
     upcoming FTX, FCX, and LFX.

44  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

methods to incorporate to challenge the training audience.

                                                 Chapter 4

                  Table IV – Field Training Exercise

    This chapter describes the requirements of Platoon Table IV, FTX. The chapter describes how
    the requirements integrate into the unit training plan following the structure of the IWTS.
    Chapter 4 provides descriptive guidelines for the Platoon Table IV, FTX, and provides the
    purpose, method, and end state for the training event.
    The FTX supports training at squad/section, platoon, company, battalion, and brigade levels.
    The FTX integrates the total force in a realistic combat environment and supports a demanding,
    effective EXEVAL of the unit’s mission-essential tasks and supporting collective task
    proficiency.

EVENT OVERVIEW

     4-1. An FTX is a high-cost, high-overhead exercise conducted under simulated combat conditions in the field
     using laser-based TADSS, pyrotechnics, and battle effects to train a unit’s collective tasks (see figure 4-1).
     An FTX culminates in an EXEVAL of the unit’s proficiency in executing the critical collective tasks and the
     collective tasks that the evaluation commander selects.

               Figure 4-1. Platoon Table IV, Field Training Exercise

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                             47


---

Chapter 4

     4-2. An FTX involves the entire combined arms force and encompasses training, such as battle drills, crew
     drills, supporting collective tasks, and mission-essential tasks to reinforce Soldier, team, squad, crew, and
     collective training integration. The FTX provides the ability to—

          • Demonstrate proficiency maneuvering the entire formation in time and space.
          • Build skills to develop tactical situations rapidly.
          • Integrate and leverage available battlefield intelligence in a timely manner to influence leader

                decision making.
          • Evaluate the unit holistically to doctrinal standards, concepts, and TTP.
          • Employ integrated warfighting functions to provide an unfair advantage and overmatch to

                subordinate units.
          • Develop and overcome situations with real-time information in a demanding and rigorous series of

                tactical dilemmas.
          • Build unit cohesion, effectiveness, and esprit de corps.
          • Train and mentor subordinate leaders on maneuver tasks, skills, and actions.
          • Identify training weaknesses for the development of future training plans.

     4-3. The Platoon FTX provides significant training value to the key leaders and subordinates within the
     organization. The FTX trains and enables the leaders, subordinate elements, and attached elements to—

          • Move and maneuver units realistically.
          • Employ organic weapons systems effectively.
          • Build teamwork and cohesion.
          • Plan and coordinate supporting fires.
          • Plan and coordinate logistical activities to support tactical operations.
          • Integrate warfighting functions and enablers.
          • Build tactical experience and understanding.

     4-4. Platoon Table IV, FTX, is conducted in a live environment and builds proficiency in the unit’s collective
     tasks and specific commander-determined critical tasks, which directly support the unit’s mission. Platoon
     Table IV is an event the commander uses to evaluate the unit’s execution of its collective tasks during
     combat-realistic scenarios against a trained and capable near peer OPFOR.

     4-5. This event enables the training unit to execute its collective tasks that relate to the higher headquarters’
     mission-essential tasks on demand where the platoon leadership acts upon the various stimuli and dilemmas
     presented by a free-acting OPFOR. Unit leadership use their unit SOP and the appropriate training and
     evaluation outlines to develop subordinate leaders and element’s movement and maneuver, and to a limited
     degree, marksmanship lethality (TADSS-based).

     4-6. FTXs use commander developed scenarios in live environments using TADSS that include—
          • Soldiers, crews, small units, and enablers.
          • Assigned weapons, platforms, systems, and small units.
          • Training at a combat-realistic, fast-paced tempo.
          • A live-environment training area.
          • A live OPFOR that develops tactical dilemmas for the training unit to solve and overcome.

48  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                       Table IV – Field Training Exercise

PURPOSE

     4-7. Table IV, FTX, is a training and EXEVAL of a unit with its assigned and attached subordinate elements
     that demonstrate proficiency executing their collective tasks in a live environment. Table IV includes
     assessing their ability to synchronize and execute fire and movement, integrate indirect and direct fires,
     conduct critical collective tasks, and complete various combat reporting requirements.

     4-8. The FTX is an externally evaluated assessment of the unit’s proficiency conducting its assigned
     collective tasks. The evaluation team uses the appropriate training and evaluation outlines for the unit’s
     collective tasks to determine the unit’s proficiency.

METHOD

     4-9. The FTX uses a training area sufficient in size to replicate an operating environment to train maneuver
     units on tactical maneuver, movement, and engagement techniques at a real-time full tempo. The FTX uses
     a commander-defined scenario and missions to execute its collective tasks. The FTX’s scenario provides a
     timely and accurate execution of critical, supporting, collective, and individual tasks to subordinate units.

     4-10. The commander-designed scenario includes sufficient tactical stimulus to train and evaluate the
     subordinate unit operating in a combat-realistic live environment while using the appropriate laser-based
     training system and other TADSS. Evaluations are conducted by a trained and certified external evaluator or
     OC/T team using the approved training and evaluation outlines as evaluation tools.

END STATE

     4-11. The subordinate units assigned or attached to the organization successfully demonstrated in a live
     environment their ability to execute their collective tasks. Table IV trains and evaluates the collective tasks
     that support the higher echelon unit’s mission-essential tasks, in a live environment using realistic scenarios
     and trained OPFOR.

     4-12. For the commander and the platoon leadership, Table IV provides a means to—
          • Validate the subordinate unit’s SOP to ensure synchronization and consistency with the unit SOP.
          • Reinforce previous training focused on critical skills and tasks with multiple training iterations.
          • Build confidence by integrating warfighting functions and enablers.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  49


---

Chapter 4

PLAN THE TRAINING

     4-13. Platoon Table IV, FTX, is a live-environment training event where squads/sections and platoons train,
     then evaluate their collective task proficiency. Once the unit’s training is complete, the unit conducts an
     EXEVAL exercise to determine its proficiency executing its collective tasks.

     4-14. The training scenarios use laser-based training devices, blank ammunition, pyrotechnics, and battle
     effects simulators, as well as an appropriately trained and equipped OPFOR. The EXEVAL uses training and
     evaluation outlines for all maneuver collective tasks.

     4-15. Table IV, FTX, is training designed for the platoon with all assigned or attached subordinate units and
     elements. This training event is the required EXEVAL to meet the requirements of the maneuver platoon
     collective task proficiency gate, as identified in AR 220-1 and AR 350-1.

     4-16. Each company is authorized a minimum of 3 total training days (72 hours) per year to conduct Platoon
     Table IV training according to the CATS. Units may conduct Table IV, FTX, before or after Table VI, LFX,
     depending on the training area and facility availability, the commander’s intent, or the unit training plan’s
     needs.

             Note. Table IV, FTX is not a prerequisite to conduct Table VI, LFX.

     4-17. For planning purposes, unit leadership should consider several requirements when building their
     Table IV scenario for training and external EXEVAL:

          • Prerequisites—complete Platoon Tables I, II, and III prior to conducting Table IV to ensure the unit
                is adequately trained on the critical collective tasks prior to the EXEVAL.

          • Table duration:
                 The FTX is a multiday, complex, rigorous training event that provides increased stressors,
                     stimulus, and tactical dilemmas to the maneuver unit.
                 It should be integrated into the unit’s platoon training plan to maximize resources and
                     multi-echelon training opportunities.

          • Environment and conditions:
                 Units conduct FTX in a live environment on an approved training area sufficient in size to
                     accommodate the unit one level higher (platoon) during offensive and defensive operations,
                     including maneuver space for the assigned trained and equipped OPFOR.
                 Units conduct the FTX during day, night, and limited visibility.

          • Training days required:
                 Successful completion of Table IV requires a minimum of 1 training day per platoon.
                 The training day includes preparatory, tactical training to build on the STX lanes during
                     Table III and provide sufficient time for an effective tactical EXEVAL.

          • Frequency:
                 Most units complete the FTX once per year.
                 The unit may complete an FTX more than once a year if multiple, platoon-size, live-fire
                     densities are scheduled across the fiscal year or training year as directed by the higher
                     headquarters.

          • Throughput:
                 Company commanders should consider the number of platoons conducting Table IV.
                 If sufficient training space is available, all platoons can complete Table III, STX within a single
                     training day prior to executing Platoon Table IV, FTX.
                 For installations with training area constraints, units may conduct Table IV exercises in a
                     rotational fashion one platoon at a time.
                 Units may conduct Table IV exercises in noncontiguous training areas when necessary to
                     increase throughput of the company overall, although the least preferred method.
                 Regardless of the number of simultaneous FTX events, units must ensure sufficient time is
                     available to train, evaluate, and retrain, as appropriate.

50  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                       Table IV – Field Training Exercise

          • Primary training facilities:
                 Training areas that support the unit’s mission by unit type, collective tasks trained, weapons
                     and system engagement ranges (when practical), as well as depth and speed of movement and
                     maneuver required during the scenario are required (see TC 25-1 to identify the general land
                     and training area requirements for the unit type and task),
                 Units should schedule training areas sufficient to support training one echelon higher to provide
                     training and evaluation of a company’s worth of platoons simultaneously and as a precursor to
                     the platoon’s FTX.

SCENARIO REQUIREMENTS

     4-18. To accomplish their mission, commanders develop the FTX scenarios based on their assessment of the
     unit’s tactical proficiency, command training guidance, and critical collective tasks that directly affect the
     success of the platoon and company. Commanders select all appropriate collective tasks for evaluation and
     craft a scenario with varying branches and sequels that replicates the threat, collective tasks for training, and
     realistic conditions.

     4-19. Units include available enablers and specialty units within the company and battalion into their
     scenarios. The training mission should support future company and battalion collective training events while
     reinforcing the unit SOP and previous collective training events.

     4-20. Unit leadership design their scenarios in multiple phases, or stages, where each portion of the scenario
     specifically trains one or two collective tasks in a progressive manner. Successful completion of each phase
     (based on the commander’s assessment using the SOP and training and evaluation outlines as a guide) moves
     the formation tactically to a continuation of the mission, branch, or sequel. Commanders select the level of
     stimulus, rigor, and dilemma complexity based on their assessment of the unit’s demonstrated performance.

     4-21. The FTX scenario must include training and evaluation opportunities for all the unit’s tactical collective
     tasks, as well as any commander-selected tasks specific to the warfighting functions listed in figure 4-2,
     page 53. Unit leadership integrate these tasks as appropriate across the FTX scenario, during day and night
     phases of training.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  51


---

Chapter 4

     4-22. Each warfighting function includes optional or required tasks that commanders integrate into their
     training scenario. Unit leadership integrate those tasks as appropriate or when most beneficial and realistic.
     Units conduct the warfighting function integrated tasks during limited and unlimited visibility (night and
     day) conditions, across the entire FTX training scenario event, not during each maneuver collective task.

             Note. For example, during a multiday FTX at a local training area, the commander selects call for
             indirect fire during the platoon attack day phase, where the commander identifies the platoon as
             receiving priority of fires. On day 2 of the FTX, the platoon leadership includes training for the
             platoon and each subordinate element to conduct a call for fire illumination mission. Call for fire
             is incorporated into the training during FTX day operations (HE and smoke) and night operations
             (illumination), meeting one of the warfighting function integration requirements.

     4-23. Units are required to integrate the warfighting functions during the day and night phase at least once
     for evaluation purposes. They are not required to include them on every training day. This allows the unit to
     tailor the training and evaluation across the full training days available and progressively increase the rigor
     and complexity of the tactical situations presented based on their observations (see figure 4-1, page 47).

     4-24. Conducting FTX at the platoon level requires significant amounts of training areas, not only for the
     trained unit, but to provide sufficient standoff and maneuver space to support a trained and equipped OPFOR.
     Each day, the unit may opt to alter the primary direction of the mission to vary the battlespace of the training
     unit. Simply changing the orientation and direction of an attack from 1 training day or phase to another allows
     the unit to maximize the training land available.

52  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                       Table IV – Field Training Exercise

WARFIGHTING FUNCTION INTEGRATION

     4-25. Commanders must integrate specific warfighting functions during Table IV, FTX. How the unit
     integrates specific tasks for the warfighting function requirements is up to the commander. Commanders
     should elect tasks that were previously trained during Table III, and build proficiency through repetition for
     success on Table VI, LFX.
     4-26. The commander must integrate tasks associated with the warfighting functions listed within figure 4-2.
     These requirements are designed to build upon previous training provided during Tables I through III.

Figure 4-2. Warfighting function integration requirements, Table IV, Field Training Exercise

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  53

Chapter 4

TRAINING AREA BATTLESPACE

     4-27. When space is available, units can set establish a large training area that supports platoon training with
     platoon movement and maneuver space, separate assembly area locations for both the training unit and
     OPFOR, sufficient space to deploy the force from a column formation, and multiple phases of the operation
     with full-caliber depths.

     4-28. The training area must be fully capable of providing an independent OPFOR element designed to meet
     the appropriate threat composition by task and phase of the mission. This allows training and evaluation of
     several collective tasks in random order at the demands of the tactical situation as it continues to evolve.

     4-29. When sufficient training area space is available the commander has the flexibility to initiate OPFOR
     actions that change the training unit’s decision-making process. This enables the altering of the sequence of
     tasks evaluated. Doing so enables a more realistic, tactically relevant, series of dilemmas for the unit to solve
     and allows sufficient freedom of maneuver to both the training unit and the OPFOR.

     4-30. Using larger training areas provides a more robust and dynamic threat presentation to the training unit;
     however, it requires more OPFOR resources, preparatory training for the OPFOR, and support personnel
     such as fire markers, OC/Ts, and external warfighting function integration.

     4-31. During planning, the unit develops a concept sketch of the tactical set using common graphics. This
     builds the concept of training, provides the draft graphics and associated control measures, and defines the
     battlespace for training. As shown in figure 4-3 there are four phases for the mission concept, where a phase
     of the operation or mission is planned between each PL.

     4-32. Sufficient space is provided for a robust and agile OPFOR. This includes sufficient space for alignment
     of laser-based training systems, tactical assembly area to enable small-unit training, and sufficient space
     where the two forces do not have line of sight with one another prior to execution of the training event.

DRIVERS, BRANCHES, AND SEQUELS

     4-33. Units should plan to include a series of drivers, branches, and sequels to meet their training objectives
     to achieve proficiency in their platoons. These are training terms used for maneuver collective training that
     build the story boards for the training events. They provide pre-build alterations to the tactical missions based
     on the training unit’s performance, capability, or reactions to tactical dilemmas.
     4-34. They assist in steering the training unit toward the expected conditions of the task, establish the
     appropriate tempo, build the battlefield effects, and develop the tactical situation. Figure 4-4 provides various
     mission stimulus for EXCON of the training event.

PREPARE

     4-36. Units execute the FTX in a variety of ways. The primary trainer is the company commander supported
     by the platoon leadership, headquarters personnel, and assigned external evaluators or OC/Ts. Units evaluate
     the Table IV event externally and require the battalion to coordinate for trained external evaluators or OC/Ts.

                        UNIT SELF-EVALUATION NOT AUTHORIZED

          Units are not authorized to self-evaluate. The EXEVAL portion of the FTX requires that
          the higher headquarters schedule and coordinate for the appropriate number and type
          of external evaluators or OC/Ts for evaluation purposes.

     4-37. As the evaluation officer for the platoon, the company or troop commander must ensure the unit SOP
     or TACSOP, the selected high-payoff collective tasks for training and evaluation, and their respective training
     and evaluation outline are available to the subordinate leadership. The company or troop leadership is
     responsible for training platoons on the why and how the unit conducts the collective tasks to support the
     unit’s mission-essential task.

     4-38. Units include the higher headquarters’ staff and specialty elements (mortars, scouts, sniper teams,
     medics, sustainers) in the training and execution of Table IV, when appropriate. This allows subordinate
     leaders and Soldiers to understand the company or troop’s processes and procedures to broaden their
     knowledge of the unit’s operations and capabilities. Integrating specialty elements enables the unit to meet
     some of the warfighting function integration requirements.

     4-39. Units provide the following to prepare the trainers:
          • Commander’s training objectives.
          • Scenario concept with collective tasks to be trained and evaluated.
          • Rules of engagement for the training event.
          • Review of the training and evaluation outlines of the mission-essential tasks evaluated.
          • Procedures for evaluating tasks.
          • Review of supporting and individual tasks that directly support or complement the supporting
                collective and mission-essential tasks.
          • Conduct of the event brief. Commanders provide how the unit will execute the FTX.
          • Risk assessment.
          • MILES certification.
          • Identification of supporting doctrinal publications for use as primary references.
          • TADSS, pyrotechnics, blanks, and battle effects simulators used during the training event.

     4-40. To prepare for the best training event for the platoon, the unit should have specific training and
     certification for specific leaders and support personnel. Units should consider training and certifying the
     following personnel or teams common to FTX events to augment their training:

Chapter 4

EXERCISE CONTROL

     4-41. The unit plans and prepares to execute control of the training event. Planning identifies the EXCON
     structure for the event. The number of personnel varies from unit to unit and event to event. Units should
     coordinate for external support early to prepare those tasked Soldiers for their roles and responsibilities during
     the event.

     4-42. Units should consider merging the squad/section FTX event with the platoon FTX. This allows the
     evaluation of the various platoons to leverage the support personnel, training areas, systems, and EXCON
     from the platoon event. The platoon event becomes a precursor to the platoon force-on-force event.

     4-43. Figure 4-6 shows a common, although complex EXCON structure that uses organic and external
     support. This structure can easily adapt to support platoon training events such as Platoon Table III, STX and
     Platoon Table IV, FTX.

    Figure 4-6. Exercise control structure, example

                                                                                                       Table IV – Field Training Exercise

OBSERVER-COACHES/TRAINERS

     4-44. The FTX requires EXEVAL by trained and certified leaders. For the platoon, these evaluators should
     be external to the company. A best practice for the FTX is to use this training event as a precursor to the
     Platoon’s Table IV, FTX. This can maximize the use of certified external evaluators and other support
     personnel. Units should dedicate sufficient time to external evaluators or OC/Ts certification to leaders who
     can provide experienced mentorship, with the complete understanding that their external evaluators or OC/Ts
     task is also formal training for them.

FIRE SUPPORT PERSONNEL

     4-45. Inclusion of an FSO, fire direction center, and mortar platoon or platoon leadership provides for subject
     matter expertise during the training event. These leaders should be certified Infantry Mortar Leader Course
     graduates and have experience in their position. They can assist in evaluating call for fire mission tasks,
     provide direction and guidance for any fire marker administrative personnel, and provide mentorship to the
     training unit on all aspects of indirect fire use.

FIRE MARKERS

     4-46. Units employing fire marker personnel or battlefield effects devices to increase the training realism
     should certify those personnel on the proper use, handling, storage, and accountability of the various
     pyrotechnics and battle effects simulator systems planned to support the training event. Units should also
     coordinate with the supporting installation ammunition supply point for any ammunition information notices,
     safety of use messages, or required training on the devices and training munitions scheduled for the event.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  61


---

Chapter 4

RECON THE SITE

     4-47. Table IV, FTX, uses an authorized training area or series of training areas. Commanders schedule the
     training location as far in advance as practical following the installation’s policies and procedures. Once the
     method of training and evaluation is established, unit leadership should secure the training location and
     appropriate TADSS, equipment, or systems that enhance the training event.

     4-48. Once scheduled, the commander and platoon leaders conduct a reconnaissance of the training areas
     to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the training unit including branches and sequels.
          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario can be achieved with appropriate

                retraining opportunities.
          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group discussions, briefings, and

                AARs.
          • Provide a sand table or terrain board.
          • Provide terrain board kits.
          • Confirm appropriate warfighting function integration capabilities into the concept of the training

                event (see figure 4-2, page 53).
          • Verify the training area can incorporate direct fire support, special purpose weapons, and sniper

                teams into the scenario, as the commander directs.
          • Identify authorized dig locations and required permits within the training area.
          • Identify restricted terrain, environmental considerations, and any NO-GO areas within the training

                sites (movement and maneuver).
          • Identify the level of fires that can be included into the training scenario:

                 The FSO or FIST provides fires synchronization and realism.
                 Units should include a fire marker plan to enhance the battle effects of the mission, where

                     appropriate (fire support).
          • Provide the scenario a threat template that is appropriate to the unit’s mission and build a more

                robust and effective scenario (higher headquarters’ S-2).
          • Verify the training area can support the unit’s training while in a chemical environment, based on

                the scenario design (protection).
          • Identify any hazards within the training area.
          • Identify any sustainment limitations.
          • Verify CASEVAC notional or live capabilities in support of the training scenario (sustainment).
          • Identify command post capabilities and locations in the training areas.
          • Identify any external TOC configuration that can be incorporated for higher headquarters’ staff

                integration, as appropriate (mission command).

Chapter 4

ISSUE THE ORDER

     4-50. Commanders can issue an OPORD, a WARNORD, or use the training schedule, as necessary. The
     training unit must ensure all supporting elements or enablers have sufficient time to coordinate and prepare
     before the training event.

     4-51. WARNORDs to external organizations must include sufficient reaction time for the appropriate
     personnel and equipment. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-throughs, rehearsals, and AARs.

     4-52. The OPORD or WARNORD should include the list of tasks evaluated during the event and the method
     of evaluation. Unit leadership should provide sufficient time for subordinate leaders to train the tasks and
     skills listed in the order. Unit leadership should ensure the appropriate training resources are available and
     accessible. Upon receipt, subordinate leaders who have SOPs for their organizations must ensure their
     procedures support and complement the higher headquarters’ publication.

     4-53. The order should include the specific mission orders the training units will receive as part of the training
     event. Those orders, FRAGORDs, and WARNORDs define the tasks required for the training unit to perform
     and outline the scheme of maneuver for the OPFOR’s conduct during training.

REHEARSE

     4-54. The company or battalion conducts a rehearsal of the training event with all training resources,
     materials, and equipment available. During the rehearsal, commanders or battalion staff members ensure
     sufficient time is provided for the unit to train to the specified standard, including AARs and retraining time,
     and verify optional scenarios that increase complexity when warranted.

     4-55. For maneuver units expecting to cross-attach to an external unit, the rehearsal is an opportunity for the
     platoon leadership to synchronize the unit SOP with the TACSOP of their gaining unit or attached personnel
     or organizations.

     4-56. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish and confirm—
          • Training scenarios are understood by the OPFOR.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide standoff distances for the unit and OPFOR to build the
                appropriate tempo of each mission or phase of the operation.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and are established at the higher
                headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional external evaluators or OC/Ts training support packages are complete and provide for
                multiple iterations.
          • Appropriate doctrinal publications are available for reference.
          • AAR capabilities are reviewed.
          • Available warfighting function enablers are integrated with the unit or appropriate subordinate unit.
          • The process for conducting direct fire support tasks.
          • The scheme of fires.
          • Methods and actions required to provide fires.
          • The process used to incorporate intelligence activities into the scenario.
          • The location of retraining for subordinate units, as necessary.
          • The CASEVAC and MEDEVAC plans.
          • The external evaluators or OC/Ts communications plan.
          • The flow of reporting.
          • The transition plan between units being trained.
          • Communication plan and reports for training completion to higher headquarters.

     4-57. The commander makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs once the unit
     completes the walk-through and training rehearsal.

64  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                           Table IV – Field Training Exercise

EXECUTE

4-58. Once the unit arrives at the training location, it should—                          other  training
     • Assign external evaluators or OC/Ts as appropriate.
     • Receive a safety briefing for the training event.
     • Conduct the exercise briefing to establish the sequence of events, timelines, and
          synchronization information.
     • Occupy the appropriate assembly area or battle position as the commander directs.
     • Issue the OPORD for the training mission.
     • Conduct sensitive item checks before executing training.
     • Receive the company OPORD or FRAGORD.
     • Execute the training scenario.

4-59. The training unit conducts informal AARs of each progressive scenario within the training mission as
the commander directs. Once the training unit completes the mission through a specific training objective or
phase of the operation, the commander conducts a formal AAR prior to issuing a FRAGORD for the next
mission or mission task.

4-60. During training, the assigned external evaluators or OC/Ts, commander, and platoon leadership review
the subordinate unit’s performance. They follow the relevant doctrine, unit SOP, prescribed TTP, and the
appropriate training and evaluation outlines.

4-61. Commanders should escalate the analog and digital stimuli to the training units to increase the
complexity of the training event based on their observations. Unit leadership sustain the tempo of the events
to reinforce the need to execute collective tasks rapidly and on demand rather than with a canned, one task
at a time approach to training used during the STX lanes.

4-62. During execution, commanders consider increasing the difficulty, complexity, or rigor to the training
events by various means using scenario branches or sequels. The following are examples of tactical stressors
that commanders can instigate:

     • Increase OPFOR indirect fires.
     • Direct additional OPFOR engagements to provide additional tactical dilemmas.
     • Issue FRAGORDs directing the unit to change the mission rapidly.
     • Direct trainers to assess battle damage or casualties within the unit.
     • Increase OPFOR tactical responses and pressures to challenge ammunition consumption, fire

          control, distribution, and increased calls for support.
     • Introduce chemical environments into specific phases of a mission.
     • Alter the composition of the OPFOR.
     • Add neutral noncombatants to the scenario.
     • Transfer ammunition.
     • Assess key leaders as casualties.
     • Electronic warfare actions such as Global Positioning System (GPS) denial.
     • Assess a mass casualty event.
     • Introduce an air threat such as unmanned aircraft systems (UASs).

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                                                        65


---

Chapter 4

EVALUATE THE TRAINING

     4-63. The unit should include all assigned and attached Soldiers and elements in the training event based on
     the commander’s determination of how the unit will fight to support the combatant commander. The intent
     of the FTX is to provide an EXEVAL of the organization, its subordinate units, and attached elements on
     their collective tasks that the commander deems critical to its success.

     4-64. Periodically after completing a phase, branch, or sequel of the training scenario, the commander may
     direct the unit to conduct—

          • An informal AAR facilitated by the assigned external evaluators or OC/Ts, commander, and platoon
                leadership, as appropriate.

          • A formal AAR led by the commander using the training notes provided by the external evaluators
                or OC/Ts, executive officer, first sergeant, and platoon leadership.

     4-65. At the completion of the training event, key leaders and their senior leaders discuss any shortcomings,
     gaps, or disconnects between SOPs by echelon, and determine any corrective actions. With input from the
     external evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership, the commander provides retraining opportunities as
     necessary, including additional scenarios.

             Note. At the completion of the platoon training strategy, the key leaders and their commander
             assess their performance during all six training events (Tables I through VI), review their SOPs,
             and validate their accuracy and effectiveness for continued use.

     4-66. External evaluators or OC/Ts should provide clear and articulate comments and insight on the positive
     and negative actions of the subordinate units they observed. External evaluators or OC/Ts use the training
     and evaluation outlines to guide them through the evaluation process and use a variety of doctrinal
     publications to support their comments, discussions, and critiques of the unit observed.

     4-67. External evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership should review the training and evaluation outlines,
     training notes, and observations, to determine which tasks require additional attention through the remainder
     of the training density. They also can identify where additional complexities and tactical challenges can be
     induced during training where mastery has been demonstrated.

     4-68. The commander should share the overall performance of the unit, including observations, with the
     entire training unit. This allows subordinate units to learn from other units’ best practices, procedures, and
     tactics, and, at times, from their shortcomings or mistakes. The commander should identify leaders within
     the organization who have demonstrated superior performance and tactical competence by assigning them as
     an external evaluator or OC/T during future training events, internal and external to the organization.

     4-69. At the completion of the FTX, unit leadership gain insight into which of their unit’s SOPs are working
     and which need additional attention. If time is available, commanders, platoon leaders, and platoon sergeants
     should capture which SOPs require attention, update, and provide those changes or revisions to the unit prior
     to executing any live-fire event.

     4-70. During the AAR and with input from the external evaluators or OC/Ts, attached leaders, the facility
     instructors or operators, and unit leadership identify the unit or subordinate unit that needs to retrain specific
     tasks. Once the unit leadership identify a unit’s retraining needs, they should execute a retraining event of
     the scenario. For minor tactical adjustments for the unit or subordinate units, the commander may opt to
     discuss different methods to complete the task, followed by backbriefs using terrain models or sand tables,
     rather than executing the entire training scenario. A retraining event of the scenario may consist of the
     following:

          • The entire platoon executing the training scenario again.
          • A select subordinate unit executing the training scenario.
          • Subordinate units rotating through different individual and collective tasks to support the

                mission-essential task.

66  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

                                                                                                       Table IV – Field Training Exercise

RETRAIN

     4-71. Unit leadership identify the unit or subordinate unit that requires retraining of specific tasks during the
     AAR with input from the external evaluators or OC/Ts, attached leaders, and the facility instructors or
     operators. Once the commander or the platoon leadership identify the retraining needs of the unit, they should
     execute a retraining event of the scenario. This may consist of—

          • The entire platoon executing the training scenario again.
          • Rotating platoons through different mission tasks in specific phases of the FTX to reinforce

                supporting collective tasks that need additional repetitions to achieve the desired proficiency.
          • Minor tactical adjustments for the unit, and the platoon leader may opt to discuss different methods

                to complete the task followed by backbriefs using terrain models or sand tables rather than executing
                the entire training scenario.

     4-72. Once the unit leadership is satisfied with the unit’s demonstrated performance, they should review the
     unit SOP and TTP for required updates, revisions, or modifications again. The platoon leader and platoon
     sergeant should assess which components of the unit’s SOP and TTP need additional attention during the
     upcoming FCX and LFX.

BEST PRACTICES

     4-73. Units should consider including the following best practices in Table IV, FTX, at the platoon level.
     Incorporation of these practices will assist the unit get its Soldiers, teams, and platoons to a higher proficiency
     rapidly. Figure 4-8, page 68 provides a quick reference to some of the best practices for the unit to consider
     when planning, preparing, and executing Table IV.
