                                                 Chapter 5

                Table V – Fire Coordination Exercise

    Chapter 5 describes the commander’s flexibility to rehearse critical collective tasks and battle
    drills during the FCX. The chapter discusses how the exercise integrates into the unit training
    plan following the structure of the IWTS. Units can use the descriptive guidelines in this
    chapter for the platoon FCX. This chapter details the purpose, method, and end state for the
    training event.
    The FCX provides an excellent environment for the simultaneous performance of
    multi-echelon training activities to evaluate and sustain the skills of Soldiers, leaders, teams,
    and units. Exercises simulate battle conditions to train leaders under mission-unique conditions
    and standards for training, evaluating, and applying the best TTP to the mission being trained.
    The FCX is recommended for commanders to provide sufficient dry-fire rehearsals and live
    fire reduced tempo practice on their critical collective tasks prior to Table VI, LFX.

EVENT OVERVIEW

     5-1. An FCX is a live-fire training event. The training event is designed to train commanders and key leaders
     in planning, integrating, and synchronizing direct and indirect fires, close air support (CAS), attack aviation,
     and other warfighting functions to support maneuver under live-fire conditions (see figure 5-1). Typically,
     an FCX is a reduced-force exercise at a reduced tempo. At a minimum, key leaders, key subordinate leaders,
     and fire support personnel represent the units. Units may use subcaliber devices, pyrotechnics, or battle
     effects simulators as substitutes for full-caliber munitions that represent the appropriate combat effects.

               Figure 5-1. Platoon Table V, Fire Coordination Exercise

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                               69


---

Chapter 5

     5-2. Units conduct the exercises in Platoon Table V, FCX, in a live environment under live-fire conditions.
     Units conduct the FCX with key leaders and enablers to build proficiency of the unit’s synchronization and
     coordination of fire and maneuver across multiple warfighting functions. The FCX verifies and validates the
     leader’s proficiency with critical collective and supporting collective tasks that the unit performs in a combat
     environment when executing its mission-essential tasks.

     5-3. During the FCX, leaders must build and demonstrate proficiency with individual and collective tasks
     and battle drills. These tasks support integration and synchronization of direct and indirect fires, CAS, attack
     aviation, and other warfighting function enablers while executing supporting collective tasks and
     mission-essential tasks on a reduced scale.

             Note. Table V is an optional training event for commanders to rehearse and execute complex,
             hazardous, or high risk direct or indirect fire tasks, battle tasks, or integrated warfighting functions
             prior to Table VI, LFX.

PURPOSE

     5-4. The FCX provides a focused training event for unit leaders to synchronize direct and indirect fires, CAS,
     attack aviation, and other integrated warfighting functions. Leaders train while operating in the offense or
     defense at a reduced tempo using full or subcaliber training munitions.

     5-5. The FCX requires a reduced force replicating critical subordinate elements. The FCX builds leader skills
     in the integration, timing, coordination, and execution of critical live-fire actions.

METHOD

     5-6. The FCX is commander-designed for a limited size force (typically, key leaders, subordinate leaders,
     and integrated enabler leadership). During the exercise, the force executes critical direct and indirect fires,
     ground to air coordination, and other critical tasks on a live-fire facility or established training area.

     5-7. Table V is the commander’s training event to validate the subordinate leader’s ability to synchronize
     fires and coordinate within the unit’s battlespace. Also, Table V is a training event to integrate key enablers
     into a reduced tempo live-fire event before conducting a full-scale, combined arms LFX.

END STATE

     5-8. Organizations train to a level of proficiency that allows them to accomplish their combat or deployed
     operation successfully. The unit and its subordinate assigned or attached elements must demonstrate (in a
     live environment under live-fire conditions) their ability to execute supporting, collective, critical, or directed
     tasks. Units must adequately demonstrate their proficiency on commander-selected collective tasks,
     procedures, drills, and battle tasks that synchronize direct fires, indirect fires, and other enablers.

     5-9. The FCX provides a means for commanders to—
          • Rehearse complex collective tasks in a dry-fire manner prior to live-fire execution.
          • Instruct the collective tasks to their subordinate units in a live environment under live-fire condition
                using realistic scenarios.
          • Validate the subordinate unit’s SOP to ensure synchronization and consistency with the unit SOP.
          • Reinforce previous training focused on critical skills and tasks with multiple training iterations.
          • Build confidence integrating warfighting functions and enablers during a live-fire event.
          • Ensure timing for direct and indirect fires synchronizes effectively to promote fire and maneuver of
                the organization.

70  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                  Table V – Fire Coordination Exercise

PLAN THE TRAINING

     5-10. Platoon Table V, FCX, is a live-fire event that trains commanders, staffs, and key leaders in planning
     and integrating direct fires, indirect fires, attack aviation, and CAS to support maneuver. At a minimum, units
     execute Table V as a reduced force exercise; key leaders, key subordinate leaders, and fire support personnel
     with their organic platforms and systems represent units. Units may substitute full-caliber munitions with
     subcaliber ammunition and devices based on resources available or restrictions beyond the evaluation
     commander’s control.

     5-11. The FCX requires integrating specific warfighting functions by echelon and stresses the
     synchronization of fire and maneuver at a reduced tempo. The FCX builds upon skills trained and tested
     during Tables I, II, and III and prepares the platoon for the LFX, trained at full tempo.

     5-12. Planning begins with identifying whom to train during the FCX. Next, the commander conducts a
     training assessment to determine who needs additional training. By referencing the appropriate supporting
     collective task, mission-essential task, and universal task list, the commander can identify and incorporate
     each task’s conditions and standards into the training plan.

     5-13. Once complete, the commander establishes the training objectives for the FCX. Unit commanders
     develop an OPORD for the mission in advance of the training event; this allows them to develop an event
     list and timeline. The commanders tie the event list and timeline to the specific direct and indirect fire, mission
     command, and warfighting functions integrated into the training event. This process allows commanders to
     focus their training resources on the tasks selected for training during the training assessment process.

     5-14. Platoon Table V, FCX, is a complex training event for the platoon prior to Platoon Table VI, LFX. This
     training event is for all Soldiers who are assigned, attached, or designated to the platoon as a member of a—

          • Subordinate unit.
          • Assigned specialty unit.
          • Cross-attached enabler small unit.

             Note. Commanders have the option to execute Table V using only key leadership from
             subordinates, attached enabler, and specialty units. These leaders will act as a representation of
             their full-scale, individual unit. Commanders can conduct the FCX at full scale with all personnel,
             equipment, and full-caliber munitions, if resources are available.

     5-15. During the planning process, commanders must be familiar with the following general information
     related to the company FCX:

          • Prerequisites—all subordinate units, attachments, and integrated warfighting function enablers must
                be qualified to perform their core tasks before providing those skills for the platoon.

          • Condemnation criteria—Table V is relevant only to the current training density and may not be used
                to meet any prerequisites beyond that training density.

          • Environment and conditions:
                 The FCX is conducted in a live environment under live-fire conditions.
                 The unit rehearses through sand tables, walk-throughs, or other methods based on the
                     commander’s intent and guidance.
                 Units must plan for sufficient resources and coordinate with external enablers to ensure
                     resources are scheduled and available.
                 Typically, this training event is executed with key leaders and attached enabler leadership.
                 Although the entire unit is present, typically only a portion of the unit fires training ammunition
                     for synchronization and effects.

          • Training days required:
                 Each platoon-size element is authorized to have a minimum of 1 training day to conduct the
                     FCX.
                 Additional days may be required for retraining, as necessary.

          • Frequency—when directed, units conduct the FCX annually as part of their platoon training plan.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  71


---

Chapter 5

          • Throughput—a company can expect to complete four platoon-size elements per day on the training
                facility as follows:
                 Day 1: unit preparation, walk-through, and dry-fire rehearsal.
                 Day 2: day and night fire coordination live-fire execution.

             Note. Primary and alternate facilities are needed. The FCX may be conducted on a digital range
             facility (Digital Multipurpose Range Complex, Digital Air-Ground Integration Range, battle area
             complex), on a large maneuver live-fire range, or on a safety-certified training area. Units may use
             multiple ranges and training areas simultaneously to support the size and structure of their
             formation synchronized by the unit’s mission command nodes, installation support, and other
             methods.

     5-16. Planning includes the identification and allocation of ammunition, petroleum, oils, lubricants,
     equipment, and support personnel. Coordination of facilities and resources is a continuous process through
     execution. Prerequisite individual and collective training continue throughout the planning phases.

     5-17. Detailed planning by the evaluation commander’s headquarters focuses on the training objectives and
     development of the scenario. The evaluation commander’s specified mission may be the same primary
     mission for each platoon, or it may vary between elements based on their collective task assessments,
     capabilities, combatant commander guidance, or other requirements. The commander’s initial guidance may
     specify the exercise tasks or develop as the commander designs the scenario.

     5-18. Each unit, with their specific weapon systems, involved in the upcoming LFX must be included in the
     planning process. The following is a discussion of the key considerations for the different types of units,
     enablers, and warfighting functions. The unprecedented ground mobility of stabilized weapon system
     platforms necessitates the design of deep ranges to maximize the use of their advanced capabilities. Targets
     on ranges are heated (thermalized) to facilitate acquisition by weapon system platforms and sensors.

             Note. See appendix B for detailed scenario development requirements for all live-fire training
             events. Appendix B applies to Table IV, FTX; Table V, FCX; and Table VI, LFX, as well as any
             unit developed live-fire training events or exercises that utilize a scenario to guide the training unit
             through specific tasks.

              INTEGRATING SQUAD/SECTION TABLE V WITH PLATOON
                                                  TABLE V

          Units should consider integrating the Squad/Section Table V with the Platoon Table V.
          This reduces resourcing constraints and enables the platoons to train all specified
          tasks seamlessly. Units must ensure external evaluators or OC/Ts and the range safety
          officer (RSO) structure support section and platoon observation, monitoring, and
          evaluation.

72  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                  Table V – Fire Coordination Exercise

SCENARIO REQUIREMENTS

     5-19. Units include available enablers and specialty units within the company and battalion into their
     scenarios. The training mission should support future company and battalion collective training events while
     reinforcing the unit SOP and previous collective training events.

     5-20. The live-fire scenario is based on the OPORD provided to the platoon leader for dissemination to the
     training platoons. The OPORD controls the phases of the live-fire events by clearly articulating the tactical
     mission tasks the platoon and its subordinate elements will execute. Those mission tasks translate to a tactical
     dilemma designed to the range complex. Targetry for each phase is derived from the tactical mission task in
     a realistic manner.

     5-21. From those target sets developed by the commander with support from the operations staff officer, a
     scenario is created. The hit standards for each phase are determined by the tactical mission task directed by
     the OPORD or planned FRAGORDs during the mission itself. Each tactical mission task equates to a
     percentage of target sets that must be defeated to achieve a GO for the phase.

     5-22. Overall, the platoon must achieve a “T” on all training and evaluation outlines associated to the tasks
     selected by the commander. The platoon must also receive 70-percent GO on all live-fire phases based on
     the kill standards of the tactical mission task. See appendix C for more in-depth information on collective
     live-fire evaluations.

     5-23. Unit leadership design their scenarios in multiple phases, or stages, where each portion of the scenario
     specifically trains one or two collective tasks in a progressive manner. Successful completion of each phase
     (based on the commander’s assessment using the SOP and training and evaluation outlines as a guide) moves
     the formation tactically to a continuation of the mission, branch, or sequel. Commanders select the level of
     stimulus, rigor, and dilemma complexity based on their assessment of the unit’s demonstrated performance.

     5-24. Each warfighting function includes optional or required tasks that commanders integrate into their
     training scenario. Unit leadership integrate those tasks as appropriate or when most beneficial and realistic.
     Units conduct the warfighting function integrated tasks during limited and unlimited visibility (night and
     day) conditions, across the entire FCX training scenario event, not during each maneuver collective task.

             Note. Call for fire munitions are resourced for each platoon, day and night. Commanders who
             wish to incorporate training munitions rather than notional mortar fire missions must coordinate
             for those munitions.

     5-25. Conducting FCX at the platoon level requires an approved range facility or authorized live-fire capable
     training area. Each day, the unit may opt to alter the primary direction of the mission to vary the battlespace
     of the training unit.

     5-26. The FCX scenario must include training and evaluation opportunities for all the unit’s tactical collective
     tasks, as well as any commander-selected tasks specific to the warfighting functions listed in figure 5-2,
     page 74. Unit leadership integrate these tasks as appropriate across the FCX scenario, during day and night
     phases of training.

WARFIGHTING FUNCTION INTEGRATION

     5-27. Commanders must integrate specific warfighting functions during Table IV, FTX. How the unit
     integrates specific tasks for the warfighting function requirements is up to the commander. Commanders
     should elect tasks that were previously trained during Table III, and build proficiency through repetition for
     success on Table VI, LFX.
     5-28. The commander must integrate tasks associated with the warfighting functions listed within figure 5-2.
     These requirements are designed to build upon previous training provided during Tables I through III.

                                                        requirements

                                                                                                  Table V – Fire Coordination Exercise

AMMUNITION REQUIREMENTS

     5-29. The ammunition requirements are listed by the weapon, system, or element that participates in the
     training event (see appendix A). The unit may adjust training ammunition requirements based on resources
     available within its training accounts but should not be less than those quantities listed.

     5-30. Not all weapons, systems, or platforms are resourced training ammunition for the squad/section and
     platoon-level FCX. Units should review their training ammunition accounts to determine the appropriate
     amount of munitions for their event. Most training strategies provide sufficient ammunition during the
     previous echelon’s training, such that harvested munitions (first-round hit savings) can be utilized for the
     FCX. Harvested ammunition are munitions that typically are returned after the previous training events,
     sometimes referred to as first-round hit savings.

                             TRAINING MUNITIONS RESOURCING

          For maneuver units, no training munitions are resourced specifically for platoon live
          fire. The munitions are provided as part of the platoon live-fire events in
          DA Pam 350-38 per weapon or system. The training munitions presented in
          appendix A are recommended quantities to achieve proficiency.

          Units must review their training ammunition accounts to identify any shortfalls based
          on the commander’s plan. Shortfalls must be identified and requested as necessary
          through their brigade ammunition officer.

     5-31. See appendix A to review the minimum training munitions requirements for each training event.
     Appendix A lists recommended munitions quantities and may not reflect the authorized munitions found in
     DA Pam 350-38 for the fiscal year. The recommended munitions quantities are provided to create the most
     realistic and effective training event. Units should see DA Pam 350-38 to identify the fiscal year’s
     authorizations for each event by weapon type and to review the commander’s intent and munitions expected
     for use during the training event. Units should augment the quantities listed with munitions they have within
     their Total Ammunition Management Information System account.

     5-32. Mortar training ammunition requirements provide a minimum of two call for fire missions during the
     day phase and two fire missions during the night phase (minimum one illumination mission). The day phase
     includes smoke, illumination, and HE rounds.

     5-33. Ammunition referred to in paragraph 5-23 supports one complete maneuver company through the
     platoon FCX. Units should focus on one day and night fire mission for each platoon. Units should focus
     additional munitions on training during the upcoming LFX, unless additional, indirect fire synchronization
     training is required. The brigade combat team (BCT) commander must approve additional training
     ammunition to support the maneuver Platoon’s Table V.

     5-34. Units must prepare to issue and turn in large quantities of small-arms ammunition. Typically, units
     provide small-arms ammunition in larger quantities to the training unit so the Infantry Soldiers can experience
     a combat-realistic weight burden. Units should only provide small-arms ammunition to modified table of
     organization and equipment (MTOE) combat arms firers who are part of the training maneuver unit. Units
     should not provide ammunition to noncombat arms Soldiers unless required by specific scenario events.

Chapter 5

RANGE REQUIREMENTS

     5-35. Maneuver installations have various training facilities to support the platoon FCX. No matter the
     installation, multiple facilities, training areas, and dedicated and temporary impact areas are required to
     conduct the FCX to standard.

     5-36. The Army’s family of maneuver ranges provide a vast array of capabilities that offer a combat-realistic
     scenario. Each installation and its facilities have limitations and restrictions. Units must fully understand the
     capabilities, limitations, constraints, and restrictions that affect their training event’s development and
     execution.

     5-37. See TC 25-8 for detailed information on live-fire range capabilities, limitations, layout, and general
     targetry information. The common facilities capable of supporting platoon live fire are listed in figure 5-3.
     See DA Pam 385-63 for surface danger zones, personnel requirements, and other live-fire information. Units
     must review the appropriate range regulations and Department of the Army pamphlets, as well as their local
     range regulations, policies, and procedures during their planning process to ensure their training scenarios
     are viable and safe.

Chapter 5

LIVE-FIRE BATTLESPACE

     5-38. Units use a live-fire complex and one or more training areas to support platoon training with platoon
     movement and maneuver space, separate assembly area locations for both the training unit, off-facility
     chemical decontamination site, and supporting mortar firing points (as munitions are available).

Training Area

     5-39. The training area must be fully capable of providing training and evaluation of several collective tasks
     for assembly area operations, local security, troop leading procedures, maintenance, and other non-live fire
     tasks. The assembly area should provide tactically relevant distances for a company/troop element training
     their platoons.

Boresighting and Alignment

     5-40. Areas should be provided for boresighting, alignment, harmonization, or other weapons’ alignment
     procedures based on the platoon’s armament, sensors, and optics. Sufficient space should be available to
     conduct the preparation to fire tasks in a tactical manner, day and night.

Chemical Decontamination

     5-41. At the completion of the day phase and night phase, once cleared by the RSO, the training element
     should be ordered to a chemical decontamination site. This site should be off the range facility and clear of
     the range’s composite surface danger zone. This enables a continuation of training for the next platoon and
     its subordinate elements.

Live-Fire Concept Sketch

     5-42. During planning, the unit develops a concept sketch of the tactical set using common graphics. This
     builds the concept of training, provides the draft graphics and associated control measures, and defines the
     battlespace for training. As shown in figure 5-4 there are four phases for the mission concept, where a phase
     of the operation or mission is planned between each PL.

Chapter 5

DRIVERS, BRANCHES, AND SEQUELS

     5-43. Units should plan to include a series of drivers, branches, and sequels to meet their training objectives
     to achieve proficiency in their platoons. These are training terms used for maneuver collective training that
     build the story boards for the training events. They provide pre-build alterations to the tactical missions based
     on the training unit’s performance, capability, or reactions to tactical dilemmas.
     5-44. They assist in steering the training unit toward the expected conditions of the task, establish the
     appropriate tempo, build the battlefield effects, and develop the tactical situation. Figure 5-5 provides various
     mission stimuli for EXCON of the training event.

         Figure 5-5. Mission stimulus as drivers, branches, and sequels matrix, example

    5-45. Figure 5-6 provides a generalized driver, branch, and sequel synchronization matrix to steer the training
    in the direction of the commander’s intent. This is an extremely rudimentary version. Units should prepare a
    template for each phase of the operation to provide the maximum flexibility to the evaluation commander to
    shape the tactical situation.

Chapter 5

PREPARE

     5-46. The platoon’s training strategy events completed prior to the FCX provide preparation for Table V.
     Units should maximize the platoon training plan, as well as the Table V rehearsal period (typically day 1) to
     prepare for the live-fire portion of Table V, FCX. Unit leadership should ensure the platoon’s training plans
     support the critical, supporting collective, critical leader, and individual tasks that support platoon FCX and
     LFX. Companies should ensure the platoon’s training plans are integrated into their unit training plan.

     5-47. Units preparing to execute platoon training events use their SOP throughout the training density.
     Commanders use the SOP to validate the platoon’s SOP and to confirm the higher headquarters’ SOP is
     supported. The training unit’s subordinate elements should be aware of the purpose, method, and end state
     of Table V and how its training plans support the unit’s collective tasks and the company’s mission-essential
     tasks.

RESOURCE THE TRAINING

     5-48. The FCX requires the unit to coordinate and secure the following resources (see appendix A and
     appendix B for detailed information):

          • Full and subcaliber training munitions.
          • Demolitions.
          • Pyrotechnics.
          • Battle effects simulators.
          • Live-fire facility or safety-certified training area based on unit composition (see TC 25-1 and

                TC 25-8 for more information).
          • Training areas for unit assembly area activities before and after FCX execution.
          • Maneuver areas that support the tactical scenario for the FCX before and after live fire.

     5-49. Units must plan and secure resources early to ensure availability and accessibility during the training
     event. Ammunition resources must be scheduled a minimum of 90 days in advance of training to allow
     sufficient time to ship to the servicing ammunition supply point.

OBSERVER-COACHES/TRAINERS

     5-50. Training and certifying external evaluators or OC/Ts are critical during the preparation phase. External
     evaluators or OC/Ts must be tactically and technically proficient in the task they are evaluating. The external
     evaluators or OC/Ts must conduct an effective AAR that facilitates self-discovery of key issues for the unit,
     establish cause and effect, solve, or lead to solving the problems identified, and foster an environment for
     continuous improvement.

     5-51. External evaluators or OC/Ts have an important role in ensuring live-fire training is conducted safely
     and are a key mitigation measure in the risk assessment process. As such, external evaluators or OC/Ts must
     be thoroughly familiar with the weapons that will be employed and must understand their effects. They must
     be familiar with the range to include firing limits, firing points, maneuver boxes, target locations, and indirect
     fire targets. To ensure safe live-fire maneuver, external evaluators or OC/Ts must know all fire control
     measures used in the FCX, including PLs, target reference point (TRP) locations, azimuths for firing, triggers,
     and signals that drive shifting or ceasing fires, and any other controls the commander chooses to employ.

     5-52. Additionally, the external evaluators or OC/Ts should reference and visualize applicable doctrine,
     training, and evaluation outlines, and TTP during the conduct of the AAR to facilitate understanding. They
     must be completely familiar with the conditions and standards of the tasks trained and must know the
     evaluated units’ TACSOPs.

EXERCISE CONTROL

     5-53. The unit plans and prepares to execute control of the training event. Planning identifies the EXCON
     structure for the event. The number of personnel varies from unit to unit and event to event. Units should
     coordinate for external support early to prepare those tasked Soldiers for their roles and responsibilities during
     the event.
     5-54. Figure 5-7 shows a common, although complex EXCON structure that uses organic and external
     support. This structure can easily adapt to support platoon training events such as Platoon Table III, STX and
     Platoon Table IV, FTX.

Chapter 5

TRAIN UNIT LEADERS ON RELATED TACTICS, TECHNIQUES, AND PROCEDURES

     5-55. Training unit leaders on related TTP is key to executing the FCX successfully. Unit commanders ensure
     their subordinate leaders train on the tasks, conditions, and standards of the FCX. Training should include a
     review of the appropriate training and evaluation outlines and related TTP for the mission and tasks.

     5-56. A series of officer, professional development sessions, or other informal training seminars is one
     method for training subordinate leaders. These should include junior leader seminars, officer professional
     development, and noncommissioned officer (NCO) professional development classes to best educate the
     junior leaders prior to the event.

RECON THE SITE

     5-57. Table V, FCX uses authorized range facilities and training areas. Commanders schedule the training
     location as far in advance as practical following the installation’s policies and procedures. Once the method
     of training and evaluation is established, unit leadership should secure the training location and appropriate
     TADSS, equipment, or systems that enhance the training event.

     5-58. Once scheduled, the commander and platoon leaders conduct a reconnaissance of the training locations
     to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the training unit including branches and sequels.
          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario can be achieved with appropriate

                retraining opportunities.
          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group discussions, briefings, and

                AARs.
          • Provide a sand table or terrain board.
          • Provide terrain board kits.
          • Confirm appropriate warfighting function integration capabilities into the concept of the training

                event.
          • Verify the training area can incorporate direct fire support, special purpose weapons, and sniper

                teams into the scenario, as the commander directs.
          • Identify authorized dig locations and required permits within the training area.
          • Identify restricted terrain, environmental considerations, and any NO-GO areas within the training

                sites (movement and maneuver).
          • Identify the level of fires that can be included into the training scenario:

                 The FSO or FIST provides fires synchronization and realism.
                 Units should include a fire marker plan to enhance the battle effects of the mission, where

                     appropriate (fire support).
          • Provide the scenario a threat template that is appropriate to the unit’s mission and build a more

                robust and effective scenario (higher headquarters’ S-2).
          • Verify the training area can support the unit’s training while in a chemical environment, based on

                the scenario design (protection).
          • Identify any hazards within the training complex.
          • Identify any sustainment limitations.
          • Verify CASEVAC notional or live capabilities in support of the training scenario (sustainment).
          • Identify command post capabilities and locations in the training areas.
          • Identify any external TOC configuration that can be incorporated for higher headquarters’ staff

                integration, as appropriate (mission command).

     5-59. When conducting a reconnaissance of the training location, units should include reviewing the training
     area to accommodate integration of the required and optional warfighting functions. Figure 5-8 provides
     some considerations for each training requirement.

Chapter 5

ISSUE THE ORDER

     5-60. Commanders can issue an OPORD, a WARNORD, or use the training schedule, as necessary. The
     training unit must ensure all supporting elements or enablers have sufficient time to coordinate and prepare
     before the training event.

     5-61. WARNORDs to external organizations must include sufficient reaction time for the appropriate
     personnel and equipment. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-throughs, rehearsals, and AARs.

     5-62. The OPORD or WARNORD should include the list of tasks evaluated during the event and the method
     of evaluation. Unit leadership should provide sufficient time for subordinate leaders to train the tasks and
     skills listed in the order. Unit leadership should ensure the appropriate training resources are available and
     accessible. Upon receipt, subordinate leaders who have SOPs for their organizations must ensure their
     procedures support and complement the higher headquarters’ publication.

     5-63. The order should include the specific mission orders the training units will receive as part of the training
     event. Those orders, FRAGORDs, and WARNORDs define the tasks required for the training unit to perform
     and outline the scheme of maneuver for the OPFORs conduct during training.

86  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                  Table V – Fire Coordination Exercise

REHEARSE

     5-64. Rehearsals help leaders and subordinates understand the conduct of events and their responsibilities.
     Rehearsals help the organization synchronize training with times, places, logistics, and training support.
     Rehearsing a concept drill helps leaders visualize an event as it unfolds as well as likely branches and sequels
     if leaders must adjust the training. Leaders conduct rehearsals using the various levels and techniques
     described in ADP 7-0.

     5-65. Units use sand tables or terrain models to reinforce subordinate leader understanding of the conduct of
     the training event, as well as rehearse the critical collective tasks trained during the FCX. The rehearsals
     should include digital and analog stimulus and drivers that initiate action by the training unit, including the
     various branches and sequels developed.

     5-66. The operations officer uses rehearsals to verify the conduct of the range events, movement, and control
     of reconnaissance elements and to synchronize maneuver unit actions. In addition, rehearsals help the
     operations officer to proof the scenario stimulus events or actions that drive the training unit to the appropriate
     tasks, drills, or actions.

     5-67. For maneuver units expecting to cross-attach to an external unit, the rehearsal is an opportunity for the
     platoon leadership to synchronize the unit SOP with the TACSOP of their gaining unit or attached personnel
     or organizations.

     5-68. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish and confirm—
          • Training scenarios are understood by the training unit.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide standoff distances for the unit to build the appropriate tempo
                of each mission or phase of the operation.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and are established at the higher
                headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional external evaluators or OC/Ts training support packages are complete and provide for
                multiple iterations.
          • Appropriate doctrinal publications are available for reference.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • AAR capabilities are reviewed.
          • Available warfighting function enablers are integrated with the unit or appropriate subordinate unit.
          • The process for conducting direct fire support tasks.
          • The scheme of fires.
          • Methods and actions required to provide fires.
          • The process used to incorporate intelligence activities into the scenario.
          • The location of retraining for subordinate units, as necessary.
          • The CASEVAC and MEDEVAC plans.
          • The external evaluators or OC/Ts communications plan.
          • The flow of reporting.
          • The transition plan between units being trained.
          • Communication plan and reports for training completion to higher headquarters.

     5-69. The commander makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs once the unit
     completes the walk-through and training rehearsal.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  87


---

Chapter 5

EXECUTE

     5-70. The FCX is a rehearsal of all critical tasks, battle tasks, and drills prior to Table VI, LFX. It should be
     conducted in a dry-fire manner prior to live-fire execution.

     5-71. Units complete Table V prior to Table VI, LFX. Depending on the higher headquarters’ training plan,
     units can execute Table V before or after Table IV, FTX. Units may execute Table V in a manner that fits
     the commander’s plan and range facility, training area availability, and accessibility.

     5-72. Table V consists of one training day. The FCX has a day and a night phase with multiple progressive
     actions determined by the commander. An example flow for the platoon FCX is—

          • Day 1, day phase:
                 Assembly area occupation.
                 Assembly area activities.
                 Tactical movement to the range facility or authorized training area; walk-through or rehearsal.
                 Establishment of hasty defensive positions.
                 Scenario rehearsal.
                 Fire coordination, distribution, and synchronization rehearsal.
                 Reconsolidation and reorganization rehearsal.
                 Tactical movement to an assembly area or battle position not on the training facility.
                 Day hotwash.
                 Repeat with select live-fire munitions and tasks.
                 Day AAR.

          • Day 1, night phase:
          • Preparation for night operations.

                 Assembly area activities.
                 Tactical movement to the range facility or authorized training area.
                 Establishment of hasty defensive positions.
                 Scenario rehearsal.
                 Fire coordination, distribution, and synchronization rehearsal.
                 Reconsolidation and reorganization rehearsal.
                 Tactical movement to an assembly area or battle position not on the training facility.
                 Night hotwash.
                 Repeat sequence under live-fire conditions.
                 Night AAR.

88  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                  Table V – Fire Coordination Exercise

EVALUATE THE TRAINING

     5-73. At the completion of each phase of Table V, key leaders and their senior leaders discuss any
     shortcomings, gaps, or disconnects between SOPs by echelon, actions that require additional training or
     synchronization efforts, missing tasks or actions that should be included, and determine any corrective actions
     necessary. Commanders use the AAR process to educate junior leaders and subordinates to reinforce the
     training objectives, identify shortfalls and corrective measures, as well as identify strengths within their
     formation.

PREPARE AND CONDUCT THE AFTER ACTION REVIEW

     5-74. The evaluation process for an FCX is continuous. The AAR is informal and focuses on the tasks,
     conditions, and standards selected for training. The training evaluations and subsequent AARs are integral to
     the training management process.

             Note. FM 7-0 provides a detailed description of the AAR process.

     5-75. Commanders review the notes from the various external evaluators or OC/Ts, subordinate leaders,
     FSOs, and others to determine the proficiency of participating key leaders and their ability to execute the
     critical synchronization and maneuver tasks. Once complete with the review, the commander assesses the
     organization’s ability to complete the tasks at the reduced tempo, identifies key areas to retrain at the platoon
     level, and develops a retraining plan, as appropriate.

     5-76. At the completion of the training event, key leaders and their senior leaders discuss any shortcomings,
     gaps, or disconnects between SOPs by echelon, and determine any corrective actions. With input from the
     external evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership, the commander provides retraining opportunities as
     necessary, including additional scenarios.

             Note. At the completion of the platoon training strategy, the key leaders and their commander
             assess their performance during all six training events (Tables I through VI), review their SOPs,
             and validate their accuracy and effectiveness for continued use.

     5-77. External evaluators or OC/Ts should provide clear and articulate comments and insight on the positive
     and negative actions of the subordinate units they observed. External evaluators or OC/Ts use the training
     and evaluation outlines to guide them through the evaluation process and use a variety of doctrinal
     publications to support their comments, discussions, and critiques of the unit observed.

     5-78. External evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership should review the training and evaluation outlines,
     training notes, and observations, to determine which tasks require additional attention through the remainder
     of the training density. They also can identify where additional complexities and tactical challenges can be
     induced during training where mastery has been demonstrated.

     5-79. The commander should share the overall performance of the unit, including observations, with the
     entire training unit. This allows subordinate units to learn from other units’ best practices, procedures, and
     tactics, and, at times, from their shortcomings or mistakes. The commander should identify leaders within
     the organization who have demonstrated superior performance and tactical competence by assigning them as
     external evaluators or OC/Ts during future training events, internal and external to the organization.

     5-80. During the AAR and with input from the external evaluators or OC/Ts, attached leaders, the facility
     instructors or operators, and unit leadership identify the unit or subordinate unit that needs to retrain specific
     tasks. Once the unit leadership identifies a unit’s retraining needs, they should execute a retraining event of
     the scenario. For minor tactical adjustments for the unit or subordinate units, the commander may opt to
     discuss different methods to complete the task, followed by backbriefs using terrain models or sand tables,
     rather than executing the entire training scenario.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  89


---

Chapter 5

RETRAIN

     5-81. Corrective training allows participants to translate observations and evaluation into corrective action.
     Additional training allows participants to apply the lessons learned during the execution and AAR. Leaders
     understand that during the training event, participants may not perform all the evaluated tasks to the desired
     standard. Therefore, during the short-range and near-term planning process, commanders provide flexibility
     within the training events that facilitate additional training immediately following the AAR.

     5-82. The FCX focuses on the basic level of training—the coordination of direct and indirect fires with
     maneuver and mission command. Once a unit has successfully demonstrated performance on the selected
     tasks, the unit prepares to move on to more complex, rigorous, and demanding collective training events.

     5-83. Unit leadership identify the unit or subordinate unit that requires retraining of specific tasks during the
     AAR with input from the external evaluators or OC/Ts, attached leaders, and the facility operators. Once the
     commander or the platoon leadership identify the retraining needs of the unit, they should execute a retraining
     event of the scenario. This may consist of—

          • The entire platoon executing the training scenario again.
          • Rotating platoons through different mission tasks in specific phases of the FCX to reinforce

                supporting collective tasks that need additional repetitions to achieve the desired proficiency.
          • Minor tactical adjustments for the unit, and the platoon leader may opt to discuss different methods

                to complete the task followed by backbriefs using terrain models or sand tables rather than executing
                the entire training scenario.

     5-84. Once the unit leadership are satisfied with the unit’s demonstrated performance, they should review the
     unit SOP and TTP for required updates, revisions, or modifications again. The platoon leader and platoon
     sergeant should assess which components of the unit’s SOP and TTP need additional attention during the
     upcoming FCX and LFX.

BEST PRACTICES

     5-85. Units should consider including the following best practices in Table V, FCX at the platoon level.
     Incorporation of these practices will assist the unit get its Soldiers, teams, and platoons to a higher proficiency
     rapidly. Figure 5-9 provides a quick reference to some of the best practices for the unit to consider when
     planning, preparing, and executing Table V.

                                                 Chapter 6

                      Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

    This chapter describes the requirements of Platoon Table VI, LFX and how it integrates into
    the unit training plan following the structure of the IWTS. Units use the descriptive guidelines
    in this chapter to conduct Platoon Table VI, LFX. This chapter provides the purpose, method,
    and end state for the training event. LFXs provide an excellent environment for the
    simultaneous performance of multi-echelon training activities to evaluate and sustain the skills
    of Soldiers, leaders, teams, staffs, and units.

EVENT OVERVIEW

     6-1. An LFX is an externally evaluated maneuver live-fire event that measures a unit’s proficiency in
     executing a series of collective tasks based on its higher headquarters’ mission-essential task. An LFX
     evaluates the key and subordinate leaders’ abilities to integrate organic weapons systems, subordinate units,
     and multiple warfighting functions at full tempo using full-caliber ammunition. Attached units employ their
     associated weapons systems. Figure 6-1 illustrates where the platoon LFX falls within the overall platoon
     training strategy.

                             Figure 6-1. Platoon Table VI, Live-Fire Exercise

6-2. LFXs simulate the operational environment conditions to train leaders under mission-unique conditions
and standards for training, evaluating, and applying the best TTP to the mission being trained. This exercise
may use minimal troop support. Units can execute the event at home station to provide commanders, leaders,
and staffs with realistic practice in executing wartime missions. Goals associated with live-fire training
exercises, including the LFX, are—

     • Sustain Soldier, leader, and collective skills.
     • Develop and sustain mission command skills.
     • Increase combat realism with complex and challenging conditions and effects.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  93


---

Chapter 6

     6-3. Units conduct Platoon Table VI, LFX, in a live environment under live-fire conditions. Units conduct
     the LFX with all subordinate elements and enablers to build proficiency of the unit’s synchronization and
     coordination of fire and maneuver across multiple warfighting functions. The LFX verifies and validates the
     leader’s proficiency on critical, collective, and mission-essential tasks that the unit performs in a combat
     environment.

     6-4. During Table VI, leaders must demonstrate proficiency with individual and collective tasks. These tasks
     support integration and synchronization of direct and indirect fires, CAS, attack aviation, and other
     warfighting function enablers. Table VI is the maneuver platoon’s live-fire proficiency event required to
     progress to company and above live-fire training events.

PURPOSE

     6-5. LFXs provide a focused training event for the unit’s leaders to synchronize direct and indirect fires,
     CAS, attack aviation, and other integrated warfighting functions, while operating in the offense or defense,
     at a full, combat-realistic tempo using full-caliber training munitions. The LFX is conducted with a day and
     night phase to ensure collective task proficiency in varying visibility conditions.

     6-6. The LFX is the unit’s live-fire proficiency EXEVAL. It must be successfully completed, day and night,
     before advancing to a company or higher combined arms live-fire exercise (CALFEX).

METHOD

     6-7. Table VI is designed for a maneuver platoon-size unit with all associated or attached elements. The unit
     executes direct and indirect fires, ground to air coordination, and other critical tasks on a live-fire facility or
     established training area. The unit conducts a training mission replicating a minimum of one collective task,
     day and night.

     6-8. The LFX is externally evaluated by trained and certified external evaluators or OC/Ts team using the
     approved training and evaluation outlines. The evaluation commander determines the overall unit proficiency
     of the live-fire collective task using the training and evaluation outlines provided by the external evaluators
     or OC/Ts team.

END STATE

     6-9. Units train to a level of proficiency that allows them to accomplish their combat mission or deployment
     operation successfully. The training unit (with all assigned or attached subordinate elements) must
     demonstrate (in a live environment, under live-fire conditions) its ability to execute mission-essential, critical,
     or directed tasks. For commanders, Table VI, LFX (EXEVAL) provides a means to—

          • Evaluate the collective tasks to their subordinate units in a live environment under live-fire
                conditions using realistic scenarios.

          • Validate the subordinate unit’s SOP to ensure synchronization and consistency with the unit SOP.
          • Reinforce previous training focused on critical skills and tasks with multiple training iterations.
          • Build confidence integrating warfighting functions and enablers.
          • Ensure timing for direct and indirect fires is synchronized to promote fire and maneuver of the

                organization.

94  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

PLAN THE TRAINING

     6-10. Platoon Table VI, LFX is an externally evaluated maneuver live-fire event that measures a unit’s
     proficiency in executing a series of collective tasks based on its higher headquarters’ mission-essential task.
     Table VI evaluates the key and subordinate leader’s abilities to integrate organic weapons systems,
     subordinate units, and multiple warfighting functions in combat-realistic conditions, both day and night.
     Table VI uses full-caliber training ammunition on an authorized live-fire facility or safety-certified training
     area.

     6-11. Table VI is the platoon’s externally evaluated live-fire event. The event provides commanders with a
     common standard to create an unbiased assessment of the element’s overall proficiency. Successful
     completion of the LFX is required to progress to any higher echelon live-fire event.

CONCEPT

     6-12. An LFX is a costly, resource-intensive exercise in which combined arms teams maneuver and employ
     organic and supporting weapon systems, enablers, and warfighting functions. An LFX is the most realistic
     measure of combined arms combat readiness and is an integral part of every maneuver unit’s training
     program.

     6-13. Commanders must be aware of LFX limitations, particularly regarding flank maneuvering while firing
     on live-fire ranges. Available terrain on installation range facilities rarely supports this type of maneuver.
     Using nonfiring maneuver areas, in conjunction with live-firing ranges, assists in achieving realism and adds
     tactical training not possible on live-fire ranges alone.

     6-14. Commanders should use LFXs to train certain aspects of combat readiness, such as distribution,
     coordination, and synchronization of fires. Commands should try to link multiple, digital, multipurpose range
     facilities (Digital Multipurpose Range Complex; Digital Multipurpose Training Range; Digital Air-Ground
     Integration Range). Commanders should synchronize, coordinate, and control coordination of all warfighting
     function enablers available whether at the same ranges, multiple facilities, or at a variety of live-fire ranges
     for maximum training value whenever possible.

     6-15. For an exercise of this nature to be effective, it must involve detailed planning and careful resourcing.
     Commanders should integrate supplementary missions and a wide range of supporting tasks into the exercise.
     Resources—including ammunition, personnel, equipment, maneuver areas, and firing ranges—must be
     programmed and coordinated early in the planning process to ensure that units can conduct the exercise day
     and night.

     6-16. This information is not intended to supersede the live-fire program at the collective training centers but
     to complement it. Certain planning aspects described herein address common constraints at Army
     installations worldwide. These same constraints may be relaxed at the collective training center to enhance
     realism and because of the nature of the terrain.

             INTEGRATING SQUAD/SECTION TABLE VI WITH PLATOON
                                                 TABLE VI

          Units should consider integrating the Squad/Section Table VI with the Platoon
          Table VI. This reduces resourcing constraints and enables the squads/sections and
          platoons to train all specified tasks seamlessly. Units must ensure external evaluators
          or OC/Ts and RSO structures support platoon observation, monitoring, and evaluation.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  95


---

Chapter 6

TRAINING METHODOLOGY

     6-17. Command elements (battalion and brigade) participate in all aspects of planning, executing, and
     evaluating LFX participants. They receive reports from the maneuvering platoon and company team, issue
     FRAGORDs, portray the friendly and enemy situation throughout the battlefield, and coordinate the
     maneuver and support of sustainment unit elements not under the company’s span of control. If the exercise
     actively integrates sustainment tasks, the command elements provide mission control and direct and support
     resupply and reconstitution.

     6-18. Company commanders are primary trainers during the platoon LFX. Their participation reinforces the
     chain of command and provides first-hand observation of the subordinate elements, platoon leadership, and
     supporting assets. An LFX gives units the opportunity to accomplish multi-echelon training and evaluation
     throughout the company.

     6-19. An LFX’s training objective is to accomplish a designated combat mission (attack, area defense, attack
     by fire, support by fire, and so forth) with full-caliber ammunition, a realistic target array, and required
     support and sustainment unit assets. The CATS contains applicable standards for the selected mission as
     modified by local conditions and the commander’s guidance. (See appendix B for more information on LFX
     scenario development.)

     6-20. Planning begins with identifying whom to train during the LFX. Next, the commander conducts a
     training assessment to determine which tasks and subtasks need additional training. The unit references the
     appropriate collective task based on the higher headquarters’ mission-essential task and universal task list to
     identify and incorporate each task’s conditions and standards into the training plan and to integrate them into
     the event’s scenario.

     6-21. Once complete, the commander establishes LFX training objectives. The unit commander and staff
     develop an OPORD for the mission in advance of the training event. The unit uses an OPORD to develop an
     event list and timeline that is tied to the specific direct and indirect fire, mission command, and warfighting
     functions integrated into the training event. This process allows commanders to focus their training resources
     on the tasks selected for training during the training assessment process.

             Note. Platoon Table VI, LFX is developed and coordinated at the battalion level. This chapter uses
             the plan, prepare, execute, and assess operations process as an outline for units developing their
             Table VI event.

     6-22. During the planning process, commanders must be familiar with the following information:
          • Prerequisites—all subordinate units, attachments, and integrated warfighting function enablers must
                be qualified to perform their core tasks prior to providing those skills for the platoon.
          • Environment and conditions:
                 The LFX is conducted in a live environment under live-fire conditions.
                 The unit is required to rehearse using sand tables, walk-throughs, or other methods based on
                     the commander’s intent and guidance prior to live-fire execution.
                 Units must plan for sufficient resources for their unit and coordinate with external enablers to
                     ensure sufficient resources are forecasted and available.
          • Training days required—platoon-size elements are authorized 2 training days to conduct their LFX.
                Additional days may be required for retraining, as necessary.
          • Frequency—LFX is conducted once annually as part of their platoon training plan for maneuver
                units unless specifically directed otherwise.
          • Throughput—a company can expect to complete four platoon-size elements per day on the training
                facility:
                 Day 1: unit preparation and rehearsal.
                 Day 2: day and night mission execution.
          • Primary and alternate facilities:
                 The LFX may be conducted on a digital range facility (Digital Multipurpose Range Complex,
                     Digital Air-Ground Integration Range, battle area complex), on a large maneuver live-fire
                     range, or on a safety-certified training area.
                 Units may conduct an LFX using a combination of facilities and training areas as authorized by
                     the installation.

96         TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

SCENARIO REQUIREMENTS

     6-23. Units include available enablers and specialty units within the company and battalion into their
     scenarios. The training mission should support future company and battalion collective training events while
     reinforcing the unit SOP and previous collective training events.

     6-24. The live-fire scenario is based on the OPORD provided to the platoon leader for dissemination to the
     training platoons. The OPORD controls the phases of the live-fire events by clearly articulating the tactical
     mission tasks the platoon and its subordinate elements will execute. Those mission tasks translate to a tactical
     dilemma designed to the range complex. Targetry for each phase is derived from the tactical mission task in
     a realistic manner.

     6-25. From those target sets developed by the commander with support from the operations staff officer, a
     scenario is created. The hit standards for each phase are determined by the tactical mission task directed by
     the OPORD or planned FRAGORDs during the mission itself. Each tactical mission task equates to a
     percentage of target sets that must be defeated to achieve a GO for the phase.

     6-26. Overall, the platoon must achieve a “T” on all training and evaluation outlines associated to the tasks
     selected by the commander. The platoon must also receive 70-percent GO on all live-fire phases based on
     the kill standards of the tactical mission task. See appendix C for more information on collective live-fire
     evaluations.

     6-27. Unit leadership design their scenarios in multiple phases, or stages, where each portion of the scenario
     specifically trains one or two collective tasks in a progressive manner. Successful completion of each phase
     (based on the commander’s assessment using the SOP and training and evaluation outlines as a guide) moves
     the formation tactically to a continuation of the mission, branch, or sequel. Commanders select the level of
     stimulus, rigor, and dilemma complexity based on their assessment of the unit’s demonstrated performance.

     6-28. Planning includes identifying and allocating ammunition; petroleum, oils, and lubricants; equipment,
     and support personnel. Coordinating facilities and resources is a continuous process through execution.
     Prerequisite individual and collective training continue throughout the planning phases.

     6-29. Each warfighting function includes optional or required tasks that commanders integrate into their
     training scenario. Unit leadership integrate those tasks as appropriate or when most beneficial and realistic.
     Units conduct the warfighting function integrated tasks during limited and unlimited visibility (night and
     day) conditions, across the entire LFX training scenario event, not during each maneuver collective task.

             Note. Call for fire munitions are resourced for each platoon, day and night. Commanders who
             wish to incorporate training munitions rather than notional mortar fire missions must coordinate
             for those munitions.

     6-30. Conducting LFX at the platoon level requires an approved range facility or authorized live fire capable
     training area. Each day, the unit may opt to alter the primary direction of the mission to vary the battlespace
     of the training unit.

     6-31. Detailed planning by the evaluation commander’s headquarters focuses on the training objectives and
     development of the scenario. The mission specified by the evaluation commander may be the same primary
     mission for each platoon, or it may vary between platoons or platoons based on their collective task
     assessments. The exercise tasks may be specified in the commander’s initial guidance or developed as the
     scenario is designed.

     6-32. See appendix B for detailed scenario development requirements for all live-fire training events.
     Appendix B applies to Table VI, LFX as well as any unit-developed live-fire training events or exercises that
     utilize a scenario to guide the training unit through specific tasks.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  97


---

Chapter 6

WARFIGHTING FUNCTION INTEGRATION

     6-33. Units are required to integrate the warfighting functions during the day and night phase at least once
     for evaluation purposes (see figure 6-2). They are not required to include them on every training day. This
     allows the unit to tailor the training and evaluation across the full training days available and progressively
     increase the rigor and complexity of the tactical situations presented based on its observations.

    Figure 6-2. Integrated warfighting function requirements

98  TC 3-20.20                                                25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

AMMUNITION REQUIREMENTS

     6-34. Appendix A lists the minimum ammunition requirements by the weapon, system, or element that
     participates in the training event. The unit may adjust the ammunition requirements based on resources
     available within its training accounts but should not be less than those quantities listed.

     6-35. Not all weapons, systems, or platforms are resourced adequate training ammunition for the
     squad/section and platoon-level LFX’s complexity. Units should review their training ammunition accounts
     and determine the appropriate amount of munitions for their event. Most training strategies provide sufficient
     ammunition during the previous training events so that the LFX can utilize the harvested munitions
     (first-round hit savings). Harvested ammunition are munitions that are turned-in typically after the previous
     training events, sometimes referred to as first-round hit savings.

                             TRAINING MUNITIONS RESOURCING

          For maneuver units, no training munitions are resourced specifically for platoon live
          fire. The munitions are provided as part of the platoon live-fire events in
          DA Pam 350-38 per weapon or system. The training munitions presented in
          appendix A are recommended quantities to achieve proficiency.

          Units must review their training ammunition accounts to identify any shortfalls based
          on the commander’s plan. Shortfalls must be identified and requested as necessary
          through their brigade ammunition officer.

     6-36. The mortar ammunition requirements provide a minimum of two call for fire missions during the day
     phase with smoke and HE and two fire missions during the night phase (minimum one illumination mission).
     The training and ammunition resource requirements listed in appendix A are sufficient to support all
     squads/sections and platoons within the company or troop. The recommended ammunition resources listed
     in appendix A support the platoon LFX. Units should focus on one fire mission day and night for each
     platoon.

             Note. Units must be prepared to issue and turn in large quantities of small-arms ammunition.
             Typically, units provide small-arms ammunition in larger quantities to the training unit to provide
             a combat-realistic weight burden to the dismounted Infantry Soldiers. Units should only provide
             small-arms ammunition to those MTOE combat arms firers who are part of the training maneuver
             unit. Units should not provide ammunition to noncombat arms Soldiers unless specific scenario
             events require it.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  99


---

Chapter 6

RANGE REQUIREMENTS

     6-37. Maneuver installations have various training facilities that can support the platoon LFX. The Army’s
     family of maneuver ranges provide combat-realistic scenarios. Each installations’ facilities have limitations
     and restrictions. Units must fully understand the capabilities, limitations, constraints, and restrictions that
     directly affect their training event’s development and execution.

     6-38. See TC 25-8 for detailed information on live-fire range capabilities, limitations, layout, and general
     targetry information. See DA Pam 385-63 for surface danger zones, personnel requirements, and other
     live-fire information. The common facilities capable of supporting platoon live fire are listed in figure 6-3.
     Units must review the appropriate range regulations and Department of the Army pamphlets, including their
     local range regulations, policies, and procedures during their planning process to ensure their training
     scenarios are viable and safe.

     Figure 6-3. Range facilities—platoon live fire capable

100  TC 3-20.20                                              25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

LIVE-FIRE BATTLESPACE

     6-39. Units use a live-fire complex and one or more training areas to support platoon training with platoon
     movement and maneuver space, separate assembly area locations for both the training unit, off-facility
     chemical decontamination site, and supporting mortar firing points (as munitions are available).

Training Area

     6-40. The training area must be fully capable of providing training and evaluation of several collective tasks
     for assembly area operations, local security, troop leading procedures, maintenance, and other non-live fire
     tasks. The assembly area should provide tactically relevant distances for a company/troop element training
     their platoons.

Boresighting and Alignment

     6-41. Areas should be provided for boresighting, alignment, harmonization, or other weapons alignment
     procedures based on the platoon’s armament, sensors, and optics. Sufficient space should be available to
     conduct the preparation to fire tasks in a tactical manner, day and night.

Chemical Decontamination

     6-42. At the completion of the day phase and night phase, once cleared by the RSO, the training element
     should be ordered to a chemical decontamination site. This site should be off the range facility and clear of
     the range’s composite surface danger zone. This enables a continuation of training for the next platoon and
     its subordinate elements.

Live-Fire Concept Sketch

     6-43. During planning, the unit develops a concept sketch of the tactical set using common graphics. This
     builds the concept of training, provides the draft graphics and associated control measures, and defines the
     battlespace for training. As shown in figure 6-4, page 102 there are four phases for the mission concept—
     where a phase of the operation/mission is planned between each PL.

           Figure 6-4. Live-fire battlespace, example

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

DRIVERS, BRANCHES, AND SEQUELS

     6-44. Units should plan to include a series of drivers, branches, and sequels to meet their training objectives
     to achieve proficiency in their platoons. These are training terms used for maneuver collective training that
     build the story boards for the training events. They provide pre-build alterations to the tactical missions based
     on the training unit’s performance, capability, or reactions to tactical dilemmas.
     6-45. They assist in steering the training unit toward the expected conditions of the task, establish the
     appropriate tempo, build the battlefield effects, and develop the tactical situation. Figure 6-5 provides various
     mission stimulus for EXCON of the training event.

     Figure 6-5. Mission stimulus as drivers, branches, and sequels matrix, example

6-46. Figure 6-6, page 104 provides a generalized driver, branch, and sequel synchronization matrix to steer
the training in the direction of the commander’s intent. This is an extremely rudimentary version. Units
should prepare a template for each phase of the operation to provide the maximum flexibility to the evaluation
commander to shape the tactical situation.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  103
