Chapter 6

           Figure 6-6. Driver, branch, and sequel synchronization matrix, example

104        TC 3-20.20                                                              25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

PREPARE

     6-47. Platoon Tables I through IV provide preparation for Table VI, LFX. Units should maximize the platoon
     and company training plans and the Table VI rehearsal period (typically day 1) to prepare for execution.
     Units should ensure subordinate unit training plans that support the critical collective tasks and the critical
     leader tasks that support the platoon LFX are integrated into their training plans, respectively.

     6-48. Units preparing to execute platoon training events use their SOP throughout the training density. Use
     of the unit SOP enables the commander to validate the subordinate unit’s SOP and to confirm the higher
     headquarters’ SOP is effectively supported. The subordinate elements of the training unit should be aware of
     the purpose, method, and end state of Table VI. The subordinate elements should know how their training
     plans support their collective tasks and the higher echelon unit’s mission-essential tasks.

RESOURCE THE TRAINING

     6-49. The LFX requires the unit to coordinate and secure the following resources at a minimum. Units should
     see appendix A, appendix B, and appendix C for the most comprehensive lists of resource requirements. The
     basic resources for the LFX are:

          • Full-caliber training munitions.
          • Demolitions.
          • Pyrotechnics.
          • Battle effects simulators.
          • Live-fire facility or safety-certified training area based on unit composition (see TC 25-1 and

                TC 25-8 for more information).
          • Training areas for unit assembly area activities prior to and after the LFX execution.
          • Maneuver areas that support the tactical scenario for the LFX before and after live fire.

OBSERVER-COACHES/TRAINERS

     6-50. Training and certifying external evaluators or OC/Ts are critical during the preparation phase. External
     evaluators or OC/Ts must be tactically and technically proficient in the tasks they will evaluate. They must
     be able to conduct an effective AAR that facilitates self-discovery of key issues for the unit, establish cause
     and effect, solve, or lead to solving the problems identified, and foster an environment for continuous
     improvement.

     6-51. External evaluators or OC/Ts have an important role in ensuring live-fire training is conducted safely
     and are a key mitigation measure in the risk assessment process. As such, external evaluators or OC/Ts must
     be thoroughly familiar with the weapons that will be employed and must understand their effects. They must
     be familiar with the range to include firing limits, firing points, maneuver boxes, target locations, and indirect
     fire targets. To ensure safe live-fire maneuver, external evaluators or OC/Ts must know all fire control
     measures used in the LFX including PLs, TRP locations, azimuths for firing, triggers and signals that drive
     shifting or ceasing fires, and any other controls the commander chooses to employ.

     6-52. Additionally, the external evaluators or OC/Ts should reference and visualize applicable doctrine and
     TTP during the conduct of the AAR to facilitate understanding. They must have a thorough understanding of
     the conditions and standards of the task that will be trained and must know the evaluated units’ TACSOP.

TRAIN UNIT LEADERS ON RELATED TACTICS, TECHNIQUES, AND PROCEDURES

     6-53. Training unit leaders on related TTP is key to executing the LFX successfully. Unit commanders ensure
     their subordinate leaders are trained on the tasks, conditions, and standards of the LFX. This should include
     a review of the appropriate training and evaluation outlines and related TTP for the mission and tasks.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  105


---

Chapter 6

EXERCISE CONTROL

     6-54. The unit plans and prepares to execute control of the training event. Planning identifies the EXCON
     structure for the event. The number of personnel varies from unit to unit and event to event. Units should
     coordinate for external support early to enable those tasked Soldiers to prepare for their roles and
     responsibilities during the event.
     6-55. Figure 6-7 shows a common, although complex EXCON structure that uses organic and external
     support. This structure can easily adapt to support platoon training events such as Platoon Table IV, FCX
     and Platoon Table VI, LFX.

     Figure 6-7. Exercise control structure, example

106  TC 3-20.20                                       25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

RECON THE SITE

     6-56. Table VI, LFX uses authorized range facilities and training areas. Commanders schedule the training
     location as far in advance as practical following the installation’s policies and procedures. Once the method
     of training and evaluation is established, unit leadership should secure the training location and appropriate
     TADSS, equipment, or systems that enhance the training event.

     6-57. Once scheduled, the commander and platoon leaders conduct a reconnaissance of the training locations
     to—

          • Verify sufficient space is available to support the training unit including branches and sequels.
          • Ensure multiple iterations or rotations through the scenario can be achieved with appropriate

                retraining opportunities.
          • Ensure sufficient administrative areas are available to support group discussions, briefings, and

                AARs.
          • Provide a sand table or terrain board.
          • Provide terrain board kits.
          • Confirm appropriate warfighting function integration capabilities into the concept of the training

                event.
          • Verify the training area can incorporate direct fire support, special purpose weapons, and sniper

                teams into the scenario, as the commander directs:
                 Identify authorized dig locations and required permits within the training area.
                 Identify restricted terrain, environmental considerations, and any NO-GO areas within the

                     training sites (movement and maneuver).
          • Identify the level of fire support that can be included into the training scenario:

                 The FSO or FIST provides fires synchronization and realism.
                 Units should include a fire marker plan to enhance the battle effects of the mission, where

                     appropriate (fire support).
          • Provide the scenario a threat template that is appropriate to the unit’s mission and build a more

                robust and effective scenario (higher headquarters’ S-2).
          • Verify the training area can support the unit’s training while in a chemical environment, based on

                the scenario design (protection).
          • Identify any hazards within the training complex.
          • Identify any sustainment limitations.
          • Verify CASEVAC notional or live capabilities in support of the training scenario (sustainment).
          • Identify command post capabilities and locations in the training areas—identify any external TOC

                configuration that can be incorporated for higher headquarters’ staff integration, as appropriate
                (mission command).

     6-58. When conducting a reconnaissance of the training location, units should include reviewing the training
     area to accommodate integration of the required and optional warfighting functions. Figure 6-8, page 108
     provides some considerations for each training requirement.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  107


---

Chapter 6

           Figure 6-8. Warfighting function integration, examples

108        TC 3-20.20                                              25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

ISSUE THE ORDER

     6-59. Commanders can issue an OPORD, a WARNORD, or use the training schedule, as necessary. The
     training unit must ensure all supporting elements or enablers have sufficient time to coordinate and prepare
     before the training event.

     6-60. WARNORDs to external organizations must include sufficient reaction time for the appropriate
     personnel and equipment. The WARNORD should include the appropriate tasks, required leaders, and
     equipment needed, as well as the unit’s training timeline including walk-throughs, rehearsals, and AARs.

     6-61. The OPORD or WARNORD should include the list of tasks evaluated during the event and the method
     of evaluation. Unit leadership should provide sufficient time for subordinate leaders to train the tasks and
     skills listed in the order. Unit leadership should ensure the appropriate training resources are available and
     accessible. Upon receipt, subordinate leaders who have SOPs for their organizations must ensure their
     procedures support and complement the higher headquarters’ publication.

     6-62. The order should include the specific mission orders the training units will receive as part of the training
     event. Those orders, FRAGORDs, and WARNORDs define the tasks required for the training unit to perform
     and outline the scheme of maneuver for the OPFORs conduct during training.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  109


---

Chapter 6

REHEARSE

     6-63. Rehearsals help leaders and subordinates understand the conduct of events and their responsibilities.
     Rehearsals help the organization synchronize training with times, places, logistics, and training support. A
     rehearsal of a concept drill helps leaders visualize an event as it unfolds, as well as likely branches and sequels
     if leaders must adjust the training. Leaders conduct rehearsals using the various levels and techniques
     described in ADP 7-0.

     6-64. Units use sand tables or terrain models to reinforce subordinate leader understanding of the conduct
     training event, as well as rehearse critical collective tasks trained during the LFX. The rehearsals should
     include digital and analog stimulus and drivers that serve to initiate action by the training unit, including the
     various branches and sequels developed.

     6-65. Rehearsals provide the operations officer with the ability to verify the conduct of the range events,
     movement and control of reconnaissance elements, and synchronize maneuver unit actions. Units then proof
     the scenario stimulus events or actions that drive the training unit to the appropriate tasks, drills, or actions.

     6-66. For maneuver units expecting to cross-attach to an external unit, the rehearsal is an opportunity for the
     platoon leadership to synchronize the unit SOP with the TACSOP of their gaining unit or attached personnel
     or organizations.

     6-67. Units should rehearse the training event from start to finish and confirm—
          • Training scenarios are understood by the training unit.
          • Sufficient space is available to provide standoff distances for the unit to build the appropriate tempo
                of each mission or phase of the operation.
          • Analog and digital systems are operational within the organization and are established to the higher
                headquarters regardless of their location.
          • Additional external evaluators or OC/Ts training support packages are complete and provide for
                multiple iterations.
          • Appropriate doctrinal publications are available for reference.
          • Sufficient administrative areas are available.
          • AAR capabilities are reviewed.
          • Available warfighting function enablers are integrated with the unit or appropriate subordinate unit.
          • The process for conducting direct fire support tasks.
          • The scheme of fires.
          • Methods and actions required to provide fires.
          • The process used to incorporate intelligence activities into the scenario.
          • The location of retraining for subordinate units, as necessary.
          • The CASEVAC and MEDEVAC plans.
          • The external evaluators or OC/Ts communications plan.
          • The flow of reporting.
          • The transition plan between units being trained.
          • Communication plan and reports for training completion to higher headquarters.

     6-68. The commander makes any necessary adjustments to the plan and issues FRAGORDs once the unit
     completes the walk-through and training rehearsal.

110  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

EXECUTE

     6-69. The platoon conduct LFX with all assigned or attached subordinate units and enablers. The warfighting
     function enablers support the platoon during LFX. The platoon can execute its LFX before or after Table IV,
     FTX in a manner that best fits the commander’s plan and range facility or training area availability and
     accessibility depending on the higher headquarters’ training plan.

     6-70. Table VI consists of 2 training days. Each training day has a day and a night phase with multiple
     progressive actions determined by the commander. The unit conducts dry rehearsals prior to live fire
     execution as part of the 8-step training model. An example of a rudimentary flow for the platoon LFX is—

          • Day 1, day phase:
                 Assembly area occupation.
                 Assembly area activities.
                 Tactical movement to the range facility or authorized training area; walk-through or rehearsal.
                 Establishment of hasty defensive positions.
                 Scenario rehearsal.
                 Fire coordination, distribution, and synchronization rehearsal.
                 Reconsolidation and reorganization rehearsal.
                 Tactical movement to an assembly area or battle positions not on the training facility.
                 Day AAR.

          • Day 1, night phase:
                 Preparation for night operations.
                 Assembly area activities.
                 Tactical movement to the range facility or authorized training area.
                 Establishment of hasty defense positions.
                 Scenario rehearsal.
                 Fire coordination, distribution, and synchronization rehearsal.
                 Reconsolidation and reorganization rehearsal.
                 Tactical movement to an assembly area or battle position not on the training facility.
                 Night AAR.

          • Day 2, day phase:
                 Assembly area occupation.
                 Assembly area activities.
                 Tactical movement to the range facility or authorized training area.
                 Establishment of hasty defense positions.
                 Scenario execution.
                 Reconsolidation and reorganization.
                 Tactical movement to an assembly area or battle positions not on the training facility.
                 Day AAR.

          • Day 2, night phase:
                 Preparation for night operations.
                 Assembly area activities.
                 Tactical movement to the range facility or authorized training area.
                 Establishment of hasty defense positions.
                 Scenario execution.
                 Reconsolidation and reorganization.
                 Tactical movement to an assembly area or battle position not on the training facility.
                 Night phase AAR.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  111


---

Chapter 6

EVALUATE THE TRAINING

     6-71. At the completion of each phase of Table VI, key leaders and their senior leaders discuss any
     shortcomings, gaps, or disconnects between SOPs by echelon. The discussion includes actions that require
     additional training or synchronization efforts, missing tasks or actions that should be included, and the
     determination and identification of any corrective actions necessary. Commanders use the AAR process to
     educate junior leaders and subordinates to reinforce the training objectives, identify shortfalls and corrective
     measures, as well as identify strengths within their formation.

PREPARE AND CONDUCT THE AFTER ACTION REVIEW

     6-72. The evaluation process is continuous and must be planned for the LFX. These evaluations are informal
     and are focused on the tasks, conditions, and standards selected for training. Leaders at every level conduct
     training evaluation, which is integral to training management.

             Note. FM 7-0 provides a detailed description of the AAR process.

     6-73. Commanders review the notes from the various external evaluators or OC/Ts, subordinate leaders,
     FSOs, and others to determine the proficiency of participating key leaders and their ability to execute the
     critical synchronization and maneuver tasks. Once complete with the review, the commander assesses the
     organization’s ability to complete the tasks at the reduced tempo, identifies key areas that must be retrained
     at the platoon level, and develops a retraining plan, as appropriate.

     6-74. At the completion of the training event, key leaders and their senior leaders discuss any shortcomings,
     gaps, or disconnects between SOPs by echelon, and determine any corrective actions. With input from the
     external evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership, the commander provides retraining opportunities as
     necessary, including additional scenarios.

             Note. At the completion of the platoon training strategy, the key leaders and their commander
             assess their performance during all six training events (Tables I through VI), review their SOPs,
             and validate their accuracy and effectiveness for continued use.

     6-75. External evaluators or OC/Ts should provide clear and articulate comments and insight on the positive
     and negative actions of the subordinate units they observed. External evaluators or OC/Ts use the training
     and evaluation outlines to guide them through the evaluation process and use a variety of doctrinal
     publications to support their comments, discussions, and critiques of the unit observed.

     6-76. External evaluators or OC/Ts and unit leadership should review the training and evaluation outlines,
     training notes, and observations to determine which tasks require additional attention through the remainder
     of the training density. They also can identify where additional complexities and tactical challenges can be
     induced during training where mastery has been demonstrated.

     6-77. The commander should share the overall performance of the unit, including observations, with the
     entire training unit. This allows subordinate units to learn from other units’ best practices, procedures, and
     tactics, and, at times, from their shortcomings or mistakes. The commander should identify leaders within
     the organization who have demonstrated superior performance and tactical competence by assigning them as
     external evaluators or OC/Ts during future training events, internal and external to the organization.

     6-78. During the AAR and with input from the external evaluators or OC/Ts, attached leaders, the facility
     instructors or operators, and unit leadership identify the unit or subordinate unit that needs to retrain specific
     tasks. Once the unit leadership identify a unit’s retraining needs, they should execute a retraining event of
     the scenario. For minor tactical adjustments for the unit or subordinate units, the commander may opt to
     discuss different methods to complete the task, followed by backbriefs using terrain models or sand tables,
     rather than executing the entire training scenario.

112  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                              Table VI – Live-Fire Exercise

RETRAIN

     6-79. Corrective training allows participants to translate observations and evaluation into corrective action.
     Additional training allows the participants to apply the lessons learned during execution and the AAR.
     Leaders understand that during the training event, participants do not typically perform all tasks to the desired
     standard. Therefore, during the short-range and near-term planning process, commanders provide flexibility
     within the training events that facilitate additional training immediately following the AAR.

     6-80. Units may not continue with more advanced, higher echelon collective live-fire events unless they have
     successfully completed this event based on the evaluation criteria. (See appendix B for collective training
     and live-fire evaluation.)

     6-81. Unit leadership identify the unit or subordinate unit that requires retraining of specific tasks during the
     AAR with input from the external evaluators or OC/Ts, attached leaders, and the facility operators. Once the
     commander or the platoon leadership identify the retraining needs of the unit, they should execute a retraining
     event of the scenario. This may consist of—

          • The entire platoon executing the training scenario again.
          • Rotating platoons through different mission tasks in specific phases of the FCX to reinforce

                supporting collective tasks that need additional repetitions to achieve the desired proficiency.
          • Minor tactical adjustments for the unit, and the platoon leader may opt to discuss different methods

                to complete the task followed by backbriefs using terrain models or sand tables rather than executing
                the entire training scenario.

     6-82. Once the unit leadership are satisfied with the unit’s demonstrated performance, they should review the
     unit SOP and TTP for required updates, revisions, or modifications again. The platoon leader and platoon
     sergeant should assess which components of the unit’s SOP and TTP need additional attention during the
     upcoming live-fire events.

BEST PRACTICES

     6-83. Units should consider including the following best practices in Table VI, LFX at the platoon level.
     Incorporation of these practices will assist the unit to get their Soldiers, teams, and platoons to a higher
     proficiency rapidly. Figure 6-9, page 114 provides a quick reference to some of the best practices for the unit
     to consider when planning, preparing, and executing Table VI.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  113

           Figure 6-9. Live-fire exercise best practices, example

                                                 Chapter 7

                            Platoon Proficiency

    Platoon proficiency is derived from the EXEVALs from Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX.
    This chapter describes the principles and procedures for determining platoon proficiency once
    the unit has completed the training plan. The chapter includes the proficiency rating
    condemnation criteria, methods to mitigate personnel loss, and various examples to manage
    the personnel turbulence between training densities.

OVERVIEW

     7-1. Units should maximize their time and resources by designing their platoon training plans such that
     platoons train through proficiency to support their platoon and company, whether as an organic small unit,
     an attachment, or a cross-attachment. This level of synchronization will enable the unit to transition to
     advanced, higher echelon collective training seamlessly.

     7-2. Platoon proficiency is based on two separate training events that are externally evaluated. These
     externally evaluated events provide the commander the primary assessment tool to evaluate the unit’s
     proficiency against an established standard as defined within the respective training publication(s), task
     training and evaluation outlines, and the unit’s SOPs. Platoon proficiency is derived from the externally
     evaluated events from Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX.

PLATOON TABLE IV, FIELD TRAINING EXERCISE

     7-3. Table IV is the platoon’s collective task proficiency evaluation, which supports the company’s overall
     collective task proficiency metric (see chapter 4 of this publication). This table includes execution of critical
     skills in a live environment. The training scenarios use blank ammunition, pyrotechnics, and battle effects
     simulators, as well as an appropriately trained and equipped OPFOR.

     7-4. It is a live-environment training event where platoons conduct an externally evaluated FTX. The
     EXEVAL utilizes the training and evaluation outlines for all the platoon’s supporting collective tasks directly
     related to the higher headquarters’ maneuver mission-essential tasks.

PLATOON TABLE VI, LIVE-FIRE EXERCISE

     7-5. Table VI is the platoon’s externally evaluated live-fire proficiency event. This live-fire proficiency event
     provides commanders with a common standard to create an unbiased assessment of the element’s overall
     proficiency. Successful completion of the Platoon Table VI is required to progress to any advanced collective
     live-fire event.

     7-6. It is an externally evaluated maneuver live-fire event that measures a unit’s proficiency in executing a
     series of supporting collective tasks based on their higher headquarters’ mission-essential task. It evaluates
     the key and subordinate leader’s ability to integrate organic weapons systems, subordinate units, and multiple
     warfighting functions. Table VI uses full-caliber training ammunition on an authorized live-fire facility or
     safety-certified training area.

     7-7. Units should understand the prerequisites and requirements for the platoon to achieve collective task and
     live-fire proficiency as a holistic view of their demonstrated proficiency. Figure 7-1, page 116 shows the
     platoon proficiency requirements for all platoons, regardless of composition.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  115


---

Chapter 7

           Figure 7-1. Platoon proficiency training requirements

116        TC 3-20.20                                             25 April 2025

                                                                                                                           Platoon Proficiency

COLLECTIVE TASK PROFICIENCY

     7-8. Platoon Table IV, FTX is used to determine the element’s collective task proficiency. It is an assessment
     of their collective supporting tasks that support the company and battalion mission-essential tasks.

LIVE-FIRE PROFICIENCY

     7-9. Platoon Table VI, LFX provides the EXEVAL that determines the live-fire proficiency. The live-fire
     proficiency for each type of platoon is described in detail within the appropriate training publication. It
     includes the standards that must be achieved to complete the training event successfully and describes the
     level of proficiency achieved based on those standards.

EXTERNAL EVALUATIONS

     7-10. Table IV, FTX, the collective task proficiency event and Table VI, LFX the live-fire proficiency event
     are externally evaluated using training and evaluation outlines. The training and evaluation outlines for the
     FTX and LFX EXEVALs can be downloaded from either the Army Training Network or the Central Army
     Registry websites.
     7-11. Table VI, LFX is externally evaluated to create an unbiased assessment of demonstrated performance
     of the required tasks and skills. For platoons, the evaluation commander determines the appropriate method
     of evaluation that meets their intent within the requirements stated in the training publication.
     7-12. The EXEVAL requirements are in figure 7-2. These requirements are for both Table IV and Table VI.

                              Figure 7-2. Platoon external evaluation requirements

                            SELF-EVALUATION NOT AUTHORIZED

          Units are not authorized to self-evaluate Table IV, FTX or Table VI, LFX unless they
          meet the parameters found within their training publication’s validation events for
          certifying proficiency in austere environments.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  117


---

Chapter 7

PROFICIENCY RATINGS

     7-13. The platoon training publications will provide guidance on the appropriate proficiency ratings awarded
     to a maneuver platoon. The proficiency ratings awarded by the evaluation commander are identified within
     the respective training publication. Generally, commanders award one of three proficiency ratings based on
     the element’s performance overall during Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX based on their tactical and
     live-fire performance (see figure 7-3).

T (Fully Trained)

     7-14. Unit has attained task proficiency to the Army standard, achieved a GO in 80 percent or more of both
     performance measures and leader performance measures, and has met 100 percent of all critical performance
     measures. The task is externally evaluated and meets the remaining requirements as outlined in the training
     and evaluation outline in accordance with the objective task evaluation criteria matrix.

P (Practiced)

     7-15. Unit has attained basic task proficiency with shortcomings, achieved a GO in 65 percent or more of all
     performance measures, achieved 75 percent or more of all leader performance measures, and has met
     100 percent of all critical performance measures. The unit’s shortcomings require significant training to meet
     the Army standard. The task is not externally evaluated and meets the remaining requirements as outlined in
     the training and evaluation outline in accordance with the objective task evaluation criteria matrix.

U (Untrained)

     7-16. The unit cannot perform the task. It achieved a GO in less than 65 percent of all performance measures,
     less than or equal to 75 percent in all leader performance measures, and less than 100 percent in all critical
     performance measures. The unit requires complete training on the task to achieve the Army standard.

     Figure 7-3. Platoon proficiency ratings

118  TC 3-20.20                               25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                                           Platoon Proficiency

VALIDATION

     7-17. Validation is a commander’s option to execute the Army standard live-fire proficiency through the
     minimum requirements to achieve a satisfactory result. Where validation events are authorized, the
     requirements are defined in the platoon’s training publication. Validation is only used in extreme
     circumstances where the commander requires confirmation of successful completion of a live-fire proficiency
     event.

             Note. There is no validation event for Table IV, FTX. Units can conduct Table IV with available
             resources and training areas.

     7-18. Units actively participating in regionally aligned force missions, deployments, or other critical missions
     outside the continental United States are authorized to exercise the validation event option, based on the
     following guidelines:

          • The brigade or equivalent commander authorizes the use of the validation event.
          • EXEVAL criteria are stated within the respective training publication, and these requirements will

                prescribe procedures when standard external evaluator or OC/T requirements cannot be achieved
                due to mission constraints.
          • Units must complete all critical live-fire tasks listed in the training publication.

SUSTAINING PROFICIENCY

     7-19. Once the element has successfully completed Table IV and Table VI, the unit must consider how the
     condemnation criteria affects their subordinate’s proficiency. The following paragraphs describe how units
     maintain their platoon proficiency, how to identify when a proficiency rating has triggered one or more
     condemnation criteria, and some options the commander can consider to reduce their impacts on unit
     readiness.

     7-20. The platoon training publication will describe in greater detail methods to sustain proficiency. The
     information is in general terms and provides an example of when a small unit sustains their proficiency, as
     well as an example of when the proficiency is condemned.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  119


---

Chapter 7

CONDEMNATION CRITERIA

     7-21. Each type of platoon follows up to four of the six condemnation criteria. Once the element achieves
     squad proficiency, if one or more of the following triggers exist the element loses its proficiency rating.

Time

     7-22. Twelve months have elapsed since the date the squad live-fire proficiency event was achieved. This is
     the maximum qualification duration for any qualification, certification, or proficiency rating.

Key Leader Turnover

     7-23. The minimum identified key leaders at echelon are no longer assigned to the positions within the small
     unit. Units have some flexibility to mitigate the loss of some senior leaders by promoting junior leaders to
     key leader positions provided they have participated as a leader in the previous Table IV or Table VI, for
     example.

Commander Assessment

     7-24. Any commander within the chain of command determines the subordinate unit’s proficiency is no
     longer at a trained “T” level. Commanders should continuously assess their subordinate unit’s proficiency.

Subordinate Element Proficiency

     7-25. When the unit fails to maintain the minimum required proficient subordinate elements (one level
     down). When losing proficiency, units should make corrections at the subordinate unit level first—fixing
     from the ground up.

Specialty Proficiency

     7-26. Where assigned, subordinate specialty units fail to maintain their required level of proficiency or
     required specific additional skill identifiers for critical personnel are not maintained.

                NO ALTERATIONS TO THE CONDEMNATION CRITERIA
                                              AUTHORIZED

          Units may not alter, change, or modify the condemnation criteria. Evaluation
          commanders have the option, based on their assessment of the element, to condemn
          any proficiency rating they are authorized to assign.

     7-27. The condemnation criteria apply to both the collective task proficiency and the live-fire proficiency
     events. Once any of the condemnation criteria are met, both the collective task proficiency and live-fire
     proficiency ratings for the squad are void, and the squad is considered unqualified.

120  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                                           Platoon Proficiency

TALENT MANAGEMENT

     7-28. Commanders can mitigate the impacts to their unit’s proficiency in a variety of methods. Talent
     management skills before, during, and after the conduct of the maneuver platoon training strategy is required
     by leaders to ensure the maximum proficiency is maintained and sustained within the larger organization.
     Platoon proficiency has a direct impact on the unit’s overall readiness.

Select Key Leaders Together

     7-29. This maximizes the key leader’s longevity and future proficiency ratings are more stable. Commanders
     should be cognizant of the junior leader’s promotion potential and dates of rank as promotions may cause
     turbulence in platoon proficiency.

Position by Potential

     7-30. Identify those young leaders that have solid promotion potential. Place those leaders in the next higher
     position (for example, gunner to vehicle commander or team leader as a squad leader) and have them achieve
     proficiency in advance of their pending or expected promotion.

Transfer Trained Key Leaders Together

     7-31. Promotions may initiate inter-battalion transfers. Commanders should consider moving the newly
     promoted key leader and key leader combinations together where the gaining unit within the battalion will
     benefit from maintaining proficient key leaders.

Cross-Train Potential Replacements

     7-32. Units should invest first-round-hit ammunition savings, commonly referred to as “ammunition
     harvesting,” to cross-train young leaders in key leader positions. In doing so, commanders can mitigate key
     leader turnover with trained and proficient leaders in stride with key leader turbulence.

Train to Employ Warfighting Functions

     7-33. Platoon formations provide commanders significant flexibility creating effective tactical units.
     Although one squad, for example, is not expected to operate as part of a platoon, training the squad leader
     how to direct external crew platforms, teams, and capabilities during squad training allows for plug-and-play
     platoon creation. The critical task of any platoon is the ability of the platoon leader to direct the movement
     and fires of an attached element effectively. The platoon leader that shows proficiency directing an element
     to perform functions they are certified on provides multiple platoon formations for a unit to employ without
     retraining.

     7-34. If commanders are not completely confident in the unit cohesion, capability, experience, and
     performance, they must rate them as unqualified/not proficient through the next training density. The unit
     should continue to train to the standards within the platoon training strategy.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  121


---

Chapter 7

PLATOON FORMATIONS

     7-35. For platoons, this publication describes the qualification process and defines a proficient platoon. Units
     will manage platoons by the platoon leadership and their subordinate leaders to maintain and sustain the
     greatest amount of experience within their formation.

     7-36. A platoon led by a lieutenant and consists of approximately 30 Soldiers, depending on the formation
     type. (See ADP 3-90.) The platoon is one of the most versatile formations a commander can employ. They
     can be tailored to any mission by attaching the desired warfighting functions as the commander requires.
     With that flexibility comes the inherent difficulty determining their true proficiency.

     7-37. Although there are multiple variations of platoon within the BCTs, there are two categories of platoons
     based on their composition. These are—

          • Organic-platoon formations where all elements of the platoon are the same type.
          • Task organized-platoon formations with multiple types of sections, squads, crews, or warfighting

                function elements.

     7-38. Figures 7-4 through 7-7, pages 123 to 126 show some examples of organic platoons that are common
     in maneuver formations. These formations are based on current MTOEs for various units.

     7-39. For formations where the commander task organizes warfighting functions within an MTOE platoon
     structure to meet the capabilities of the assigned missions, the commander must maintain the qualifications
     or proficiency ratings of the platoon and those of the integrated warfighting functions.

122  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                                     Platoon Proficiency

7-40. Figure 7-4 provides example MTOE structures for an Armor platoon and Infantry brigade combat team
(IBCT) rifle platoon, respectively. These structures are used for follow-on examples of proficiency, personnel
turbulence impacts to the platoon’s readiness, and an example of commander mitigation and talent
management skills applied to those issues to restore the platoon’s proficiency rating.

               Figure 7-4. Armor and rifle platoon formation structure, examples

Chapter 7

ARMOR PLATOON

     7-43. A proficient Armor platoon is a crew proficient platoon leader or platoon sergeant, with the minimum
     required proficient subordinate elements (one level down, by type) that together have successfully completed
     Platoon, Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX events, and have not met any of the condemnation criteria of the
     qualification period, as shown in figure 7-7.

     Figure 7-7. Proficient Armor platoon, example

126  TC 3-20.20                                     25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                                     Platoon Proficiency

7-44. Figure 7-8 provides an example of an unqualified Armor platoon through personnel turbulence. In this
example, there are two subordinate element key leaders that leave the organization. First, the gunner on the
platoon sergeant’s tank leaves through a normal permanent change of station move. This causes the 4-tank
to lose its qualification rating. Second, the vehicle commander from the platoon leader’s wing tank also
permanent change of station moves out of the unit. This causes the 2-tank to also lose its qualification rating.

7-45. Together, losing two of the four qualified crews condemns the platoon proficiency rating by triggering
the condemnation criteria where the platoon fails to maintain the required minimum qualified subordinate
elements. In this case, failing to maintain at least three of four crews condemns the platoon proficiency rating.

               Figure 7-8. Unqualified Armor platoon, example

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                      127


---

Chapter 7

     7-46. Figure 7-9 shows the continuing situation in the platoon. The commander reviews the loss of
     proficiency at the platoon-level and identifies a way to restore proficiency at the crew-level. The commander
     moves the gunner from the 2-tank to the platoon sergeant’s tank. The 2-tank has lost both the tank commander
     and gunner from the roster, but the platoon gains a tank commander/gunner combination on the platoon
     sergeant’s tank. The commander assesses the platoon sergeant and gunner during simulations training and is
     confident in their demonstrated performance to carry them qualified.

                              Figure 7-9. Commander mitigation options, example

     7-47. This change and assessment restores the platoon’s proficiency rating by correcting the minimum
     number of qualified subordinates in the formation. The platoon does require a new tank commander/gunner
     combination for the 2-tank for manning purposes. The commander will use the talent management skills
     (discussed in chapter 6) and assign a tank commander/gunner combination with longevity together to those
     positions when available.

128  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

Chapter 7

MECHANIZED RIFLE PLATOON

     7-53. A proficient mechanized rifle platoon is a crew-qualified platoon leader or platoon sergeant with a
     minimum of three qualified subordinate crews and a minimum of two proficient subordinate squads (see
     figure 7-13). The quantities of subordinate types of elements are addressed by the types of subordinates one
     echelon down. In this case, qualified crews and proficient squads.

     7-54. The commander may add or remove subordinate elements as necessary to build the desired small-unit
     formation to meet the intent. Alterations of the platoon can be made at any time, provided the commander
     assesses the new formation structure against the condemnation criteria to develop the platoon’s updated
     proficiency assessment.

     Figure 7-13. Proficient mechanized platoon, example

132  TC 3-20.20                                           25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                                     Platoon Proficiency

7-55. Figure 7-14 provides an example of a mechanized Infantry platoon that has experienced personnel
turbulence that results in its loss of their proficiency rating. In this example, the 1st Squad leader is promoted
to sergeant first class and is assigned as a platoon sergeant in another platoon. Additionally, the 2nd Squad’s
team leaders are promoted and assigned as squad leaders within another platoon. These turbulent moves
allow the experience and cohesion from one platoon to transfer to another platoon.
7-56. The loss of proficiency of two subordinate squads triggers the platoon’s proficiency condemnation
criteria. The platoon fails to maintain the minimum number of proficiency subordinate squads (2 of 3).

               Figure 7-14. Unqualified mechanized platoon, example

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                            133

Chapter 7

     7-57. Figure 7-15 shows one method a commander could use to restore the proficiency of the platoon. In this
     example, the commander elects to assign the senior team leader in 1st Squad as the squad leader. The
     commander also assigns an experienced Soldier within 1st Squad as the team leader. During the assessment,
     the commander rates the squad as proficient. These actions restore the platoon’s proficiency rating as both
     1st and 3rd Squads are proficient, achieving the minimum number of proficiency subordinate squads.

Figure 7-15. Commander’s talent management options restore platoon proficiency, example

WEAPON SYSTEM AND COMMON REMOTELY OPERATED WEAPON STATION-JAVELIN

     7-58. A proficient motorized platoon is a platoon leader or platoon sergeant, with the minimum required
     proficient crew platforms, two of three proficient squads, and a proficient weapons squad that together have
     successfully completed Platoon, Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX events, and have not met any of the
     condemnation criteria of the qualification period. This formation requires the unit to have redundant qualified
     vehicle commanders on both the medium caliber weapon system and Common Remotely Operated Weapon
     Station-Javelin (CROWS-J) to overcome frequent personnel turbulence (see figure 7-16).

     7-59. Figure 7-17 depicts common personnel and qualification losses that negatively impact the motorized
     platoon’s readiness and proficiency. In this example, the qualified medium caliber weapon station and
     CROWS-J vehicle commanders leave the organization. Additionally, the gunner for the heavy weapons
     squad loses its qualification rating due to exceeding the qualification duration of 12 months since the last
     qualification.

     7-60. Independently, the loss of the crews and the loss of the qualified gunner in the weapons squad cause

7-65. Figure 7-21 shows where the commander elects to man the crews with new personnel. In this example,
a new crew is assigned to the 3-track and a new gunner is assigned to the 6-track. The commander assesses
the platoon as unqualified due to the inexperience of the new crew. The commander moves an experienced
team leader from within 1st Squad to the team leader position. The commander also views the experience of
the new team leader insufficient to rate the team as proficient.

7-66. These commander decisions are more common than having the ability to restore proficiency with
qualified, cross-trained junior leaders. The platoon remains unqualified and continues to train through the
live-fire density planned in the coming months.

ASSAULT PLATOON

     7-67. A proficient assault platoon consists of a platoon leader and platoon sergeant with four subordinate
     crew platforms of varying types (see figure 7-22). Proficiency of this platoon is defined as a platoon leader
     or platoon sergeant and a minimum of three of four subordinate crew platforms (type agnostic) that have
     successfully completed Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX and have not triggered any condemnation criteria.

     7-68. The commander may add or remove subordinate elements as necessary to build the desired small-unit
     formation to meet their intent. Alterations of the section can be made at any time, provided the commander
     assesses the new formation structure against the condemnation criteria to develop the section’s updated
     proficiency assessment.

     Figure 7-22. Proficient assault platoon, example

4-vehicle is no longer assigned to the platoon. The loss of the two qualified gunners through qualification
duration or personnel movement condemn the platoon’s proficiency. The platoon fails to maintain the
minimum proficient subordinate elements.

Chapter 7

     7-70. Figure 7-24 provides an example of the unit making corrections to the assault platoon. In this example,
     the unit has created an effective cross-training and qualification program. Using qualified gunners from the
     cross-training program to back-fill those gunner positions with qualified firers, the platoon’s proficiency
     rating can be restored.
     7-71. One of the key principles of the IWTS is to build depth to the qualification rosters within the unit to
     maintain and sustain proficiency over the long-term.
     7-72. Units that include cross-training and qualification depth to their crew rosters provide multiple
     assignment options to the commander to build longevity of proficiency.

                                           Appendix A

                   Ammunition Requirements

This appendix lists the training ammunition, pyrotechnics, explosives, and other devices
recommended for the training events for the platoon. The information in this appendix lists the
recommended munitions for each training event. The quantities listed apply to a single weapon,
system, or platform. Training planners use the quantities listed as a guide to develop their
scenario and apply them appropriately across the training unit’s formation. The munition
quantities are recommended. Units may add additional quantities based on their training
objectives and scenario design to support the commander’s training plan.

        AMMUNITION RECOMMENDATIONS ARE FOR SECTION AND
                            PLATOON TRAINING COMBINED

     The ammunition quantities listed is a recommendation and does not reflect the current
     DA Pam 350-38, which is a resource tool that allows the unit planner to forecast
     ammunition for future training events.

     All training ammunition quantities listed in this appendix are recommendations. The
     quantities are to conduct both section and platoon training. Units must build their unit
     training plan and ammunition requirements for both section and platoon events and
     distribute those ammunition requirements.

Effective resourcing is the unit’s responsibility. Units must ensure sufficient time to forecast,
secure, and draw the appropriate munitions. Failure to provide a minimum of 90 days for
forecasting may not allow sufficient delivery time to the training installation to support the
event.

       Note. The munitions listed are recommended by type, quantity, and the Department of Defense
       identification code (DODIC). Units may encounter shortfalls when resourcing the munitions by
       type or quantity listed for several reasons. Unit commanders should coordinate with their higher
       headquarters’ operations staff office or assistant chief of staff, operations office to ensure the
       appropriate amount and type of munitions are available, authorized, and coordinated 90 days
       before executing the training event.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  145


---

     A-1. This platoon details the recommended munitions for Tables III through VI to support the platoon
     training strategy. Figure A-1 lists the recommended munitions by event but may not reflect the authorized
     munitions as listed in DA Pam 350-38 for the fiscal year. The recommended quantities create an effective
     and realistic training event that supports the unit’s live fire, supporting and key collective tasks battle effects,
     and combat realisms. The unit should plan for realistic training that includes blank, subcaliber, full caliber,
     pyrotechnics, simulators, and other special purpose munitions.

     A-2. When the recommended munitions are greater than the authorized munitions listed in DA Pam 350-38,
     the unit secures the appropriate quantities from the existing overall authorizations. When deficiencies exist,

for the firer, team, or small unit.

                                                Appendix B

                      Building Collective Training

    This appendix is an overview of the general process to build a collective training event for
    maneuver units. It includes warfighting function integration requirements, scenario
    development, targetry, and other actions to develop robust and effective training events.
    Commanders can use this appendix as a guide to build a scenario that meets the integration
    requirements of their event, particularly those that are evaluated externally. For each
    requirement at echelon, examples are given to show possible methods of completing the
    integration requirement. These examples provided are not finite. They provide the intent of the
    integration requirement and various methods.

    For each integrated function, notes are provided for live and virtual environments, as well as
    live-fire and force-on-force conditions, as appropriate. Units cannot integrate all warfighting
    functions in all environments or under all conditions. Units can select from multiple tasks to
    meet the requirements, notional options, or other methods to achieve the intent of the integrated
    function.

    Commanders and staff developing the training scenarios should review the examples to
    understand the intent of the integration. Units are encouraged to develop other methods to
    integrate the warfighting function requirements, provided the intent is maintained. All training
    maneuver collective training events focus on the unit’s mission-essential tasks and inherent or
    implied collective tasks.

UNIT TRAINING PLAN DEVELOPMENT

     B-1. Developing a unit training plan is a lengthy process. It requires research and analysis to create the
     commander’s assessment, mission-essential tasks with enabling and supporting collective tasks, warfighting
     skills, training goals, training objectives, and collective training best practices, at a minimum. Units conduct
     the research and analysis of these and other topics and integrate the command training guidance into the
     development process. Leaders and planners must be familiar with and see FM 7-0 for detailed information
     pertaining to unit training.

COMMANDER’S ASSESSMENT

     B-2. Prior to developing any unit training plan, commanders must make a complete assessment of the unit’s
     proficiency, training strengths, weaknesses, and the standards they wish to achieve. This assessment must be
     comprehensive to design training that achieves, improves, or sustains proficiency. Commanders assess and
     evaluate all aspects of training, including the planning, preparation, and execution. Leaders continuously
     monitor the unit’s collective tasks that support the higher echelon unit’s mission-essential task proficiency to
     determine a quantifiable snapshot of the unit’s current, direct fire training proficiency. From this snapshot,
     the unit can develop a training plan to correct deficiencies, sustain fundamentals, improve mastery, and build
     collective proficiency.

     B-3. The commander uses the specific task standards whenever possible to measure the abilities of
     subordinate unit proficiency using assessments from previous training and evaluation outlines and LFX
     records. Accurate and honest evaluations are necessary to identify where to place training emphasis.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  179


---

Appendix B

COLLECTIVE TASKS

     B-4. The Department of the Army standardizes the unit collective tasks for maneuver units to the platoon
     level to ensure that similar units have like-capabilities, regardless of their location, higher headquarters, or
     expected area of operations.

     B-5. Time, resources, and command emphasis must focus on training tasks that support the
     mission-essential task list. Commanders, platoon leaders, and platoon sergeants should use the appropriate
     individual and collective tasks that support their training objectives using the following references:

          • CATS.
          • Central Army Registry.
          • Digital Training Management System (DTMS).
          • Soldier’s manuals.
          • Soldier training publications.
          • DA Pam 350-38 for the fiscal year of training.
          • Deployment or mobilization plans.
          • Mission-essential tasks.
          • Supporting collective tasks.
          • Tactical mission tasks.
          • Army, area command, and local regulations.
          • Local SOPs.

     B-6. Commanders use training and evaluation outlines for the collective tasks, drills, and individual tasks
     that support the unit’s mission-essential task list. Commanders can access the outlines through DTMS and
     CATS found on the Army Training Network website.

     B-7. For the purposes of maneuver collective training at the platoon level, there are 16 maneuver formation
     variations within the three BCT formation types. Each of these formations has their specific mission-essential
     tasks and branch-unique enabling task sets. The mission-essential tasks and the enabling task sets create the
     foundation of collective training for the organization.

     B-8. Commanders build the entire training plan so their organizations can train to become proficient in their
     collective tasks. Commanders use their assessments of their organizations and subordinate units’ proficiency
     and training deficiencies to determine which supporting collective and supporting individual tasks to include
     in each of the prerequisite table training events. Table IV, FTX, serves as the unit’s collective task proficiency
     EXEVAL to rate the unit’s demonstrated proficiency executing supporting collective and mission-essential
     tasks against a trained OPFOR. Table VI serves as the unit’s LFX EXEVAL. This evaluation includes tactical
     and live-fire demonstrations of proficiency for the commander.

180  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

DEVELOPING THE TRAINING SCENARIO

     B-9. Figure B-1 describes a general outline for developing a squad/section and platoon-size training event.
     The outline is for reference only and is not all-inclusive. The outline shows the key actions, tasks, or steps
     units should review when developing the training scenario for their training event.

               Figure B-1. Developing the training scenario

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                    181


---

Appendix B

DETERMINE REQUIRED TASKS

     B-10. The section and platoon training strategies require organizations to perform certain tasks for evaluation
     using either Table IV, FTX or Table VI, LFX. These tasks are required to achieve unit proficiency.

     B-11.   The required tasks are derived from the following:
          •     Mission-essential task.
          •     Supporting collective task—five to seven tasks listed as critical, collective tasks as part of the
                mission-essential task.
          •     Decisive action enabling task.
          •     Basic tactical task.
          •     Integrated warfighting function tasks—integrated warfighting function tasks required to show
                proficiency at a specific capability, function, or action.
          •     Operational environment—four or more operational environment conditions must be included in
                the scenario.
          •     Commander selected—determined by commanders based on their current unit assessment or
                command training guidance.

Collective Task Requirements

     B-12. Each collective task and supporting collective task have an associated training and evaluation outline
     that the evaluation commander and the training unit can use during the evaluation to gain an objective
     assessment of their unit’s performance. The commander and training unit use collective tasks training and
     evaluation outlines alone during the evaluation. The collective tasks training and evaluation outlines are not
     aggregated to the supported collective task. Task and evaluation outlines can be accessed through the Army
     Training Network or CATS website.

     B-13. Figures B-2 through B-5, pages 183 through 184 illustrate the required collective task training and
     evaluation requirements for Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX. Commanders have the option to execute the
     platoon live-fire collective tasks by integrating into the platoon’s training event, another unit’s live-fire event,
     or executing a commander-designed LFX as a larger organization provided sufficient external evaluators or
     OC/Ts are available.

     B-14. Commanders must prioritize the training objectives for the prerequisite tables (Tables I, II, and III).
     Table IV requires the execution and EXEVAL of all collective tasks. For Tables V and VI, commanders
     select one or more collective tasks that can be evaluated under live-fire conditions as depicted in figures B-2
     through B-5, pages 183 through 184. The collective tasks selected for Table VI are externally evaluated.

                                                                                                               Building Collective Training

Integrated Warfighting Function Task Requirements

     B-19. Commanders develop their collective training scenarios based on their unit’s collective tasks and a
     critical, objective assessment of their unit’s tactical proficiency, and command training guidance that directly
     affect the success of the platoon to accomplish their mission. Commanders select all the tasks trained during
     the unit training plan based on collective tasks that require a demonstrated proficiency during Table IV and
     Table VI.

     B-20. Commanders design their scenarios in multiple lanes, phases, or stages, where each portion of the
     scenario trains one or two collective tasks in a progressive manner. Successful completion of each lane (based
     on the commander’s assessment using the SOP and training and evaluation outlines as a guide) moves the
     formation tactically to the next lane.

     B-21. Trainers brief the training audience on the tasks for each lane for Table III. This builds their proficiency
     so that they execute the tasks intuitively based on tactical situations during Tables IV, V, and VI, respectively.

     B-22. Table III scenarios must include training on commander-selected tasks specific to the warfighting
     functions. Commanders integrate these tasks across Table II through Table VI. Commanders should include
     a repetition of the selected tasks during both the day and night phase of the training scenario.

     B-23. Each warfighting function includes optional or required tasks commanders integrate into their training
     scenario. For special purpose weapons, commanders determine how many iterations of the special weapons
     to integrate during the day and night. Commanders select from the special purpose weapons available to
     them, as well as how the scenario initiates using those special purpose weapons.

     B-24. Commanders integrate tasks as appropriate and where they deem them most beneficial. The section
     and platoon conduct warfighting function, integrated tasks during limited and unlimited visibility conditions,
     across the entire training table scenario, not on each maneuver task trained.

             Note. For example, during a 2-day Table III at a local training area, the commander selects call
             for indirect fire during the platoon attack by fire day phase, identifying priority of fires to each
             platoon conducting the lane. On day 2 of the STX, the commander includes training for each
             platoon to conduct a call for fire illumination mission. The commander incorporates the call for
             fire task into the STX day operations using HE and smoke and into night operations using
             illumination rounds.

     B-25. During Tables IV and VI, the scenario must include various warfighting function tasks that are relevant
     to the squad’s/section’s and platoon’s collective tasks. Figure B-6, page 186 shows the requirements for
     squad through platoon to illustrate the progressive nature of the training. This figure also serves to articulate
     the mandatory training tasks or topics units must integrate across their training plan.

     B-26. Commanders must incorporate and evaluate the required elements of the warfighting functions listed
     in figure B-6, page 186 during the execution phase of Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX, at a minimum.
     Elements indicated as optional are not required due to limited availability; however, units are highly
     encouraged to include these elements into the scenario when possible.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  185

            Figure B-6. Maneuver collective training integration requirements

                                                                                                               Building Collective Training

Operational Environment Requirements

     B-27. Units are required to develop scenarios that include influences of operational variables (political,
     military, economic, social, information, infrastructure, physical environment, and time) on METT-TC (I).
     Figure B-7 provides scenario examples of operational environment variables. For section and platoon training
     events, scenarios must be dynamic and complex. Scenarios must include four or more operational
     environment conditions that include a hybrid threat, various types of terrain, time restrictions, and social
     variables. Commanders can select additional variables at their discretion.

               Figure B-7. Operational environment variable scenario, examples

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                                       187


---

Appendix B

Threat or Target Presentations

     B-28. There are three method threats presented to the training unit: static, dynamic, and complex. These
     methods vary based on the nature of the event environment.

Static

     B-29. The threats or targets are fixed in nature. Static threats are not used during force-on-force events.
     During live fire, static threats are represented as a fixed target scenario that is presented once per engagement.
     Targets that are not defeated are not re-presented.

Dynamic

     B-30. Dynamic targetry or threats deplete through battlefield loss during the engagement. During live fire,
     the dynamic threat is depicted through a depleting series of presentations. During the initial target
     presentation, the unit is presented with a fixed number of enemy targets. Subsequent target presentations
     include only the number and type that are not adequately serviced or defeated by the firing unit.

Complex

     B-31. The complex threats or targets presented to the training element deplete through battlefield loss during
     the engagement. through all environments, the training unit may experience losses of combat power during
     the engagement. This is done either through simulations modeling, laser-based devices during force-on-force
     events, or OC/T interaction during live fire. The complex threats provide the most realistic dilemmas within
     the constraints of the training environment.

          • Force-on-force events:
                 The OPFOR has the maximum latitude to aggressively conduct movement and maneuver to
                     gain positions of advantage.
                 A complex live training environment requires a minimum of four—economic (terrain), time,
                     military (threat), and social (population)—or more operational variables.
                 Brigade and higher units require all eight operational variables to be replicated in varying
                     degrees based on the task being trained.

          • Live-fire events:
                 Complex threats in live-fire scenarios have depleting targetry and can move tactically,
                     evasively, or close-with the training unit.
                 The complex aspect is depicted through a depleting or reinforcing enemy scenario.
                 During the initial target presentation, the unit is presented with a fixed number of enemy targets.
                 Subsequent target presentations are based upon the speed, accuracy, and efficiency of the firing
                     unit’s ability to maximize direct fires and integrate indirect fire to defeat the enemy.

     B-32. Figure B-8 shows the type of threat presentations by training event environment that can be used in
     each of the training events. Table I, TACSOP is a hands-on and written evaluation and does not require any
     threat or targetry presentations.

188  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                           Building Collective Training

          Figure B-8. Threat presentation methods by event (training environment)

B-33. The decisive action training environment is a composite of conditions, circumstances, and influences
that units could encounter in current and future military operations. A decisive action training environment
reflects a real-world operational environment as it is geographically centered on a region that provides the
entire operational environment and hybrid threat (OPFOR) conditions needed to represent training conditions
realistically and effectively that challenge any Army task; it uses fictitious names to comply with AR 350-2.

B-34. The exercise support application and the exercise design tool are online programs that host existing
exercise support packages complete with—

     • Validated operational environment compliant scenarios.
     • OPORDs and road to war.
     • Role player scripts and human networks.

B-35. The exercise support application allows users to download exercise products into computer-based
program tools. The application enables training developers to use automated tools that update or change
existing exercise support packages or create new ones from scratch. The exercise support application and the
exercise design tool are available at the U.S. Army Training and Doctrine Command G-2 Information
Operations Network website.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  189


---

Appendix B

IDENTIFY THE TRAINING ENVIRONMENT AND CONDITIONS

     B-36. The unit conducts each training event in a specific training environment. The conditions of the training
     environment determine the supporting requirements that build the training scenario. In general, the training
     strategy includes the following environment and condition combinations:

          • Virtual.
          • Live, TADSS-based.
          • Live, live fire.

     B-37. The commander uses the appropriate environment and condition set to determine the training resource
     requirements. The training unit acquires the training resources organically or externally from the evaluation

Appendix B

TRAINING FACILITIES

     B-41. Each training event requires sufficient facilities to support the company-size element. This includes
     movement and maneuver space, surface danger zone considerations, bivouac locations, OPFOR assembly
     areas and maneuver space, and life support for the training audience, OPFOR, and external evaluator or OC/T
     elements.

     B-42. The design of most maneuver live-fire range and digital multipurpose range facility live-fire ranges
     support crew through platoon-size elements through qualification. Planners must identify and secure these
     limited resources early in the planning process. Planners, if necessary, should strive to link noncontiguous
     training areas and live-fire ranges that are near one another to facilitate a realistic flow of the scenario. The
     close proximity of the training areas and live-fire ranges enables planners to develop realistic and logical
     graphic control measures and to support accurate reports.

     B-43. Due to the sensitive infrastructure found on many live-fire ranges, obstacle emplacement and breaching
     with live demolitions may be prohibited. Local installation training areas should support these types of
     tactical enabling tasks. An effective method is to have the training unit perform the breach (offense) or
     establish an obstacle (defense) in an adjacent training area prior to moving onto the live-fire range. Units
     must work closely with their local range operations during this nonstandard range development process. The
     most common facilities that support maneuver collective live-fire training are shown in figure B-13.

ENVIRONMENTAL CONSIDERATIONS

     B-44. Leaders should be prepared for seasonal environmental impacts during their range time. (See
     figure B-14.) Leaders should consider the following conditions during range planning:

     Figure B-14. Environmental consideration examples
