                                                                                                               Building Collective Training

Facility Support Personnel

     B-45. Except for force-on-force training areas, each facility used during the company training plan has
     assigned or required support personnel. These personnel ensure the safe conduct of the event, the delivery of
     combat-realistic scenarios, and administrative functions. Units must coordinate with their installation to
     understand the organic support personnel for each facility scheduled, their hours of operation during the
     training event, the level of service provided, and identify any unit-provided support requirements.

Training Ammunition

     B-46. Appendix A includes lists of training ammunition recommendations for each training event by weapon,
     system, or unit. The munitions listed, including pyrotechnics, are the baseline start point recommendation.
     The amount of ammunition authorized and available has a direct correlation to the number of targets that
     trainers can incorporate into the scenario throughout the development process.

     B-47. When selecting the number of live-fire targets to incorporate and present during the live-fire scenario,
     commanders, Master Gunners, and planners must consider the amount of ammunition available.

     B-48. Throughout the unit’s live-fire training plan, it is likely that units will be able to harvest ammunition
     from previous live-fire events, commonly known as first-round hit savings. Units that have additional training
     ammunition should include additional targets within their scenarios during Tables V and VI or ensure
     sufficient munitions are secured for any necessary retraining. When adding additional targetry where
     harvested training ammunition is available, units should follow the target increase rules of thumb as described
     in figure B-15, page 198. This figure provides the standard ammunition-to-target ratio for the most common
     weapons found within maneuver platoons.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  197


---

Appendix B

                           Figure B-15. Targetry increases by ammunition available

     B-49. During scenario or engagement development for the collective training event, the unit determines the
     training combat load by type of firer, platform, or system. The training combat load is multiplied by the
     number of firers, platforms, or system, respectively. Dividing those results by the ratios listed provide the
     maximum number of target sets the scenario can integrate for the entire event.

     B-50. A best practice for small arms for the individual Soldier is to provide training munitions comparable
     to the actual combat load per Soldier. For example, a Soldier assigned an M4-series carbine should be
     provided 210 rounds of the training munition for the event. This represents a complete combat load and
     reinforces the “train as you fight” tenant of training. It is not meant that the Soldier will expend all these
     rounds, but rather have the weight and restrictive range of motion carrying the combat load during training.
     The unit will reconcile the unused munitions back into their training account at the end of the training density
     and reforecast them as appropriate.

     B-51. When unexpected training munition availability occurs, units should work to integrate their use into
     the training event. Units can incorporate these unforeseen munitions in several ways that are beneficial to the
     training unit, proficiency, the number of repetitions, and increased experience.

     B-52. One option is to incorporate an additional branch or sequel into the live-fire scenario. One example is
     to have the unit break contact, bound back to a previous battle position, and then re-present the targets
     associated with that phase of the scenario. This allows the commander to incorporate displacement criteria,
     evaluate an additional collective task, and provides the unit with additional live-fire training.

198         TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                               Building Collective Training

     B-53. Another option is to present the appropriate number of alternate targets (based upon the amount of
     additional ammunition) together with primary targets. Doing so exposes the unit to a target-rich environment
     and provides the unit with additional live-fire training without the need to develop additional drivers,
     branches, or sequels. These additional target sets in the presentation may negatively impact the unit’s ability
     to achieve the kill standards expected of their live-fire tactical mission tasks or collective tasks. See the kill
     standards platoon for more information.

Targetry to Support the Live-Fire Scenario

     B-54. Master Gunners and planners determine the number of targets or target sets that must be presented
     during the training event overall. They apply the ratios listed in figure B-14, page 196 to the training
     ammunition available to the unit. The scenario develops when the targets are presented by phase of the
     operation and how they are presented to the appropriate subordinate unit so the units can effectively engage
     them.

                           TRAINING AMMUNITION TO TARGETRY

          Although the ammunition quantity is available, it is not expected that it is all expended.
          It is provided to provide sufficient targetry in tactical arrays, the unit can adequately
          demonstrate its lethality, provide sufficient munitions for retraining, and replicate
          combat loads for individual and some crew-served weapons. Unused training
          munitions must be quickly accounted for, turned-in to the supporting ammunition
          supply point, reconciled in the Total Army Munitions Information System, and
          re-forecasted to support subsequent training events.

     B-55. Targets support the live-fire scenario by portraying the enemy threat and evoke specific actions from
     the training unit. Planners must use special consideration when selecting the amount, location, and types of
     threat targets to present to the training unit. Companies, platoons, sections, squads, crews, and Soldiers train
     to use specific battle drills, weapons, munitions, and engagement techniques to defeat various types of threats.
     The scenario developer must build the targets into the scenario as drivers to initiate these actions.

     B-56. Special purpose weapons and some integrated warfighting function tasks may require specialty targets.
     Specialty targets include bunkers, trenches, building facades, and high-value targets. Units must identify any
     targetry requirements that require instrumentation, either with TADSS or with facility-provided equipment
     that facilitate safety, combat realism, or effect evaluations during the training event. Planners should see
     TC 3-20.31, TC 25-8, and DA Pam 385-63 for information pertaining to live-fire operations and scenario
     development.

     B-57. All target sets must be presented to 90-percent visibility to the primary firer or element through
     90 percent of the engagement presentation, during the offense or defense tasks. Units must proof their
     scenarios to ensure the Army-standard target visibility is maintained for all presentations. For those targets
     that cannot achieve the 90-percent visibility through 90-percent movement through the firing box standard,
     units should relocate the emplacement, select another emplacement, or alter the height when reasonable.

     B-58. All targets on collective training live-fire events must have a method of thermalization applied. This
     can include thermal blankets, reverse polarity applications, or other means that provide a clear contrast
     between the target, mechanical heat sources, and the background. Thermalization is required during both day
     and night engagements as thermal optics and sensors are the primary sights on most weapons and systems.

     B-59. For the purposes of collective live-fire training, targetry has two types: panel targets and troop sets.
     Units must understand these terms to build the scenario steps correctly to evaluate the unit’s lethality,
     employment techniques, and tactical mission tasks properly involving live-fire engagements.

          • Panel—individual plywood or similar construction material panels that replicate vehicles, systems,
                or aircraft.

          • Troop set—multiple troop or troop-like panels that replicate a single dismounted troop, three troop
                rocket-propelled grenade team, or seven troop threat dismounted squad.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  199

Appendix B

     B-60. These two targetry types are used to determine the appropriate kill standard for the collective tasks
     directed within the event’s OPORD or FRAGORDs where the unit is specifically assigned to conduct a
     collective task or tactical mission task. Figure B-16 provides several examples of each live-fire targetry type.

     Figure B-16. Targetry examples by type

                                                                                                               Building Collective Training

Kill Standard

     B-61. A kill standard determines if a target is defeated, a target set is effectively serviced, or a tactical mission
     task is completed to standard. Units must understand these three types of kill standards to build the live-fire
     scenario and the threat presentations, as well as understand how they translate into live-fire proficiency.

          • Panel kill standard—the number of hits required to defeat a target or target set by caliber.
          • Troop set kill standard—typically, when one silhouette in the target set is hit with small-arms

                munitions, or when the strike of a grenade or simulated explosive training projectile (for example,
                40-millimeter grenade) strike within 5 meters around the target set itself.
          • Tactical mission task kill standard—the percentage of the scenario step’s overall targets and target
                sets have met their target or target kill standards.

     B-62. For example, if three targets and one troop set are presented during a defensive task, the unit must
     defeat 75 percent of those threats in the presentation by achieving the corresponding target or target set kill
     standard. In this example, the training unit must successfully achieve the kill standard on three of the four
     targetry presented overall.

     B-63. Figure B-17, page 202 provides an example of kill standards for panels and troop sets by caliber.

             Note. Not all caliber or munition types can defeat each threat target. This list is for training
             purposes only. It imposes common range restrictions (for example, medium and large caliber
             versus troop sets) and does not include varying capabilities of some munition types for range to
             target, spin decay, or other considerations.

                                                                                                               Building Collective Training

Training Aids, Devices, Simulators, and Simulations

     B-66. TADSS are key enablers to effective maneuver training in force-on-force and live-fire events. Units
     must plan for the training, draw, installation, and alignment of the available systems.

     B-67. During force-on-force, Table III, STX and Table IV, FTX events, units must ensure the appropriate
     laser-based systems are available for their entire formation, including the OPFOR. This includes all weapons,
     systems, and crew platform systems. When systems are not available for a weapon or system, units should
     coordinate for a similar system. Units must ensure the appropriate training munitions (blanks, backblast
     simulators, and main gun simulators) are forecasted to support the TADSS. Units must also ensure sufficient
     controllers are available for the external evaluator or OC/T structure assigned to the event.

     B-68. During live fire, Table V, FCX and Table VI, CALFEX events, units must use TADSS such as the
     laser target interface device for antitank guided missile systems when practice or live missiles are unavailable.
     The TADSS allows the scenario to include specific targetry capable of providing downrange feedback during
     device-based engagements. Units should consider keeping TADSS systems installed on their Soldiers,
     weapons, systems, and crew platforms as a means for the external evaluator or OC/T to assess casualties or
     battle damage and to shape the training event to meet specific drivers, branches, and sequel actions.

     B-69. All targets (panels) are required to provide a hostile fire signature after the first 8 seconds of
     presentation. Once the training unit achieves the specific kill standard for the target, the defeated target must
     present a steel-on-steel or similar battle effect simulator. This indicates to the training unit the target panel
     was defeated and is in the process of lowering.

     B-70. All troop sets (troop and troop-like targets) must provide a visible muzzle flash for the overall target
     set that begins at 8 seconds of presentation, and periodically present a muzzle flash every 5 to 8 seconds for
     the remainder of the presentation. Once the training unit achieves the kill standard for each specific target set
     presented, the target set will be lowered.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  205


---

Appendix B

DETERMINE EXTERNAL SUPPORT REQUIREMENTS

     B-71. When conducting an externally evaluated training event, the battalion staff must coordinate for the
     OPFOR and external evaluator or OC/T element support. These elements are tasked according to the
     commander’s intent. Both the OPFOR and external evaluator or OC/T element must be trained and certified
     to the evaluation commander’s satisfaction to ensure the most effective, objective assessment of the training
     audience’s proficiency can be effectively determined. The battalion staff is also responsible for ensuring the
     necessary training munitions, pyrotechnics, vehicles, and life support for the duration of the tasking are
     properly resourced.

Opposing Force

     B-72. The OPFOR element must be sufficient in composition, capability, and strength to present an effective
     aggressor for the training scenario, its drivers, branches, and sequels. The evaluation commander determines
     the OPFOR composition, training, and certification based on the assessed tasks, conditions, standard, and
     event flexibility discussed in paragraphs B-73 through B-80.

Composition

     B-73. The OPFOR must be of sufficient composition to enable multiple or simultaneous iterations. The
     OPFOR possesses the flexibility to provide retraining while another unit is conducting the event.

Ammunition

     B-74. The tasked unit provides training for the OPFOR. The evaluation commander’s staff must ensure
     sufficient munitions are available and specified to the tasked unit for proper forecasting, draw, use, and
     reconciliation.

Pyrotechnics

     B-75. The OPFOR requires pyrotechnics in the form of smoke, signals, and simulators. OPFORs’
     pyrotechnics are based on their role, composition, and number of iterations expected.

Training and Certification

     B-76. Evaluation commanders determine the training and certification process for the OPFOR element to
     ensure they are presenting the tactical dilemmas, observable, or discoverable level of threat to the training
     audience. Their actions serve as drivers during the tactical scenario that instigate expected and desirable
     reactions from the training audience. Their training and conduct are a vital component to the training event’s
     effectiveness.

Virtual Opposing Force Academy

     B-77. An online application that educates leaders on how to execute company-level OPFOR counter-tasks in
     support of unit training objectives. The curriculum is also relevant to training at the platoon level. The Virtual
     OPFOR Academy provides downloadable OPFOR tactical counter-tasks with task-condition-standard of a
     CATS to users. The Virtual OPFOR Academy also provides an instructor-led video that explains each
     OPFOR task and an immersive video that demonstrates the task being conducted. The Virtual OPFOR
     Academy website also provides users access to hybrid threat (OPFOR) doctrine, OPFOR TTP, the
     Worldwide Equipment Guide, the decisive action training environment and OPFOR smart book, and other
     useful products.

Vehicles

     B-78. The tasked unit provides vehicle support to the OPFOR. This includes not only tactical vehicles that
     replicate commanders’ OPFOR composition but also any administrative vehicles that directly support their
     mission.

206  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                               Building Collective Training

Training Aids, Devices, Simulators, and Simulations

     B-79. All OPFOR weapons, systems, and crew platforms must be provided laser-based training systems,
     respectively. Some systems, such as the M3 MAAWS or MK19, do not have associated laser-based training
     devices. Units should provide comparable devices (for example, AT-4 MILES) or direct the use of fire
     markers or external evaluators or OC/Ts to assess damage or casualties.

Life Support

     B-80. The evaluation commander is responsible to provide the OPFOR any necessary life support or task a
     subordinate unit with those functions during the conduct of the event. Life support includes bivouac or
     housing, ration cycle, and field hygiene, at a minimum. The tasked unit is responsible for maintenance and
     recovery activities to ensure the OPFOR maintains its operational readiness.

Observer-Coaches/Trainers

     B-81. The external evaluator or OC/T element must be sufficient in composition, capability, and strength to
     provide an effective assessment of proficiency of the evaluated tasks, a close-proximity safety officer, and a
     means to induce additional rigor to the training audience as the evaluation commander directs. It also may
     serve the running scenario by acting as a fire marker, assessing casualties, and determining battle damage to
     both blue force and OPFOR elements.

     B-82. The evaluation commander determines the external evaluator or OC/T composition, training, and
     certification based on the assessed tasks, conditions, standards, and event flexibility. The minimum number
     of trained and certified external evaluators or OC/Ts for squad/section and platoon-level training events are
     shown in figure B-20.

         Figure B-20. Observer-coach/trainer and radio-telephone operator driver minimum
                                                          requirements

COMPLETE THE TRAINING SCENARIO

     B-83. Planners visualize the training event by drawing an event sketch and detailing how the unit can execute
     the training. They write a list of actions that meet the specified training objectives. They consider the time

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  207


---

Appendix B

     available to train versus the number of possible iterations to attain proficiency. This visualization serves as
     the concept for executing the training event. Once planners develop a sketch and visualized concept from
     start to end and the commander approves it, additional resourcing for the event can begin.

     B-84. Planners identify and request resources early and track their availability throughout the planning and
     preparation phases. Effective planners use the CATS, training and evaluation outlines, and unit historical
     records as a starting point to identify resources. Typically, historical records document resources the unit
     needed and when it needed them. Successful planners know what resources they used previously for similar
     training events. The event planner ensures all event resources are available by including any newly identified
     resources. DTMS has a checklist tool that allows users to set up and track the status of training resources
     associated with training events.

     B-85. An event administrative OPORD is required to execute the training and includes all necessary
     coordination. It explains the training concept, concept of support, training resourcing requirements, and
     subordinate unit and staff responsibilities to execute the training event. Additionally, the plan identifies both
     tactical orders and OPFOR orders to drive the training and stimulate task execution.

     B-86. Units use the operations process to develop the training scenario by including WARNORDs, OPORDs,
     FRAGORDs, and detailed graphic control measures. The different forms of orders are used during the
     execution of the scenarios to initiate the desired action or response from the training unit beginning with an
     initial OPORD. The various forms of orders in the scenario are used as drivers, branches, and sequels within
     the scenario to contribute to the method of executing the STX lanes for each unit.

DRIVERS, BRANCHES, AND SEQUELS

     B-87. Units should plan to include a series of drivers, branches, and sequels to meet their training objectives
     to achieve proficiency in their platoons. These are training terms used for maneuver collective training that
     build the story boards for the training events. They provide pre-build alterations to the tactical missions based
     on the training unit’s performance, capability, or reactions to tactical dilemmas.

     B-88. They assist in steering the training unit toward the expected conditions of the task, establish the
     appropriate tempo, build the battlefield effects, and develop the tactical situation. Figure B-21 provides an
     example of drivers, branches, and sequels for collective training events, Table II through Table VI.

          • Drivers—
                 Are the intelligence, real or notional, used to instigate the training unit to execute a task, drill,
                     or action.
                 Are the task, drill, or action that directly supports the tempo, orientation, direction, and rigor of
                     the scenario (mission).

          • Branches—are contingency plans or options built into the base plan for changing the disposition,
                orientation, or direction of movement during the mission.
                 For collective training scenarios (missions), they provide the commander with options to alter
                     the training focus during the mission, based on observed performance.
                 They aim to create the best possible results or conditions for the training unit.

          • Sequels—are actions taken at the conclusion of the mission.
                 They are based on the outcomes of the mission.
                 Sequel actions provide missions for subordinate units and establish new objectives.
                 For collective training scenarios (missions), units use sequels when they have successfully met
                     the training objectives to posture them for a continuing mission, follow-on lane, or subsequent
                     phase.

             Note. The definitions for drivers, branches, and sequels are for training purposes only as they
             apply to collective training events and the development of the various training scenarios.

208  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                           Building Collective Training

                        Figure B-21. Drivers, branches, and sequels, example

B-89. Driver, branches, and sequels are developed to provide the most comprehensive training plan to ensure
the unit is—

     • Challenged, not overwhelmed.
     • Learning, not complacent.
     • Rewarded and retrained, as necessary.

B-90. Scenario developers must coordinate and collaborate with the installation’s Training Soldier System
assets, range safety office, and installation and other local agencies, particularly when developing live-fire
scenarios. Units must follow the most stringent regulation or policy, from a Soldier safety perspective, when
developing any training event.

MISSION COMMAND WARFIGHTING FUNCTION INTEGRATION

     B-91. Unit planners must consider building scenarios that provide commanders the opportunity to exercise
     their use of the six Army warfighting functions. This allows unit commanders to be more proficient when
     integrating available enablers that may assist in accomplishing specified tasks assigned from higher
     headquarters. Commanders may inject thought-processing scenarios during rehearsals if desired enablers are
     not readily available during Tables I and II. This allows subordinate leaders to execute tactical
     decision-making during rehearsals and the training event. Units should maximize the use of enablers during
     each table to develop or reinforce unit SOPs and build coordination between supporting elements prior to

                                                                                                               Building Collective Training

COLLECTIVE TRAINING BEST PRACTICES

     B-98. During all collective training events, units should invest time and effort developing training discipline
     to provide passive protection, freedom of maneuver, and the ability to reduce detection by threats operating
     in multiple domains (land, air, sea, space, and cyber).

     B-99. The following paragraphs list some best practices to introduce into the unit’s training discipline as
     often as possible. This is a condensed and generalized list of past and current discipline issues that plague
     operations. They have been tailored in context to the unit’s training plans but are equally important during
     the offense and defense.

ACTIVE PROTECTION AGAINST DETECTION IN ALL DOMAINS (PROTECTION)

     B-100. Units must practice and enforce noise, light, litter, and cellular discipline during training. Ongoing
     operations across the globe have proven discipline in these areas is critical to the unit’s survival. Units that
     lack discipline in these four areas provide obvious, rapid target detection and acquisition. Units must
     deliberately incorporate discipline in these four areas into their training and strictly enforce those standards
     during all training events.

TRAIN DISPLACEMENT CRITERIA (PROTECTION)

     B-101. Near-peer adversaries have enablers and capabilities across all the warfighting functions. To
     maintain overmatch, training must focus on applying combat power effectively and faster. Near-peer
     adversaries focus on counter-batteries, counter-unmanned aircraft systems (C-UASs), and other active
     protection measures against friendly combat power. To maintain initiative, combat power, and overmatch,
     units must practice displacement criteria as often as possible. A stationary maneuver force can become a
     combat-ineffective maneuver force quickly. Mortars, field artillery, and crew platforms must deliberately
     practice displacement, movement to hide, alternate, or subsequent positions to minimize or eliminate their
     stationary signature.

REDUCE ELECTROMAGNETIC FOOTPRINT (PROTECTION)

     B-102. Cell phones should not be part of the unit’s primary, alternate, contingency, and emergency (PACE)
     communication plan. Personal cellular phones have no place in any tactical training environment. Units are
     adding commercial cell phones as an emergency communications device in their PACE plan, which will
     prove lethal in a combat environment; furthermore, units create an enormous electromagnetic signature.
     However, the use of cell phones as an emergency means of communication may be prudent only for the
     personnel supporting training.

MASTER DIGITAL SKILLS, MASTER ANALOG FUNDAMENTALS (MISSION COMMAND)

     B-103. Units operate to the maximum extent using secure tactical communications systems. Units lack
     experience in maximizing system ranges, planning for retransmission positioning, and operating in frequency
     hopping modes on tactical radios. Secure communication may not be possible during live-fire training events
     based on range and installation safety requirements; however, units must force mastery whenever possible.

MAP READING IS A CRITICAL SKILL (MOVEMENT AND MANEUVER, PROTECTION)

     B-104. Units must train to operate without the aid of GPS for accurate positional data. They develop
     techniques and identifiable terrain reference systems to validate positional data through manual land
     navigation skills to enhance readiness, practice a perishable skill, and identify when GPS spoofing occurs. A
     simple survey of personnel can reveal that the average person would tend to believe an inaccurate GPS
     position over their own assessment of physical position due to lack of confidence in manual land navigation
     skills.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  217


---

Appendix B

MAINTAIN REDUNDANT CAPABILITIES (DIGITAL TO ANALOG) (MISSION COMMAND,
PROTECTION, INTELLIGENCE)

     B-105. Units must develop criteria and procedures to switch to analog tracking of the common operating
     picture to continue operations. Units focus on the cyber threat and feel that they can secure themselves from
     network intrusion by operating in more secure modes. This does not account for environmental threats to
     digital systems that likely pose a greater chance of occurrence, such as maintenance faults, component
     failures, battle damage to systems and antennas, and other incidents that may also affect digital reliance.
     Practice operating and maintaining situational awareness using analog tracking systems for extended periods,
     and then further develop the procedures to capture the data back into digital systems when they become
     available.

CONCEAL AND CAMOUFLAGE (PROTECTION)

     B-106. Units equip, practice, and discipline the use of camouflage, concealment, and deception in tactical
     operations. This is the first and easiest C-UAS mitigation strategy, which enhances force protection from
     indirect fires.

AIR DEFENSE IS MORE IMPORTANT THAN EVER (PROTECTION)

     B-107. Units practice and train on combined arms for air defense. Not only is this relevant to the C-UAS
     fight against sUASs, but the near-peer threat can employ rotary-wing aircraft with destructive effects as well.

EMPLOY ATTACK AVIATION (MOVEMENT AND MANEUVER, FIRES)

     B-108. Units plan and integrate attack aviation as a maneuver force operating in a dense air defense artillery
     environment. Units employ this enabler in this capacity to drive airspace management, suppression of enemy
     air defense planning, and mission command decision point operations when determining specific target
     criteria and conditions for commitment.

USE UNMANNED AIRCRAFT SYSTEM AT SQUAD THROUGH BRIGADE ROUTINELY
(INTELLIGENCE, PROTECTION)

     B-109. Units train and certify the appropriate number of crews and operators to leverage UASs fully. Units
     must understand the UAS operator’s training requirements and integrate their use into the maneuver
     collective training events as often as possible.

218  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                               Building Collective Training

LIVE-FIRE EXERCISE SCENARIO EXAMPLE

     B-110. During collective training, units should plan and execute their LFX through maximizing training
     time and space available. Unit planners must consider developing the type of scenario to meet the unit’s
     training objectives and commander’s intent. The scenario development process provides commanders and
     planners with the flexibility to design their scenario using the selected collective tasks, type of training space,
     resources available, and training time available. The unit may choose to conduct a single-lane or multiple-lane
     LFX scenario that best suits its collective training goals. Units should select the lane configuration that is
     optimal for the unit’s training plan and scheduled training time while still achieving the desired end state.

     B-111. Units should consider using a single-lane LFX scenario when training time or available maneuver
     space is limited. A single lane LFX scenario provides commanders with the ability to train collective tasks
     to their units using a single training lane. The selected collective tasks are arranged on a single lane in a
     progressive manner to ensure the platoon executes each task from start to finish.

     B-112. Unit planners should develop the LFX scenario based on the OPORD and the commander’s intent
     (see figure B-19 and figure B-22, pages 204 and 211). The unit should provide operational graphics that
     depict friendly or enemy templated positions, control measures, axis of advances, and any additional
     warfighting function terms and graphics as needed (see figure B-20, page 207). Unit planners must develop
     their LFX scenarios using tactical emphasis to ensure that the designated collective tasks are in logical
     sequence based on the structure of the unit and its higher command’s intent (see figure B-19 and figure B-21,
     pages 204 and 209).

     B-113. The unit creates an OPORDs that builds the context of the training event. Figure B-28 and
     figure B-29, pages 220 and 221 show an example OPORD for a collective live-fire event. This example event
     can simultaneously train and evaluate platoons and their platoons. The OPORD is written to the platoon level
     to create the concept of the operation sufficiently to build a robust collective live-fire event.

                                                Appendix C

       Roles, Responsibilities, and Leader Certification

    The collective training strategy described earlier in this manual provided general information
    used to plan and prepare for the training events properly. This appendix provides a more
    descriptive process to detail the roles and responsibilities of those who support the training.
    The information provided is not all-inclusive but provides key topics for units to include in
    their detailed planning process to enable and facilitate a well-executed training event.

    This appendix outlines the front-end analysis, procedures, personnel requirements, and other
    planning considerations to plan and establish both permanent and temporary live-fire ranges
    and tactical courses effectively. The training program requires the use of assets in the live-fire
    domain to efficiently reach collective proficiency.

OVERVIEW

     C-1. The commander is responsible for safety during all phases of training; however, the commander
     designates key personnel to run or operate the training event. The evaluation commander is responsible to
     verify all training event support personnel are certified to perform their role or function. The commander and
     executive officer will coordinate with the higher headquarters’ staff officers where appropriate.

     C-2. Supporting collective training in simulations, while conducting force-on-force events, and while
     executing live-fire events require varying levels of support. This appendix discusses the most common
     personnel that require some level of certification or understanding of their role and responsibility during the
     event. This is a generalized list which may be altered by the unit as necessary. These certifications, roles, and
     responsibilities are included for the following:

          • Personnel officer.
          • S-2.
          • Operations staff officer.
          • Logistics officer.
          • Signal officer (S-6).
          • FSO.
          • Evaluation commander.
          • Officer in charge (OIC).
          • Noncommissioned officer in charge (NCOIC).
          • RSO.
          • Radio-telephone operators.
          • Ammunition NCO.
          • Medical officer.
          • Medical support team.
          • Barrier NCO.
          • Entry control point NCO.
          • Range support personnel.
          • External evaluators or OC/Ts.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  237


---

Appendix C

EXERCISE CONTROL

     C-3. The unit plans and prepares to execute control of the training event. Planning identifies the EXCON
     structure for the event. The number of personnel varies from unit to unit and event to event. Units should
     coordinate for external support early to prepare those tasked Soldiers on their roles and responsibilities during
     the event. Figure C-1 shows a common, although complex EXCON structure that uses organic and external
     support. This structure can easily adapt to support collective training events within the training strategy. It
     includes force-on-force personnel as well as live-fire event requirements.

     Figure C-1. Common exercise control structure, example

238  TC 3-20.20                                              25 April 2025


---

                                                                           Roles, Responsibilities, and Leader Certification

C-4. The battalion or squadron commander and staff have specific tasks and functions to ensure the
collective training density is resourced, synchronized, and planned effectively. This platoon provides critical
planning considerations for the battalion or squadron commander and the staff. Figure C-2 shows their
relationship with the other range operations personnel.

               Figure C-2. Battalion/squadron live-fire planning structure, example

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20                                                            239


---

Appendix C

COMMANDER

     C-5. Effective live-fire training, executed to a high standard before, during, and after execution, is the
     cornerstone of operational success. The training of the individual critical tasks that Soldiers must accomplish
     to build and sustain proficiency is directly related to leadership involvement in all aspects of individual
     weapons training. Units must be able to run a variety of range and training area facilities, with varying levels
     of support from range operations. To conduct live-fire training events efficiently and effectively, the unit
     leadership must be present, proactive, involved, and engaged during planning, preparation, execution, and
     recovery from the training event.

     C-6. Battalion/squadron commanders must—
          • Provide training guidance and intent for the training density.
          • Comply with the installation procedures for the certification of OICs and RSOs.
          • Provide sufficient preparation time for junior leaders, range support, and prerequisite training.
          • Conduct risk management for all range operations.
          • Ensure leadership presence on the range during training.

EXECUTIVE OFFICER

     C-7. The battalion/squadron executive officer provides maintenance and sustainment planning
     considerations during the planning phase. They are responsible for planning maintenance support, activities,
     reporting, and routine preventative maintenance checks and services throughout the combat training density.
     They develop the workflow for all maintenance actions and provide coordinating instructions for the OPORD
     and any necessary alterations to the base plan through WARNORDs or FRAGORDs as necessary.

PERSONNEL OFFICER

     C-8. The personnel officer develops the personnel actions plan for the live-fire training density. This
     includes planning for all personnel actions while deployed in an austere environment, including receiving
     inbound personnel, evaluation reports coordination, awards guidelines and submission, and Soldier pay
     actions. The personnel officer develops the awards structure for the live-fire event for inclusion in the
     OPORD.

INTELLIGENCE OFFICER

     C-9. The S-2 collaborates with the Master Gunner to develop the situation template for the live-fire events.
     This includes integration of combat identification tasks, icon population on joint capabilities release systems,
     and intelligence collection activities for all training events. The S-2 develops the enemy and friendly forces
     information for the situation paragraph of the OPORD and any changes required for the WARNORDs or
     FRAGORDs.

OPERATIONS OFFICER

     C-10. The operations officer coordinates the development of the OPORD and all WARNORDs and
     FRAGORDs to support the combat training density. The operations officer directs all planning for each
     training event, the scenario development, and coordination of external assets such as vehicle crew evaluators
     or OC/Ts necessary to meet the commander’s intent.

FIRE SUPPORT OFFICER

     C-11. The assigned FSO from the supporting field artillery battalion assists integrating indirect fires, call for
     fire task training and evaluation, and other assigned functions supporting the unit’s training plan. Whenever
     possible, the FSO should be under the operational control of the training unit commander as part of the
     EXCON element.

240  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                 Roles, Responsibilities, and Leader Certification

LOGISTICS OFFICER

     C-12. The logistics officer develops the sustainment paragraph for the OPORD and any necessary logistics
     updates within the WARNORDs and FRAGORDs, as appropriate. The logistics officer collects all the
     requirements for durable and expendable supplies to support the mission and provides detailed accounting of
     the various classes of supply to complete the mission. The logistics officer coordinates and secures all training
     munitions from the ammunition supply point and provides transportation schedules for delivery.

MEDICAL OFFICER

     C-13. The medical officer develops the medical support plan for servicing patients within the training unit
     during the combat training density. They develop and submit the medical support plan for inclusion in the
     OPORD and provide any changes to the base plan as appropriate within any subsequent WARNORD or
     FRAGORD.

     C-14. The medical officer will identify all Soldiers’ status on the medical protection system not earlier than
     2 weeks prior to the combat training density. The medical officer and support will coordinate for all
     vaccination and testing capable in a field environment such that the training unit can maximize the
     CASEVAC and MEDEVAC training tasks with required measures.

     C-15. The medical officer will report daily to the OIC the number of Soldiers serviced, injuries, and any
     critical information concerning health issues of the training unit. Additionally, the medical officer will
     coordinate, plan, and establish the ability to provide:

          • Routine medical assessment for the training unit, typically referred to as sick call.
          • Dental support through the appropriate installation agencies.
          • Annual or periodic eye examinations and provide the details and procedures for inclusion in the

                OPORD, WARNORDs, and FRAGORDs as necessary.
          • Training, evaluation, and assessment of any scheduled CASEVAC or MEDEVAC task directed

                during the training event.
          • Inoculations, immunization, or vaccination requirements for the supporting Soldiers based on the

                unit’s medical protection system analysis to improve overall unit readiness, as capable.
          • Assessment the training unit’s population history of hot or cold weather injuries and monitor any

                identified Soldiers that are susceptible to those environmental injuries.
          • An assessment of the various risk for injuries based on the tasks, events, or actions planned to the

                commander for inclusion in the risk assessment.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  241


---

Appendix C

SIGNAL OFFICER

     C-16. The S-6 develops the command and signal paragraph for the OPORD and any updates for subsequent
     WARNORDs or FRAGORDs, as necessary. Some critical tasks the S-6 plans for include:

          • Development of the PACE plan.
          • Defines, coordinates, plans, and provides the communications system requirements to support the

                range personnel using land mobile radio, FM, joint capabilities release digital systems, and other
                systems.
          • Provides planning and execution efforts to support all tactical digital communications requirements
                for the training density as shown in figure C-3.
          • This list is not all inclusive and should be updated to exercise the digital communications systems
                within the organization.

                            Figure C-3. Digital task list for live-fire events, example

     C-17. All units equipped with Force XXI Battle Command, brigade and below (FBCB2) or blue force
     tracking (BFT) will conduct one day and one-night digital message, icon population, and situation report on
     Tables III, IV, V, and VI. FBCB2 includes both terrestrial (enhanced position location and report system
     [EPLRS] based) and celestial (satellite based, commonly referred to as BFT) versions. The use of FBCB2
     and FBCB2-BFT requires special planning and preparation to ensure the use of the systems do not become a
     training distracter.

242  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                  Roles, Responsibilities, and Leader Certification

Digital Communications

     C-18. The unit S-6 should ensure that all FBCB2 systems are configured properly and operational prior to
     the start of the live-fire density. Units should conduct a communications exchange in the motor pool at least
     2 weeks prior to the live fire to ensure success.

     C-19. EPLRS-based units will need to ensure that the correct communications security has been secured and
     loaded into their EPLRS radios. The S-6 should confirm that either an EPLRS platoon from the brigade
     support battalion or the fixed tactical internet is scheduled and coordinated to support the entire live-fire
     period. EPLRS-equipped units must have network support from either the EPLRS network manager unit or
     the fixed tactical internet to be successful.

     C-20. BFT-equipped units should ensure that the S-6 has made any necessary coordination for satellite time
     or support through the FBCB2-BFT network operations center (if required). Newer instrumented ranges such
     as the Digital Multipurpose Training Range, the Digital Multipurpose Range Complex, and the Digital
     Air-Ground Integration Range have FBCB2 TOC kits and ancillary equipment installed in the tower. Master
     Gunners and unit S-6 personnel should inspect the FBCB2 set up on the instrumented ranges during their
     range reconnaissance.

Digital Base Station

     C-21. To enable digital (FBCB2) communications, the unit should establish a digital base station using the
     FBCB2 TOC kit in the tower (where provided), using unit organic TOC kits, or with organic equipment using
     a vehicle within the unit.

     C-22. At a minimum, one evaluator should man, monitor, and evaluate the firing vehicles’ digital traffic at
     the established FBCB2 TOC. The evaluator will provide all digital traffic, icon populations, overlays, and
     predetermined messages to the firing vehicles as directed by the tower.

     C-23. During unit occupation of an instrumented range (digital range), the Master Gunner and S-6 personnel
     must inspect and configure the TOC kit used to conduct the training event. The following is a list (not all
     inclusive) of items to do and verify to ensure success:

          • Perform complete preventive maintenance checks and services on the FBCB2 system.
          • Configure the satellite interface (BFT only).
          • Verify the proper database is installed to ensure connectivity with all firing vehicles.
          • Configure the role to be used during the training event.
          • Clear logs and queues.
          • Verify correct map data is loaded on all systems.
          • Create an address group.
          • Set default message settings.
          • Create message folders for each table.

                 Store a free text message for each engagement for use as the “Tower Prompt.”
                 Store icon population update information.
                 Store free text messages for any call for engagement scenario as required by the unit’s training

                     plan.
                 Create and save free text and long form spot report messages for each engagement in the

                     appropriate folders.
          • Load overlays and graphics for the live-fire event from the mission data loader.
          • Conduct a communications check with all control elements and training systems.

Data Sets

     C-24. Each digital scenario requires a data set consisting of a digital script and one or more overlays. Users
     can store these data sets in the mission data loader. This provides users an easy method to distribute the data
     sets to any FBCB2-equipped vehicle in the training event.

     C-25. Units must ensure the address group is current in the controlling system to facilitate the rapid exchange
     of digital information on the range. Failure to maintain an accurate address group significantly reduces the
     training capabilities of the unit and hinders their progress completing the digital requirements.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  243


---

Appendix C

Range Overlay

     C-26. Units should develop standard overlays, both FBCB2 and hardcopy, for each range. At a minimum,
     each overlay includes the following graphic control measures:

          • Objectives: these are included for orientation.
          • Battle positions: in limited visibility, this control measure shows tower and safety personnel the

                positions of the firing vehicles.
          • Boundaries: these usually consist only of outer range safety markers.
          • TRPs: these help orient vehicle commanders on the battlefield. They usually include inner and outer

                range safety markers or easily identified points on the battlefield.
          • PLs: these help control movement.
          • Routes: these help orient the vehicle and aid in sustainment operations.

     C-27. Units must be prepared to include general threat location icon populations to support the training event.
     This includes using icon populations for UAS, close combat attack, call for fire, and call for MEDEVAC
     digital tasks as planned.

Icon Management and Engagement Flow

     C-28. Each engagement should begin with the base station operator directed to send a free text message from
     the digital base station to the firing vehicle. The free text message should include critical instructions for the
     crew to prepare for the upcoming engagement.

             Note. All free text messages and spot reports used in the live-fire event should be sent using
             FLASH (type of prioritized message) precedence. This reduces the amount of latency in message
             sending and receipt.

     C-29. Next, units send a long form spot report which should include the appropriate enemy or friendly icons
     for the engagement. These can be pre-built and saved under the combat messages folder. Each long form spot
     report can be named for the step or engagement in the scenario, as appropriate. This increases digital traffic
     efficiency and ensures that each crew receives the same prompts.

     C-30. Units should engage the targets as necessary and complete the engagement by sending a basic combat
     message situation report.

     C-31.   Sample engagement flow:
          •     Base station operator sends free text message, for example: PREPARE FOR DEFENSIVE
                OPERATIONS, OCCUPY ASSAULT POSITION BLUE, BATTLE CARRY SABOT AND
          •     REPORT REDCON ONE ON THE FIRING NET.
          •     Crew reports REDCON ONE.
          •     Base station operator sends appropriate spot report.
          •     Crew receives and interprets spot report.
          •     Targets are presented.
          •     Crew engages and reports using a combat message situation report.
                Base station operator sends free text message instructing the crew to prepare for the next
                engagement.

     C-32. Utilize the unit TACSOP to prepare, send, and receive data for digital engagements during LFX. This
     process validates the unit’s TACSOP during the training event.

     C-33. The unit should use Quick Send messages and buttons to streamline and simplify the use of FBCB2
     when possible. Units can add a Quick Send message to their training event on Tables III, IV, V, or VI, as
     needed.

244          TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                                                                                 Roles, Responsibilities, and Leader Certification

Retransmission

     C-34. Used to provide extended range secure and plain text FM traffic to outlying stations, based on the needs
     of the organization. For many installations, the training areas and live-fire facilities that support collective
     training require the use of a re-transmission site for all FM and digital systems to reach the battalion or
     squadron rear areas. Units must plan for effective retransmission locations and control to include life support,
     physical security, and maintenance.

Digital and Jump Station

     C-35. Communications are established to conduct digital information traffic using the unit’s organic mission
     command systems like FBCB2/BFT, Joint Battle Command-platform, or similar systems. This provides the
     unit the ability to conduct communication exercises daily at varying echelons, control the digital traffic to
     and from the firing vehicles, and enable icon population on digital maps based on the training scenario to
     exercise the unit’s digital capabilities.

     C-36. The jump station provides installation, troubleshooting, and communications checks for jump-radio
     configurations in support of collective training evaluations by enabling recording of the internal interactions
     of the training unit leadership. This is a valuable addition to the AAR process.

                                     Roles, Responsibilities, and Leader Certification

TRAINING UNIT COMMANDER

C-39. Effective live-fire training, executed to a high standard before, during, and after execution, is the
cornerstone of operational success. The training of the individual critical tasks that Soldiers must accomplish
to build and sustain proficiency is directly related to leadership involvement in all aspects of individual
weapons training. Units must be able to run a variety of range and training area facilities, with varying levels
of support from range operations. To conduct live-fire training events efficiently and effectively, the unit
leadership must be present, proactive, involved, and engaged during planning, preparation, execution, and
recovery from the training event.

C-40.   The unit commander must—
     •     Ensure compliance with DA Pam 385-63, applicable technical manuals, field manuals, training
           circulars, installation range guidance, and applicable SOPs for safe training and firing for each
     •     weapon system within the command.
     •     Ensure all personnel within the command are briefed on and comply with installation range
     •     procedures and safety requirements including required personal protective equipment.
           Designate an OIC and RSO for each firing event and or phase in accordance with AR 385-63 and
           DA Pam 385-63.
           Certify personnel operating the EXCON element to applicable regulations and local policies or
           procedures.

C-41. The commander has direct interaction with the other members of the EXCON. Although responsible
for the training event, the OIC and RSOs are present in the EXCON as safety representatives and not as part
of the training element. They are not authorized to have any other duties during training. Figure C-5, page 248
shows the commander’s direct relationships in the EXCON during force-on-force and live-fire training
events.

     fires, either mortar or artillery, the FSO provides clearance of fires capabilities with the higher headquarters,
     plotting/planning pre-positioned TRPs based on the indirect munitions capabilities and synchronizing (time
     and space) of fires to enhance training realism.

     C-43. The FSO, with any available Infantry Mortar Leader Course certified mortar leadership assist in the

FIRE MARKERS

     C-44. Units employing fire marker personnel or battlefield effects devices to increase the training realism
     should certify those personnel on the proper use, handling, storage, and accountability of the various
     pyrotechnics and battle effects simulator systems planned to support the training event. Units should also
     coordinate with the supporting installation ammunition supply point for any ammunition information notices,
     safety of use messages, or required training on the devices and training munitions scheduled for the event.

                       PYROTECHNICS, SMOKE, AND SIGNALS USE

          The fire markers must be certified on the use of various pyrotechnics, signals, markers,
          and smoke devices expected for use. See the pyrotechnics platoon in this appendix
          for more information.

OPPOSING FORCES

     C-45. An OPFOR must be provided for Tables III and IV. The composition of the OPFOR is determined by
     the commander and the training event design. Typically, a battalion or squadron would task a subordinate
     unit to provide the OPFOR to allow the training unit to focus on the training events.
     C-46. The OPFOR must be outfitted with the appropriate TADSS, including laser-based systems. They must
     be self-sufficient on these devices for installation, alignment, troubleshooting, and replacement, as necessary.
     C-47. The OPFOR must have certified ammunition handlers for the blank, smoke, signals, pyrotechnics, and
     other training munitions assigned. Staff sergeants and above must successfully complete any installation
     certification on the use of pyrotechnics, smoke grenades, and signals scheduled for use.
     C-48. The OPFOR may need to store training munitions in a holding area. They must follow all regulatory
     requirements, local policies, and SOPs when creating their own holding areas.

                       PYROTECHNICS, SMOKE, AND SIGNALS USE

          The OPFOR leaders, staff sergeant and above, must be certified on the use of various
          pyrotechnics, signals, markers, and smoke devices expected for use. See the
          pyrotechnics platoon in this appendix for more information.

EVALUATOR TEAM

     C-49. Trained and experienced evaluators must be used to achieve the goals and objectives of the commander
     and to determine the level of proficiency of the unit. The external evaluators are the keys to a successful
     collective training density that builds on the unit’s previous performance, increased competence, and
     confidence. Selecting, certifying, and effectively utilizing these evaluators enable a high-proficiency
     standard. Multiple teams of evaluators will be required to support a company or troop through their collective
     training for their squads or platoons (see figure C-7). Each training unit (platoon) requires an evaluator. For

MASTER GUNNER

     C-55. The Master Gunner is the commander’s direct fire technical advisor of the capabilities and limitations
     of all platform-specific weapon systems against threat systems. The Master Gunner helps the commander
     and the staff plan, develop, and conduct direct fire training. The Master Gunner develops scenarios based on
     commander’s guidance and previous assessments. Figure C-11 shows the Master Gunner certification
     process.

                                 Figure C-11. Master Gunner certification

C-56. For units without a Master Gunner, they should task a highly experienced NCO. Key areas of focus
for the Master Gunner/subject matter expert include—

     • Coordinates external resources such as ammunition, fuel, range, availability, maintenance assets,
          and recovery assets.

     • Prepares weapons and platforms for the firing events, and the following items must be verified or
          on hand as indicated:
           Verify borescope, recoil exercise, and pullover completed, or other actions as required by the
                firing platforms.
           DA Form 2408-4 (Weapon Record Data) card (in accordance with DA Pam 738-751, automated
                cards for gun tubes are located at the U.S. Army TACOM-Unique Logistics Support
                Application website.)
           DA Form 2404 (Equipment Inspection and Maintenance Worksheet) for all weapons and optics,
                as appropriate.
           DA Form 5988-E (Equipment Maintenance and Inspection Worksheet [EGA]) for all
                equipment and platforms, as required.

     • Coordinates scenarios, engagements, and exposure times for targets.
     • Coordinates maneuver box verification.
     • Coordinates setup of all ranges to ensure they meet the standards in this manual.
     • Prepares a surface danger area diagram and range overlay, if required (in accordance with

          AR 385-63 and DA Pam 385-63).
     • Ensures proper conduct of range-firing exercises.
     • Coordinates for all required TADSS to support the training plan including training, installation,

          alignment, troubleshooting, and replacement/repair, as necessary.

AMMUNITION NONCOMMISSIONED OFFICER

     C-60. The ammunition NCO provides accountability, issue, and management of the training event’s
     munitions. They maintain 100-percent accountability of training munitions, establish and maintain
     accounting of all munitions by type and DODIC, hand receipt or sign for the munitions to authorized leaders
     within the training unit to conduct training. The ammunition NCO’s certification process is listed in
     figure C-13.

     C-61. The ammunition NCO is provided with a detail of varying size depending on the volume of training
     munitions, guard requirements, and management functions. The detail personnel may rotate based on the
     needs of the ammunition NCO or fluctuations in the throughput of the training unit negotiating the training
     event.

     C-62. The ammunition NCO must adhere to Army regulations, local policies, and standard accounting
     practices to establish and maintain the ammunition pad/issue point. The ammunition NCO must be a certified
     ammunition handler in accordance with Army and local policies and procedures.

     C-63. The Master Gunner, in coordination with the RSO and NCOIC, provides updated firing order
     information, training munition package requirements, and specific guidance on the conduct of the
     ammunition pad. The ammunition NCO reports to the Master Gunner on the status of the training munitions
     a minimum of twice daily and reports any discrepancies in accountability of live munitions or dunnage
     immediately on discovery of the issue.

                                                                                 Roles, Responsibilities, and Leader Certification

BRIEFING NONCOMMISSIONED OFFICER

     C-67. One day and night. Units may rotate several experienced NCOs. Provides detailed training information
     to unit leaders who are not part of the training audience or select visitors. The briefing NCO controls the
     movement of visitors to the training event, provides the status of training, and describes an overview of the
     training tasks, conditions, and standards of the training event.

ENTRY CONTROL POINT NONCOMMISSIONED OFFICER AND GUARD DETAIL

     C-68. The entry control point NCO with the assigned guard detail secures the primary entrances to the
     training facility to control access to reduce training distractors or lost range down time to the training unit.
     Units must review their supporting installation’s range SOPs to review the various guard requirements.

BARRIER NONCOMMISSIONED OFFICER AND DETAIL

     C-69. The barrier NCO and appropriate number of detail personnel secure the range or surface danger zone
     boundaries and entry points as required by the supporting installation. Not all installations or range facilities
     require this detail. Units must review their supporting installation’s range SOPs to review the various barrier
     detail requirements.

FIREFIGHTING DETAIL

     C-70. A firefighting detail is provided as directed by the installation’s range SOP or policy. Typically,
     Soldiers are identified personnel in latter firing order to provide fire-fighting functions as necessary. Units
     must review their supporting installation’s range SOPs to review the various firefighting requirements.

LASER RANGE SAFETY OFFICER

     C-71. When lasers are in use on the training facility, a laser range safety officer (LRSO) will be required.
     One day, one night (see DA Pam 385-63). The LRSO is responsible for the safe use of laser devices. The
     LRSO reports directly to the RSO for all actions on the facility. Units must review AR 385-63,
     DA Pam 385-63, and local policies to determine if the training plan of their weapons, crew platforms, or
     sensors require an LRSO.

RADIO-TELEPHONE OPERATOR

     C-72. When units are scheduled for a range facility with a range operations control area, the facility for
     collective training includes a large footprint. The tower facility will be the primary location for the OIC.
     While completing the OIC duties, a radio-telephone operator should be tasked to support—one day and one
     night. Typically, it is a junior Soldier that has experience with radio-telephone operator procedures. This
     allows communications system operation for the range facility, mandatory means of contact with the
     installation range operations live-fire desk, and immediate access to medical support teams.

TARGET NONCOMMISSIONED OFFICER AND DETAIL

     C-73. As directed by the installation’s range SOP or policy, the target NCO and supporting detail personnel
     provides oversight of target placement, replacement, servicing, and repairs, as necessary. Some installations
     may require a target detail to assist in the construction of various targets and panels days or weeks prior to
     the training event.

                                                Appendix D

                           Collective Evaluation

    The training and evaluation outline is the Army’s source for individual and collective task
    training standards. The training and evaluation outline consists of the major procedures (steps
    or actions) a unit or individual must accomplish to perform a task to standard. A collective task
    also describes the performance required of a unit under the conditions of the training
    environment. The training and evaluation outline is a summary document that provides
    information on individual or collective task training objectives, resource requirements, and
    evaluation procedures. Training and evaluation outlines are developed, approved, and
    published by the responsible proponents. This appendix focuses on collective task training and
    evaluation outlines.
    During training events, leaders and evaluators use training and evaluation outlines to measure
    observed task proficiency. Completed training and evaluation outlines form the backbone of
    bottom-up feedback that company commanders and first sergeants review at the weekly
    training meeting. The training and evaluation outlines provide the commander with the
    necessary objective evaluations to assess unit training proficiency and to assess training
    readiness.

OVERVIEW

     D-1. Units should maximize their time and resources by designing their platoon training plans such that the
     collective element trains through proficiency to support their platoon and company, whether as an organic
     small unit, an attachment, or a cross-attachment. This level of synchronization will enable the unit to
     transition to advanced, higher echelon collective training seamlessly.

COLLECTIVE TASK PROFICIENCY

     D-2. Table IV, FTX, is used to determine the element’s collective task proficiency. It is an assessment of
     their collective supporting tasks that support the company and battalion mission-essential tasks.

     D-3. Collective task proficiency is based on two separate training events that are externally evaluated. These
     externally evaluated events provide the commander the primary assessment tool to evaluate the unit’s
     proficiency against an established standard as defined within the respective training publication(s), task
     training and evaluation outlines, and the unit’s SOPs. The unit’s proficiency is derived from the externally
     evaluated events from Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX.

LIVE-FIRE PROFICIENCY

     D-4. Table VI, LFX, provides the EXEVAL that determines the live-fire proficiency. The live-fire
     proficiency is evaluated externally on Table VI, LFX. It requires an evaluation of the targets presented and
     the demonstrated proficiency of the training unit to adequately deliver combat power effectively based on
     the varying tasks assigned.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  261


---

Appendix D

TACTICAL TASK EVALUATION

     D-5. The standards for each collective task are in the training and evaluation outlines. The Army has several
     official repositories that contain training and evaluation outlines. Users can access training and evaluation
     outlines at the—

          • Army Training Network.
          • DTMS.
          • CATS.
          • Central Army Registry.

     D-6. Users access the DTMS, CATS, and Central Army Registry via the Army Training Network website.
     The Army Training Network website does not require users to have additional privileges to view and print
     the training and evaluation outlines.

     D-7. The platoon training publications will provide guidance on the appropriate proficiency ratings awarded
     to a maneuver formation. The proficiency ratings awarded by the evaluation commander are identified within
     the respective training publication. Generally, commanders award the following proficiency ratings based on
     the element’s performance overall during Table IV, FTX and Table VI, LFX based on their tactical and
     live-fire performance (see figure D-1):

          • T (Fully trained):
                 Unit has attained task proficiency to the Army standard, achieved a GO in 80 percent or more
                     of both performance measures and leader performance measures, and has met 100 percent of
                     all critical performance measures.
                 The task is externally evaluated and meets the remaining requirements as outlined in the training
                     and evaluation outline in accordance with the objective task evaluation criteria matrix.

          • P (Practiced):
                 Unit has attained basic task proficiency with shortcomings, achieved a GO in 65 percent or
                     more of all performance measures, achieved 75 percent or more of all leader performance
                     measures, and has met 100 percent of all critical performance measures.
                 The unit’s shortcomings require significant training to meet the Army standard.
                 The task is not externally evaluated and meets the remaining requirements as outlined in the
                     training and evaluation outline in accordance with the objective task evaluation criteria matrix.

          • U (Untrained):
                 The unit cannot perform the task.
                 It achieved a GO in less than 65 percent of all performance measures, less than or equal to
                     75 percent in all leader performance measures, and less than 100 percent in all critical
                     performance measures.
                 The unit requires complete training on the task to achieve the Army standard.

262  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

               Figure D-1. Platoon proficiency ratings

Appendix D

LIVE-FIRE EVALUATION

     D-8. The live-fire events are scored separately from the tactical tasks. Figure D-2 provides the number of
     targets that must be serviced (hit to the kill standard) for the live-fire task of within the scenario step. For
     collective training, there will not be more than 20 targets (panels or troop sets) presented during any one step.
     D-9. Figure D-2 shows the kills standard for each target type, by caliber weapon. This is required to program
     the automated targetry on a live-fire facility. Each target is programmed to register a kill when the installed
     hit sensors register the correct number of hits to achieve the kill standard.

     Figure D-2. Kill standard for each type of target by caliber

264  TC 3-20.20                                                    25 April 2025


---

                                                                                                                         Collective Evaluation

TERMS OF REFERENCE

     D-10. To determine the live-fire proficiency, trainers must understand the terms of reference used in the
     instructions. For simplicity, the general terms are listed in paragraphs D-11 through D-18.

Phase

     D-11. The name of the phase of firing within a training event. There are two phases: the day phase and the
     night phase.

Scenario

     D-12. The series of steps within a day or night collective live-fire training mission. There are typically two
     scenarios: one for the day phase (day firing phase) and one for the night phase. A scenario typically has
     between 5 and 20 steps per phase.

Step

     D-13. A sequence for target arrays, presentations, tactical movement, or administrative commands within a
     scenario. Only one live-fire task can be used per step (for example, attack, suppress, defend, and so on.)

Engagement

     D-14. The actions of the training unit firing deliberately at a target or target array with the intent to achieve
     the kill standard.

Engaged Target

     D-15. The actions of the training unit firing at a threat but failing to achieve the kill standard.

Serviced Target

     D-16. When a target has been engaged and the kill standard has been achieved.

Target

     D-17. Either a panel or troop set presented during a step within the scenario.

Number of Targets in a Step

     D-18. The total number of presented targets—either panels or troop sets—for the training unit to engage.
     This number is used to determine if the training unit achieved live-fire proficiency at a specific task.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  265


---

Appendix D

PREPARING TO EVALUATE LIVE FIRE

     D-19. The evaluator team creates their own tracking mechanism for evaluating the live-fire event. Taken
     from the day phase scenario in appendix B, the evaluator can sketch out the key information that determines
     live-fire proficiency before the event.

     D-20. In the targets killed by step format example in figure D-3, the evaluator records the day phase step
     numbers along the left side of the matrix. Next, they record the tasks conducted for each step format. Each
     step format should only have one task to complete.

     D-21. Next, the evaluator cross references the task kill standard for the live-fire tasks. See figure D-4,
     page 268 for a chart that cross references the task to the kill standard percentage. For this example, the
     percentages were listed on the scenario examples in appendix B.

     D-22. Lastly, the evaluator coordinates with the unit to get the final approved number of targets used per step
     format. Static targets have a static number of targets. Dynamic and complex branches and sequels only
     present threats that were not serviced by the training unit. In essence, the target number is the same but the
     exposure time for those targets was eventually extended.

266  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025

RECORDING THE NUMBER OF SERVICED TARGETS BY STEP FORMAT

     D-24. During the live-fire event, the evaluator will communicate with the EXCON to confirm the number of
     targets presented and hit during the step format. This is done once the step format is complete, including any
     branches or sequels applied. Figure D-5 is an example where the evaluator filled out the targets killed by step

Appendix D

ASSESSING LIVE-FIRE PROFICIENCY BY STEP FORMAT

     D-25. If the unit successfully achieved the kill standard to the minimum number of threats or more, the unit
     receives a GO for the step’s live-fire outcome. If the unit hits less than the minimum number of targets
     indicated, the unit receives a NO-GO for the step format.

     D-26. Figure D-6 shows where the evaluator verified the appropriate number of kills for the step based on
     the live-fire task in the targets killed by step format. The evaluator identifies the steps failing to meet the kill
     standard by annotating NO-GO. At the bottom of the matrix, the evaluator determines the overall percentage
     of GO is 81 percent. This is greater than the minimum standard of 70 percent. This unit is rated live-fire
     proficient.

270  TC 3-20.20  25 April 2025


---

                           Collective Evaluation

          Figure D-6. Assessing proficiency by step (targets killed by step format)

D-27. To achieve live-fire proficiency, the unit must receive 70-percent GO across all live-fire steps. If the
unit does not achieve 70-percent GO for the live-fire steps, the commander may direct the unit to conduct a
branch or sequel until they achieve the passing 70-percent GO requirement. The commander may also re-cock
the live-fire event and either continue the same evaluation (increasing the total number of steps) or start over
completely.

25 April 2025  TC 3-20.20  271
